</details>


### [47] [Topics as Proxies for Sociodemographics: How Conversational Context Affects LLM Answers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02776)
*Vera Neplenbroek,Gabriele Sarti,Arianna Bisazza,Raquel Fernández*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLMs do not reliably detect users’ sociodemographics from a single chat, and demographic disparities in advice are small; the bigger issue is that conversation topic strongly and unpredictably steers high-stakes advice.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In high-stakes domains, even small differences in LLM advice can matter. Prior work suggested sociodemographic outcome disparities, so the paper aims to determine whether those disparities are truly caused by demographic inference or by other aspects of the conversation context.

Method: The paper evaluates whether LLMs can infer sociodemographic attributes from a single conversation history, measures disparity in generated advice across groups, and compares the predictive power of sociodemographics versus multiple psycho/linguistic conversation features such as topic, emotion, and readability.

Result: LLMs struggle to infer sociodemographic traits from one conversation history. Observed disparities across sociodemographic groups are minimal in magnitude. Among examined features, conversation topic is the strongest predictor of LLM advice and can act as a proxy for demographic groups, often influencing outputs unpredictably.

Conclusion: LLM-generated disparities in high-stakes advice are present but generally small, and they seem to be driven more by conversational context, especially topic, than by accurate inference of user sociodemographics.

Abstract: When large language models (LLMs) are used in high-stakes scenarios, such as legal, medical and financial advice, even a single conversation history is enough to drive differences in outcomes between users. Prior work has demonstrated that this results in outcome disparities between sociodemographic groups, with some groups receiving more advantageous outcomes than others. In this work, we demonstrate that LLMs actually struggle to infer user sociodemographics from a single conversation history and that although there are disparities between sociodemographic groups, they are minimal in magnitude. To investigate what the main driver of these disparities is, we compare user sociodemographics to a range of (psycho)linguistic features of conversations, including conversation topic, emotions, and readability. We find that conversation topics are most predictive of LLM-generated advice within a conversational context, which, to some extent, function as proxies for sociodemographic groups and often affect advice in unpredictable ways. This is cause for concern and highlights the need for future research to better understand and, if needed, mitigate the effect of conversational context on LLM outputs in high-stakes scenarios.

</details>


### [48] [Do Value Vectors in Deep Layers Need Context from the Residual Stream?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02780)
*Muyu He,Yuchen Liu,Qingya Huang,Li Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Replace value vectors in the last third of transformer layers with learned token lookup-table vectors instead of contextualized values; this improves loss and benchmark performance while reducing compute and memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard attention computes value vectors from the residual stream, making them context-dependent and costly to recompute/cache. The paper investigates whether deeper layers really need contextualized values, and whether preserving original token information more directly can improve model quality and efficiency.

Method: The authors systematically ablate design choices for context-free value vectors and propose Bank of Values (BoV), which replaces context-dependent value generation in the last third of transformer layers with learned token-specific value vectors stored in sparse lookup tables.

Result: Across 135M and 780M parameter models, BoV improves validation loss over standard attention. In the 780M model, it also improves the average score across 21 benchmarks and matches the previous best token-information-enhanced value method with lower compute and memory cost.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that in deeper transformer layers, learning context-free value vectors preserves token information effectively and improves performance; a Bank of Values (BoV) lookup-table design can match or exceed standard/context-dependent value methods while using less compute and memory.

Abstract: The success of the transformer architecture as the backbone of modern LLMs is in large part due to its use of attention layers. An attention layer follows the standard neural network paradigm: it takes the residual stream as input and thereby produces context-dependent query, key, and value vectors. However, we find that model performance meaningfully improves when deeper layers learn only a context-free value vector to preserve the original token information, without drawing on any context from the residual stream. When the model has access to this context-free value vector, adding back the context-dependent component provides little additional benefit for aggregate benchmark performance. Such context-free value vectors can be stored as sparse model parameters, eliminating the need to recompute or persistently cache these values. Through systematic ablations on the key design choices for such context-free value vectors, we propose Bank of Values (BoV), a new way of computing value vectors in attention by learning a lookup table of token-specific value vectors for each of the last third of layers. Across 135M and 780M models, BoV improves validation loss over standard attention and, at 780M, the average score across 21 benchmarks, matching the previous best method that adds token information to the value vector with less compute and memory.

</details>


### [49] [Translating Classical Poetry into Modern Prose](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02806)
*Chalamalasetti Kranti,Sowmya Vajjala*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A new 600-example benchmark for translating classical Telugu poems into modern Telugu and English prose shows that current LLMs still perform poorly, and standard MT evaluation methods are not fully reliable for this task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical Telugu poetry is linguistically and stylistically distant from contemporary prose, and there is a lack of benchmark resources for studying poem-to-prose translation into modern Telugu and English. The work aims to fill this resource gap and assess how well current LLMs handle this challenging translation setting.

Method: The paper introduces Padyam2Gadyam, a human-verified dataset of 600 classical Telugu poems paired with contemporary Telugu and English prose translations, and evaluates five modern large language models on poem-to-prose translation quality in both target languages, supplemented by qualitative analysis of evaluation approaches.

Result: The dataset was created with 600 poem–prose pairs in Telugu and English. Across five evaluated LLMs, performance differed by model but remained broadly inadequate in both languages. The qualitative study also found that existing machine translation evaluation approaches have important capability and limitation gaps for this problem.

Conclusion: Current large language models show varying but generally insufficient performance for poem-to-prose translation from classical Telugu into contemporary Telugu and English, indicating substantial room for improvement and exposing limitations in current MT evaluation methods for this task.

Abstract: We introduce Padyam2Gadyam, a dataset for the task of poem-to-prose translation from 13th-17th Century Telugu Classical Poetry to contemporary Telugu and English prose. The dataset consists of 600 poems and their human-verified Telugu and English prose translations. We evaluated 5 contemporary Large Language Models (LLMs) on their ability to do poem-to-prose translation into Telugu and English. Our results indicate that while there are differences across LLMs, their overall performance leave a large room for improvement in both languages. Through qualitative analysis, we discuss the the capabilities and limitations of contemporary MT evaluation approaches for this task.

</details>


### [50] [Fixing FOLIO and MALLS: Verified Annotations and an LLM-assisted Framework to Focus Human Relabeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02837)
*Andrea Brunello,Cristian Curaba,Luca Geatti,Michele Mignani,Angelo Montanari,Nicola Saccomanno*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: 这篇论文证明了主流NL-to-FOL基准有大规模标注错误，修正后模型表现会显著变化，并提出一种用LLM高效辅助人工清洗数据集的方法。 / This paper shows that popular NL-to-FOL benchmarks contain widespread annotation errors, that correcting them substantially changes model performance, and that an LLM can efficiently guide humans to clean such datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NL到FOL的准确翻译是神经符号AI和自然语言推理的基础，因此其基准数据集必须可靠；但这些数据集此前缺乏严格审计，可能导致模型能力被错误估计。 / Accurate NL-to-FOL translation is foundational for neurosymbolic AI and NLI, so benchmark reliability is critical; however, these datasets had not been rigorously audited, risking misleading model evaluation.

Method: 作者首先对FOLIO验证集和MALLS部分测试集进行系统性人工审查，统计FOL真值、自然语言歧义和NLI标签错误；随后发布修正后的ground truth，并用Gemma 4 31B-it、Qwen3-30B-A3B和GPT-4o-mini重新评测；最后提出一个基于LLM的人工审查辅助框架，用于优先筛查最可能出错的样本。 / The authors conduct a systematic human audit of the FOLIO validation split and a subset of MALLS test instances, quantify errors in FOL ground truths, NL ambiguity, and NLI labels, release corrected annotations, re-evaluate three SOTA LLMs, and propose an LLM-based framework that prioritizes likely erroneous instances for human review.

Result: 人工审查发现FOLIO约39%的样本、MALLS约36%的样本存在错误FOL形式化；另有16.4%和48%的自然语言存在歧义，FOLIO中还有8.4%的NLI标签错误。使用修正后的真值后，三种LLM的准确率提升了9到22个百分点。LLM引导审查可在仅审查少于24%样本时把数据集准确率提升到90%，而无引导审查需要超过70%的样本。 / The audit finds incorrect FOL formalizations in about 39% of FOLIO and 36% of MALLS, plus NL ambiguity rates of 16.4% and 48%, and 8.4% incorrect NLI labels in FOLIO. With corrected ground truths, three LLMs gain 9 to 22 accuracy points. The proposed guided review reaches 90% dataset accuracy after reviewing under 24% of instances, versus over 70% for unguided review.

Conclusion: 现有NL-to-FOL基准存在大量标注与形式化错误，会显著扭曲模型评测；通过人工校正与LLM引导审查，可以更高效地提升数据集质量。 / Existing NL-to-FOL benchmarks contain substantial annotation and formalization errors that materially distort model evaluation; human correction plus LLM-guided review can improve dataset quality much more efficiently.

Abstract: Accurate translation from Natural Language to First-Order Logic (NL-to-FOL) underpins neurosymbolic AI systems and Natural Language Inference (NLI), making the quality of NL-to-FOL benchmarks essential -- yet these datasets have never been rigorously audited. Our first contribution is to present a systematic human inspection of the validation split of \textsf{FOLIO} and a subset of \textsf{MALLS} test instances, finding that approximately 39% and 36% of entries, respectively, contain incorrect FOL formalizations (i.e., ground truth labels), with additional rates of ambiguous NL sentences (16.4% and 48%) and incorrect NLI labels in \textsf{FOLIO} (8.4%). Our second contribution is to develop and release corrected ground truths for such datasets, showing that annotation errors distort model evaluation on a reference benchmark task: testing three state-of-the-art LLMs (Gemma~4 31B-it, Qwen3-30B-A3B, and GPT-4o-mini) with the corrected ground truths yields accuracy gains from +9 to +22 percentage points. Motivated by these findings, we propose an LLM-based framework to support humans in manual reviewing NL-to-FOL datasets. By directing reviewers toward the most error-prone instances, we empirically show that it is possible to achieve 90% dataset accuracy after reviewing fewer than 24% of instances, compared to over 70% required by unguided review. We release all human-verified annotations and the code for our framework.

</details>


### [51] [Economy of Minds: Emerging Multi-Agent Intelligence with Economic Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02859)
*Zhenting Qi,Huangyuan Su,Ao Qu,Chenyu Wang,Yu Yao,Han Zheng,Kushal Chattopadhyay,Guowei Xu,Zihan Wang,Weirui Ye,Vijay Janapa Reddi,Ju Li,Paul Pu Liang,Himabindu Lakkaraju,Sham Kakade,Yilun Du*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A market-style multi-agent system lets agents compete, earn, and go bankrupt; these simple incentives automatically create coordination and adaptation, yielding stronger collective reasoning than centralized single-agent baselines across multiple tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to understand how multi-agent systems can achieve scalable coordination, adaptation, and collective intelligence without relying on centralized orchestration or hand-engineered communication protocols. The work is inspired by the idea that market-like local incentives may generate effective global behavior.

Method: The method builds an agent economy inspired by Hayekian market coordination. Agents bid in auctions for the right to act, receive environmental rewards, exchange payments, and accumulate wealth. Wealth acts as a decentralized credit signal, while an economic selection mechanism exploits successful agents through mutation and replaces bankrupt agents through exploration.

Result: The results show that, even when starting from weak agents, the economic system produces emergent multi-step reasoning strategies and surpasses stronger monolithic baselines on five agentic tasks: mathematical reasoning, financial research, scientific research, accelerator design, and distributed-system optimization. The paper also offers theoretical analysis connecting local incentives to long-term global performance.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that decentralized economic incentives can enable a population of weak agents to self-organize into stronger collective intelligence without centralized control. By using auctions, payments, wealth accumulation, and evolutionary replacement, agents develop emergent multi-step reasoning and outperform stronger monolithic baselines on several complex tasks.

Abstract: How can a population of agents self-orchestrate and self-adapt into stronger collective intelligence without centralized control? Inspired by Friedrich Hayek's economic theory of decentralized coordination in markets, we study this question through an agent economy in which agents compete via auctions for the right to act, exchange payments, and accumulate wealth from environmental rewards. These simple economic signals induce decentralized credit assignment, driving planning without global orchestration or explicit communication protocols. The population evolves through economic selection: effective agents accumulate wealth and are mutated via exploitation, while ineffective ones go bankrupt and are replaced via exploration. We show that, initialized with weak agents, the economy produces emergent multi-step reasoning strategies and outperforms stronger monolithic baselines across five agentic tasks, including mathematical reasoning, financial research, scientific research, accelerator design, and distributed-system optimization. We further provide theoretical insights into how economic dynamics shape agent behaviors, linking local incentives to long-term global performance. Our results suggest a new path to multi-agent intelligence: rather than engineering coordination, we can design decentralized incentive structures under which it automatically emerges.

</details>


### [52] [Adaptive Latent Agentic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02871)
*Dongwon Jung,Peng Shi,Yi Zhang,Junshan Zhang,Muhao Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ALAR让LLM agent平时少说、难题再展开思考，在不降甚至提升效果的情况下大幅节省推理token。


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 现有LLM agent在多轮决策中几乎每一步都生成冗长文本推理，且各轮分配推理预算近似均匀，导致token开销高、效率差。

Method: 提出双模式框架ALAR：一方面用agent动作作为监督锚点学习紧凑的潜在推理；另一方面训练模型在潜在推理足够时保持隐式，在更难决策时再自适应切换到显式chain-of-thought。

Result: 在agentic search和tool-use基准上，ALAR在保持相当或更好任务准确率的同时，token生成量分别最高减少43.6%和84.6%。

Conclusion: ALAR通过在常规步骤使用潜在推理、在困难步骤才显式展开CoT，显著改善了LLM agent的准确率-效率权衡，减少了不必要的文本推理。

Abstract: Large reasoning models improve performance by generating extended chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning, but this behavior becomes inefficient when applied to LLM agents. Current LLM agents often generate verbose textual reasoning at every decision step and allocate reasoning effort nearly uniformly across turns, leading to substantial inefficiency in multi-turn agentic trajectories. We propose Adaptive Latent Agentic Reasoning (ALAR), a dual-mode framework that uses compact latent reasoning for routine turns and selectively escalates to explicit chain-of-thought when deeper deliberation is needed. ALAR learns latent reasoning by using the agent's actions as supervision anchors and is further optimized to use latent reasoning when it is sufficient for task success and reserve explicit CoT for harder decisions. Experiments on agentic search and tool-use benchmarks show that ALAR maintains comparable or better task accuracy while substantially reducing generated tokens by up to 43.6% in search and 84.6% in tool use. These results demonstrate that ALAR improves the accuracy-efficiency trade-off of LLM agents by reducing unnecessary textual reasoning while preserving explicit deliberation for harder decision steps.

</details>


### [53] [Linear Probes Detect Task Format, Not Reasoning Mode in Language Model Hidden States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02907)
*Subramanyam Sahoo,Vinija Jain,Aman Chadha,Divya Chaudhary*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The apparent 'reasoning-type' separation in LLM hidden states is an artifact of dataset format, not evidence of distinct internal reasoning modules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work often uses linear probing of LLM hidden states to argue that models encode distinct reasoning types. The paper is motivated by the need to test whether probe separability truly reflects computationally distinct reasoning processes or merely superficial benchmark-format differences.

Method: The paper probes hidden states from Qwen3-14B on three benchmarks mapped to deductive (LogiQA 2.0), inductive (ARC-Challenge), and abductive (αNLI) reasoning. It evaluates linear separability at layer 32/40, then removes confounding factors by residualizing source identity, option count, and response length. It also measures trace-anchor similarity to estimate shared reasoning structure across tasks and performs causal steering with random controls (n=20) to test whether the geometric directions found by probes functionally control reasoning mode.

Result: Before deconfounding, linear probes reach 100% cross-validated accuracy at layer 32 with strong geometric separation and low convex-hull contamination. After residualizing format-related variables, accuracy drops to chance. Trace-anchor similarity shows substantial shared reasoning across tasks (42.5% agreement versus 33.3% chance), and causal steering finds no significant functional relationship between probe geometry and reasoning mode (p=0.286).

Conclusion: High linear-probe accuracy on Qwen3-14B hidden states does not demonstrate distinct internal representations for deductive, inductive, and abductive reasoning; it is mainly caused by task-format confounds, so mechanistic interpretability studies should routinely deconfound format variables before drawing claims about reasoning-type representations.

Abstract: Linear probing of large language model (LLM) hidden states is widely used to claim that models learn distinct representations for different reasoning types. We test this by probing Qwen3-14B on three benchmarks spanning the classical trichotomy: LogiQA 2.0 (deductive), ARC-Challenge (inductive), and $α$NLI (abductive). At layer 32 of 40, linear probes achieve 100\% cross-validated accuracy with well-separated geometry (intrinsic dimensionalities: 20.6, 28.5, 33.6; convex hull contamination $\leq$1.5\%). However, this separation is entirely driven by format confounds. Residualizing source identity, option count, and response length reduces accuracy to chance. Trace-anchor similarity indicates largely shared reasoning across tasks (42.5\% agreement vs.\ 33.3\% chance), and causal steering with random controls ($n=20$) shows no functional link between geometry and reasoning mode ($p=0.286$). Thus, high probe accuracy reflects task format rather than computational structure, motivating routine format deconfounding in mechanistic interpretability.

</details>


### [54] [WRIT: Write-Read Intensive Trajectory Synthesis for Multi-Turn User-Facing Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02908)
*Hengrui Gu,Xiaotian Han,Kaixiong Zhou*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: WRIT is a trajectory synthesis pipeline for training multi-turn agents on both long task execution and hard single-decision reasoning that requires heavy evidence gathering. By generating read-heavy as well as write-intensive trajectories, it trains smaller models to behave more efficiently and outperform stronger baselines on agent benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing trajectory synthesis pipelines mainly increase task difficulty by composing multiple user requests, which creates write-intensive data focused on sequential execution. The authors argue this misses another key source of difficulty: a single write decision may require gathering and comparing substantial read-tool evidence before a valid action can be identified.

Method: The paper proposes WRIT (Write-Read Intensive Trajectory Synthesis), a pipeline that synthesizes multi-turn agent training trajectories along two axes: number of write decisions and evidence burden per decision. It generates write-intensive and read-heavy tasks, diversifies user behavior instructions to mimic realistic conversational variation, and simulates agent-user interactions in an executable environment to produce full trajectories.

Result: With only 2K synthesized trajectories, a 4B model trained on WRIT outperforms GPT-5.1 no-think on τ²-bench and substantially reduces inference-time token usage, indicating improved efficiency and stronger evidence-based agent behavior.

Conclusion: WRIT demonstrates that compact synthesized SFT data can train agents to make robust, evidence-grounded decisions under high information load, improving benchmark performance and reducing inference-time token usage; notably, a 4B model trained on only 2K trajectories outperforms GPT-5.1 no-think on τ²-bench.

Abstract: Multi-turn user-facing agents must infer user intent from incomplete requests, collect missing information through dialogue and tools, and execute valid actions. A training trajectory records this process as an interleaved sequence of user messages, agent responses, tool calls, etc. Synthesizing sufficiently complex trajectory has become a central route to train agents: existing pipelines often increase difficulty by composing multiple user requests into longer tasks, producing write-intensive trajectories that train sequential execution.
  We argue that a single write decision can itself be difficult when the agent must gather and compare substantial read-tool evidence before its arguments become identifiable, a challenge that write-intensive data alone cannot address. Guided by this insight, we propose WRIT (\uline{W}rite-\uline{R}ead \uline{I}ntensive \uline{T}rajectory Synthesis), a pipeline for synthesizing multi-turn agent training trajectories along two complexity axes: the number of write decisions in a task and the evidence burden of each individual decision. WRIT first generates write-intensive and read-heavy tasks. It then diversifies user behavior instructions to reflect realistic conversational variation, and finally simulates agent-user interactions in an executable environment to produce complete training trajectories. The resulting data trains agents not only for longer task execution, but also for robust, evidence-grounded decision making under high information load. With only 2K synthesized trajectories, a 4B model trained on WRIT outperforms GPT-5.1 no-think on $τ^2$-bench and substantially reduces inference-time token usage, showing that compact SFT data can convert part of expensive test-time reasoning into efficient agent behavior.

</details>


### [55] [The Ghost Annotator: a Framework to Explore Human Label Variation in Content Moderation through Conformal Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02911)
*Mirko Lai,Alessandra Urbinati,Simona Frenda,Fabiana Vernero,Marco Antonio Stranisci*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A new uncertainty-analysis framework shows that LLMs track human disagreement to some extent, but larger models may be confidently wrong in ways that match no human annotator, and these errors exhibit consistent demographic bias.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is that prior work emphasizes raw model performance but pays less attention to uncertainty estimation, especially in scenarios where LLMs are used to generate or support annotations. This creates a need for methods that not only measure confidence but also characterize how LLM judgments align or misalign with diverse human annotators.

Method: The paper proposes a framework that combines conformal prediction with Collaborative Filtering-style annotator representations to model relationships between LLM outputs and human annotations. It introduces Non-Conformity Scores, the Ghost Prediction metric, and a Ghost Annotator representation to detect and quantify cases where model predictions differ from all human annotations. Cosine similarity is then used to compare behavioral differences across sociodemographic groups. The framework is evaluated on four content moderation datasets using four LLMs from different families and sizes.

Result: The experiments show that model uncertainty generally increases with annotator disagreement across all tested LLMs. However, larger models are often more confident even when their predictions are not aligned with any available human annotation. The Ghost Annotator analysis also uncovers consistent demographic misalignment patterns across datasets and models.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that uncertainty-aware analysis reveals systematic divergence between LLM predictions and human annotators. Although all models become more uncertain when annotators disagree, larger models can still be overconfident on instances unsupported by any human label. The proposed Ghost Annotator analysis further indicates stable demographic misalignment, implying structural bias likely inherited from pretraining data.

Abstract: Current research primarily focuses on model performance, while comparatively less attention has been devoted to uncertainty estimation, particularly in settings where LLMs are increasingly used to generate annotated data. We introduce a framework combining conformal prediction with Collaborative Filtering-style annotators' representation to model LLM behavior in relation to human annotators and to analyze patterns of agreement and disagreement. Using Non-Conformity Scores, we introduce the Ghost Prediction metric and the Ghost Annotator representation to quantify cases in which model predictions diverge from all available human annotations. We compute cosine similarity measures to explore differences in model behavior across sociodemographic axes. We evaluated four LLMs of different size and families across four content moderation datasets. Our finding shows that while we find that all models uncertainty increases with annotator disagreement, larger models tend to be more confident in the classification of texts that are not aligned with any human annotation. Finally, the Ghost Annotator framework reveals a consistent and robust pattern of demographic misalignment, suggesting a structural bias likely rooted in pretraining corpora.

</details>


### [56] [Linguistic Productivity in Large Language Models: Models Coerce, but do not Preempt](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02953)
*Claire Bonial,Claire Benet Post,Laura Michaelis,Harish Tayyar Madabushi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLMs学会了“如何推广”常见语言模式，但还没学会“何时不要推广”。它们体现了entrenchment，却基本不体现preemption。 / LLMs learn when to extend familiar language patterns, but not when to stop extending them: they exhibit entrenchment-like productivity, but little evidence of preemption.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 动机是检验LLMs是否像基于使用的语法理论所预测的那样，同时受到两种相反统计力量的影响：高频使用带来的生产性增强，以及未观察到预期形式带来的约束。 / The motivation is to determine whether LLMs, as usage-based learners trained on large text corpora, are shaped by the same opposing statistical forces proposed in usage-based grammar: productivity boosted by frequent exposure and constrained by absence in expected contexts.

Method: 作者跨不同模型架构比较LLMs对两类频率信号的敏感性：一是通过构式强制(coercion)测试模型是否能对新词复现构式生产性，以检验entrenchment；二是考察模型是否会避免生成在语义上合适但训练数据中未观察到的形式，以检验preemption。 / The paper evaluates LLMs across architectures on two frequency-driven mechanisms: testing whether coercive constructions let models extend productivity to nonce words as evidence of entrenchment, and testing whether models avoid semantically felicitous but unattested forms as evidence of preemption.

Result: 结果显示，模型规模越大，越能识别并生成带有新词的构式强制用法，说明其掌握了一定的构式生产性；但即便是最大的模型，也不能利用未见证据来抑制新项目上的错误泛化。 / Results show that larger models better recognize and reproduce coercive constructional uses with nonce words, indicating learned productivity, but even the largest models do not use negative evidence to suppress overgeneralization for novel items.

Conclusion: 较大的语言模型能够通过构式语境对新词表现出生产性，体现出类似于基于使用理论中的“固化/强化”(entrenchment)效应；但它们无法把负证据推广到新语言项目上，因此“先占/预阻”(preemption)不足以阻止对语义上可接受但语料中未出现模式的过度泛化。 / Larger language models show constructional productivity with nonce words under coercive contexts, reflecting an entrenchment-like effect; however, they fail to generalize negative evidence to novel items, so preemption does not prevent overgeneralization of semantically plausible but unattested patterns.

Abstract: Usage-based theories of grammars posit that creative productivity of the structures of language is both bolstered and constrained by two distinct frequency signals: entrenchment, stemming from high frequency usage, and preemption, stemming from having never observed a particular linguistic structure in a context where one might expect that structure to appear. Large Language Models are also usage-based, in the sense that the structures of language are learned through exposure to vast amounts of text. Here, we test whether or not the opposing statistical forces of entrenchment and preemption also encourage and constrain linguistic productivity in LLMs. We demonstrate across model architectures that larger models recognize and can reproduce with nonce words constructional productivity (entrenchment) in cases of coercion, wherein the broader constructional context coerces an atypical interpretation of a lexical item. However, we also show that even the largest models do not extend negative evidence to novel language, and statistical preemption does not enable models to avoid overgeneralization of patterns that are semantically felicitous, but never observed in data.

</details>


### [57] [Fast-dLLM++: Fréchet Profile Decoding for Faster Diffusion LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02955)
*Siva Rajesh Kasa,Yasong Dai,Sumit Negi,Hongdong Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Fast-dLLM++ is a plug-in decoding rule for diffusion LLMs that uses the whole token-confidence profile, not just the weakest token, to commit more tokens safely in parallel; this improves the accuracy-throughput frontier and reaches up to 37% higher throughput without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Fast-dLLM decoding assumes homogeneous high confidence within a candidate commit set, so selection is governed by the weakest chosen token. The authors argue this is too conservative because real decoding steps have heterogeneous confidence distributions, causing missed opportunities for safe parallel token commitment and limiting inference speed.

Method: The paper extends Fast-dLLM by introducing Fréchet profile decoding, which selects parallel commit sets using the full sorted confidence profile instead of a single minimum-confidence statistic. It keeps the model, diffusion process, and KV-cache implementation unchanged, and generalizes the original factor selector to heterogeneous-confidence settings with a provable heterogeneity bonus.

Result: On GSM8K, MATH, HumanEval, and MBPP with LLaDA-8B, the proposed profile-aware selection improves the accuracy-throughput tradeoff and achieves up to 37% higher throughput at comparable accuracy relative to weakest-token-based Fast-dLLM decoding.

Conclusion: Fast-dLLM++ is a training-free, drop-in decoding improvement for diffusion LLMs that replaces weakest-token commit selection with profile-aware Fréchet profile decoding, yielding provable gains under heterogeneous token confidences and up to 37% higher throughput at similar accuracy.

Abstract: Diffusion large language models promise parallel token generation, yet inference remains bottlenecked by deciding which masked tokens can be safely committed together. Fast-dLLM addressed this with KV caching and confidence-guided parallel decoding, but its decoding theory uses a homogeneous high-confidence assumption that effectively reduces each candidate set to its weakest selected token. We argue that this leaves speed on the table because real decoding steps exhibit heterogeneous confidence profiles. We propose \textbf{Fast-dLLM++}, a training-free extension that introduces \emph{Fréchet profile decoding}: selecting parallel commit sets from the full sorted confidence profile rather than a single worst-case confidence. The resulting rule is a heterogeneous-confidence generalization of Fast-dLLM's factor selector and it recovers the previous rule exactly in the equal-confidence case and adds a provable \emph{heterogeneity bonus} when the selected tokens have uneven confidences. Fast-dLLM++ leaves the model, diffusion process, and cache implementation entirely unchanged, making it a drop-in replacement for existing Fast-dLLM decoding. Experiments on GSM8K, MATH, HumanEval, and MBPP with the LLaDA-8B model show that the theoretical improvement translates directly into empirical gains: profile-aware selection improves the accuracy--throughput frontier by exploiting safe parallelism that weakest-token rules miss, achieving up to 37\% higher throughput at comparable accuracy. Our anonymous code release is at https://github.com/Ringo-Star/FastdLLM_plusplus.

</details>


### [58] [EURO-5K: When Does Domain Pretraining Matter? Benchmarking Transformers for EU Reporting Obligation Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02971)
*Marios Koniaris,Vasileios Kotronis,Eugenia Giannini,Panayiotis Tsanakas*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They introduce EURO-5K for EU reporting-obligation extraction and show that both BERT classifiers and fine-tuned LLMs work well; legal pretraining matters most for parameter-efficient and low-data settings, with gains flattening after roughly 3K training samples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extracting reporting obligations is important for reducing regulatory reporting burden, but these provisions are hard to distinguish from similar legal text and existing legal NLP lacks a dedicated benchmark, clear annotation guidance, and systematic comparison of modeling and adaptation strategies.

Method: The paper builds EURO-5K, a sentence-level corpus of reporting obligations and hard negative examples from 136 EU legislative acts. It compares discriminative token-classification models and generative span-extraction LLMs under full fine-tuning and QLoRA, against pattern/dependency baselines and few-shot prompting, with additional learning-curve and cross-dataset evaluations.

Result: Fully fine-tuned generic BERT and Legal-BERT reach about 0.89 F1, and fine-tuned LLMs achieve comparable sentence-level extraction performance. Legal pretraining provides limited gains for fully adapted generative models but clearly helps when tuning capacity is limited, with QLoRA Legal-BERT outperforming generic BERT. Performance saturates around 3K samples, and cross-dataset tests show the models learn reporting-obligation extraction rather than generic regulation classification.

Conclusion: EURO-5K enables robust extraction of reporting obligations from EU legislation. Fully fine-tuned BERT-style models and fine-tuned LLMs both perform strongly, while legal-domain pretraining is most useful under constrained adaptation and low-data settings. The dataset appears sufficient at around 3K samples, and released tools support practical compliance automation.

Abstract: Extracting reporting obligations from EU legislation is critical for assessing and reducing regulatory reporting burden. However, distinguishing reporting requirements from structurally similar provisions requires specialised legal understanding. Current legal NLP methods lack specialised datasets with clear guidelines and comparative evaluation of extraction paradigms and domain adaptation strategies. We curate EURO-5K, a corpus of sentence-level reporting obligations and challenging negative examples from 136 EU legislative acts. On this dataset, we train and compare discriminative token-classification models (BERT-style) and generative span-extraction models (LLMs), evaluating both full fine-tuning and parameter-efficient QLoRA against baselines (pattern and dependency-based extraction, few-shot prompting). Results show that fully fine-tuned generic and legal BERT models achieve similar performance (0.89 F1), while fine-tuned LLMs match encoder accuracy for sentence-level extraction. Legal pretraining offers only small gains for generative models. In contrast, it is clearly beneficial when adaptation capacity is constrained, as parameter-efficient tuning of Legal-BERT outperforms its generic counterpart. Learning curve analysis demonstrates that legal pretraining accelerates early learning with minimal data. All approaches converge around 3K samples with diminishing returns thereafter, validating dataset sufficiency. Cross-dataset evaluation on two external regulatory corpora shows that our models behave as specialised reporting obligation extractors rather than generic regulatory classifiers. We release EURO-5K, trained models, and an interactive demo with explainability visualizations and structured RDF export. These demonstrate that both paradigms and parameter-efficient training provide practical tools for regulatory compliance automation.

</details>


### [59] [Chatbots Output Meaningful (but Problematic) Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02973)
*Matthew Stone,Una Stojnić*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLM outputs are meaningful in the ordinary semantic sense, and we do not need to ascribe human-like minds or revise language theory to explain that.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to resolve an apparent conflict between ordinary users’ intuition that chatbot answers are meaningful and influential intentionalist theories suggesting the opposite. The authors aim to defend meaningfulness without relying on de-anthropomorphizing revisions or anthropomorphic assumptions.

Method: The paper uses a conceptual and philosophical analysis of theories of language and meaning. It contrasts dominant intentionalist views with ordinary intuitions and argues that existing human language theory already accommodates meaningful LLM output without theoretical revision.

Result: The main result is a theoretical position: LLM outputs can be treated as meaningful linguistic products even though LLMs need not possess communicative intentions or other human-like cognitive capacities. The paper also clarifies that meaningfulness is a minimal semantic status, not an endorsement of truth, value, or usefulness.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that current LLM/chatbot outputs are genuinely meaningful under an ordinary theory of human language, without needing to attribute human-like intentions, mental states, or rationality to the models. Recognizing meaning in chatbot output does not amount to endorsing the content or anthropomorphizing the system.

Abstract: Are utterances by AI chatbots meaningful? Concretely, if a user asks, say, Anthropic's agent Claude, "What is the capital of Spain?" and Claude answers, "Madrid is the capital of Spain," does that sentence have its ordinary meaning -- and does it express a true proposition? Most ordinary users, as well as AI engineers, take the answer to be trivially "yes." However, many cognitive scientists, linguists, and philosophers of language argue that dominant intentionalist accounts of language and meaning deliver the opposite conclusion.
  Theorists more sympathetic to ordinary users' intuitions have therefore advocated a radical "de-anthropomorphization" of language, revising our understanding of mental states, intentions, and semantic content to capture the intuition that the outputs of LLMs are meaningful. We take a different approach. While we, too, argue that LLM outputs are meaningful, we contend that a proper theory of human language already applies, as is, to current chatbots. Meaning is a low bar: claiming that LLM outputs are meaningful does not require positing mental states, intentions, rationality, or the cognitive capacities requisite for communication in LLMs -- or, indeed, making any other anthropomorphic assumptions. People do have communicative intentions (typically successful ones), but nevertheless, even in humans, language production can depart from what the speaker has in mind.
  Our view has important consequences for how we should theorize about -- and critically engage with -- both human linguistic output and synthetically generated text. In particular, to say that chatbots produce meaningful text is not by any means to endorse what they output, or to assume that the technology is (or is not) good, powerful, appropriate, or useful.

</details>


### [60] [Memory Retrieval for Changing Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02976)
*Yuehan Qin,Li Li,Linxin Song,Wei Yang,Jiate Li,Yuqing Yang,Yue Zhao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Use Bayes factor to estimate how useful each past dialogue turn is for predicting the current response, so the system can decide both when to use memory and what to retrieve, leading to better performance on preference-sensitive long-context dialogue tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long-context dialogue systems either use heuristic retrieval rules or always-on memory, which do not handle evolving or inconsistent user preferences well. The paper is motivated by the need for a principled way to identify when memory is useful and which past turns truly reflect the user's current latent preference state.

Method: The method formulates personalized memory retrieval as utility estimation over historical dialogue turns. It measures each turn's contribution with a Bayes factor, defined by how much including that turn improves the model's likelihood of the reference response. This signal is then used for both memory access and memory selection.

Result: Across four heterogeneous memory benchmarks, the approach outperforms embedding-based retrieval on long-context, preference-heavy tasks where changing preferences matter, while staying competitive in low-memory-density settings where simple semantic similarity is often enough.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that memory retrieval in long-context dialogue should be driven by utility for modeling a user's changing latent preferences, and that a Bayes-factor-based framework provides a principled unified signal for both deciding when to access memory and which turns to retrieve.

Abstract: Long-context dialogue systems must decide both when to access memory and which parts of the interaction history are relevant. Existing approaches typically rely on heuristic retrieval signals or always-on memory usage, failing to account for the changing and potentially inconsistent nature of user preferences. In this work, we propose a unified framework for memory access and selection based on changing preferences. We formulate personalized memory retrieval as identifying which historical turns provide evidence about a user's latent preference state, rather than relying on surface-level semantic similarity. To this end, we quantify the utility of each memory turn using a Bayes factor, defined as the improvement in the model's likelihood of the reference response when the turn is included in context. This provides a principled measure of evidence strength and a unified signal for both memory access and selection. By framing memory retrieval as utility estimation, the model learns to identify salient turns and regulate memory usage based on expected utility. Experiments on four heterogeneous memory benchmarks show that our approach outperforms existing embedding-based retrieval on long-context, preference-intensive tasks where modeling changing preferences is essential, while remaining competitive in low-density regimes where semantic similarity suffices.

</details>


### [61] [Predicting Inference-Time Scaling Gains from Labeled Validation-Set Output Statistics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02981)
*Luyang Zhang,Jingyan Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that best-of-N gain can be predicted accurately from one labeled validation sampling pass using just a few stable features, avoiding the need to run full reward-model scoring for every candidate setup.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Best-of-N inference can improve model accuracy, but its gain varies across models and currently must be measured by running the full procedure. The paper aims to predict this gain cheaply and identify which output statistics form a stable, minimal predictor.

Method: The paper computes candidate features from a single labeled validation-set sampling pass, fits ridge-regression predictors for best-of-N gain, applies bootstrap-Lasso to assess feature stability, and provides a concentration analysis that includes an explicit linear-approximation residual.

Result: Across three model families, six post-training methods, and math/reasoning domains, the analysis finds a strict three-feature core: prompt-level agreement spread, label-assisted first-correct-sample position, and completion-length variance. A compact ridge predictor using this core plus entropy achieves Spearman rho = 0.90 with actual best-of-N gain under a reward-model verifier.

Conclusion: A stable and compact predictor of best-of-N inference gain can be built from a small core of validation-sampling features, allowing practitioners to screen candidate model configurations before incurring full reward-model scoring cost.

Abstract: Best-of-$N$ inference scaling (drawing $N$ candidate answers from a language model and returning the one a reward model ranks highest) improves accuracy by an amount that varies across models, but predicting that amount in advance currently requires running the procedure end-to-end. Prior work links cheap statistics of a model's sampled outputs and validation-set correctness (how often samples agree, how diverse they are, how confident the model is, and where correct samples appear) to model behavior, but does not isolate which of these form a stable, compact predictor of best-of-$N$ gain. We fit ridge predictors on features computed from a single labeled validation-set sampling pass, use bootstrap-Lasso as a stability analysis of the candidate feature set, and give a concentration analysis with an explicit linear-approximation residual. Across three base-model families, six post-training methods, and math and reasoning task domains, the stability analysis identifies a strict three-feature core spanning prompt-level agreement spread, label-assisted first-correct-sample position, and completion-length variance; a compact ridge predictor built from this core plus an entropy add-on reaches Spearman $ρ= 0.90$ with actual best-of-$N$ gain under a reward-model verifier. The intended use is labeled validation-set screening of candidate configurations before paying the full reward-model scoring cost.

</details>


### [62] [A Locally Deployed RAG-Based Academic Advising System for Course Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02983)
*Feng Li,Yoritaka Iwata*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents a privacy-preserving local RAG academic advisor that uses structured syllabus data and LLMs to help students choose courses and understand prerequisites.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Students often struggle to determine a correct course-taking sequence because of limited recognition of prerequisite relationships and information overload, while institutions also lack sufficient advising resources.

Method: The paper proposes a locally deployed retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) system that integrates large language models with retrieval from structured syllabus information to provide academic advising.

Result: The abstract indicates the system is designed to support course selection, prerequisite understanding, and personalized study planning in a privacy-preserving setting, though no quantitative evaluation results are reported.

Conclusion: A locally deployed RAG-based academic advising system built on syllabus data can improve course sequencing support, prerequisite comprehension, and personalized study planning while preserving privacy.

Abstract: The correct sequence of courses in the curriculum based on prerequisites between courses is of great importance for students to develop their knowledge and skills holistically. However, students crafting this sequence in isolation frequently struggle with recognition limitations and information overload that leads to confusion. Simultaneously, education institutions encounter difficulties in providing adequate academic advice for the correct sequence due to limited education resources. To address these challenges, we propose a locally deployed RAG-based academic advising system grounded in syllabus information. By combining large language models with retrieval from structured syllabus data, the system is designed to support course selection, prerequisite understanding, and personalized study planning in a privacy-preserving manner.

</details>


### [63] [Pretraining Language Models on Historical Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02991)
*Xiaoxi Luo,Zachary Shinnick,Niclas Griesshaber,Yixuan Wang,Junchi Yu,Freda Shi,Philip Torr,Yao Lu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A 7.24B historical English LM was trained only on pre-1913 text, with new corpus construction, leakage control, grounded instruction tuning, and benchmark design to improve historically consistent language modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing historical language models face major problems in historical data quality and scale, temporal leakage from later sources, post-training inconsistency, and weak evaluation of historical grounding.

Method: The paper trains a 7.24B language model only on English text from before 1913 using a newly built 54B-token historical corpus, adds leakage mitigation and data cleaning, applies lexically grounded instruction tuning with History-LIMA and History-SelfInstruct, and evaluates with the new History-Event benchmark.

Result: The work delivers TypewriterLM, TypewriterCorpus, two historical instruction-tuning datasets, and the History-Event benchmark as a complete resource stack for research on history-focused language models.

Conclusion: TypewriterLM demonstrates that a historically grounded language model can be built with strong temporal consistency by combining a pre-1913 corpus, leakage-aware training, grounded instruction tuning, and dedicated evaluation resources.

Abstract: We introduce TypewriterLM, a 7.24B History language model (LM) trained exclusively on English text predating 1913. Developing History LMs requires addressing challenges in data quality and availability, preventing temporal leakage, designing temporally consistent post-training pipelines, and constructing reliable evaluations. To address these issues, we construct TypewriterCorpus, a 54B-token historical corpus collected from diverse archival and linguistically annotated sources with extensive data cleaning and leakage mitigation procedures. Furthermore, we introduce lexically grounded instructing tuning, a post-training framework that constraints responses to remain directly grounded in historical source documents. Using this framework we construct two historical instruction tuning datasets: History-LIMA and History-SelfInstruct. To evaluate capability and temporal consistency, we introduce History-Event, a benchmark suite for evaluating competence, temporal grounding and data leakage. We release TypewriterLM and all associated resources to support future research on historical language models.

</details>


### [64] [Hint-Guided Diversified Policy Optimization for LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03021)
*Zhiyu Cao,Kaixin Wu,Mingjie Zhong,Peifeng Li,Xiaobo Li,Can Ye,Qiaoming Zhu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: HDPO trains LLMs to first propose several solution outlines, then pick the best one before reasoning further, which improves reasoning accuracy and solution diversity over standard outcome-only RLVR.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RLVR methods mainly reward final answer correctness and do not provide explicit incentives for exploring diverse solution paths. Since human problem solving often compares multiple approaches before committing to one, the authors aim to build this behavior into LLM training.

Method: The paper proposes Hint-Guided Diversified Policy Optimization (HDPO), a two-stage framework consisting of Cold Start for Structured Reasoning and Hint-Guided Diversified Reinforcement Learning. The model follows a 'propose-select-think' trajectory: first generating candidate solution hints, then choosing the most reliable hint, and finally performing detailed reasoning.

Result: Experiments indicate that HDPO enhances reasoning performance, increases the diversity of candidate solutions, and improves the model's ability to identify reliable solution paths.

Conclusion: HDPO improves LLM reasoning by explicitly encouraging the model to propose multiple candidate solution outlines, select the most reliable one, and then continue reasoning, leading to better performance and more diverse solution exploration.

Abstract: Recent developments in Large Language Models (LLMs) have showcased impressive reasoning capabilities, with Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) being a promising enhancement strategy. However, existing reward mechanisms are constrained to the outcome-level correctness and lack explicit signals to guide the model to consider diverse solutions. In contrast, human problem solving typically involves evaluating multiple potential approaches and selecting the most reliable solution, a cognitive process that current RLVR frameworks do not explicitly incentivize. Inspired by this, we propose Hint-Guided Diversified Policy Optimization (HDPO), allowing the model to first list all potential candidate solution outlines as hints and then select the most reliable one for further reasoning. HDPO comprises two stages of Cold Start for Structured Reasoning and Hint-Guided Diversified Reinforcement Learning to incentivize the model to generate diverse and reliable solutions following the ``propose-select-think'' trajectory. Experimental results show that HDPO effectively boosts LLM reasoning and enhances the diversity of candidate solutions as well as the LLM's ability to identify reliable solutions.

</details>


### [65] [Hallucinations as Orthogonal Noise: Inference-Time Manifold Alignment via Dynamic Contextual Orthogonalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03022)
*Mingkuan Zhao,Wentao Hu,Tianchen Huang,Yuheng Min,Suquan Chen,Yide Gao,Yanbo Zhai,Shuangyong Song,Xuelong Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper treats hallucinations as off-manifold orthogonal noise in LLM representations and introduces DCO, a lightweight inference-time method that removes those noisy components to improve faithfulness without significantly hurting knowledge recall.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to reduce hallucination in LLMs, which remains a major barrier to reliable deployment, while avoiding the common trade-off where hallucination mitigation harms knowledge-intensive performance.

Method: The method proposes Dynamic Contextual Orthogonalization (DCO), an inference-time intervention that uses the input residual stream as a dynamic context anchor, performs orthogonal decomposition on attention head outputs, and applies layer-wise Z-score suppression to selectively attenuate outlier orthogonal components.

Result: Experiments on Llama-3-8B and 70B over XSum, NQ-Swap, and IFEval show improved contextual faithfulness over state-of-the-art intervention baselines, while strong results on TriviaQA and TruthfulQA indicate better retention of parametric knowledge.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that hallucinations in LLMs can be interpreted geometrically as orthogonal noise that deviates from the semantic manifold of the residual stream, and that the proposed Dynamic Contextual Orthogonalization (DCO) can efficiently suppress such noise at inference time while preserving useful parametric knowledge.

Abstract: Hallucination in Large Language Models (LLMs), characterized by the generation of content inconsistent with contextual facts or logical constraints -- remains a persistent challenge for reliable deployment. In this work, we address this issue through a geometric framework rooted in the linear representation hypothesis. We propose that hallucinations manifest as orthogonal noise relative to the semantic manifold of the residual stream. Specifically, we hypothesize that while attention heads ideally propagate information congruent with the context subspace, hallucinations arise when specific heads introduce components orthogonal to this subspace, disrupting the coherence of the latent representation. Based on this formulation, we introduce Dynamic Contextual Orthogonalization (DCO), an inference-time intervention method. DCO utilizes the input residual stream as a dynamic context anchor to perform orthogonal decomposition on attention head outputs. To distinguish between context-aligned semantic updates and divergent noise, DCO employs a layer-wise Z-score suppression mechanism that selectively attenuates outlier orthogonal components based on statistical distributions. Evaluations on Llama-3-8B and 70B across benchmarks such as XSum, NQ-Swap, and IFEval demonstrate that DCO achieves superior contextual faithfulness compared to state-of-the-art intervention baselines. Furthermore, DCO maintains high performance on knowledge-intensive tasks like TriviaQA and TruthfulQA, effectively mitigating the trade-off between hallucination suppression and parametric knowledge retention often observed in existing methods. Our findings validate the geometric interpretation of hallucinations and establish DCO as a computationally efficient approach for enforcing manifold alignment.Our code is available at https://github.com/Harry-Miral/DCO

</details>


### [66] [SEA-Embedding: Open and Reproducible Text Embeddings for Southeast Asia](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03027)
*Peerat Limkonchotiwat,Raymond Ng,Sarana Nutanong,Jian Gang Ngui*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: An open and reproducible embedding pipeline for Southeast Asian languages matches or surpasses prior systems on SEA-BED and shows how data, objective, and encoder initialization affect robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing top embedding models are hard to reproduce because they depend on closed or undisclosed training data, and they do not provide sufficient robustness for Southeast Asian languages in real-world NLP settings.

Method: The paper introduces SEA-Embedding, an open text-embedding training pipeline for Southeast Asian languages using only publicly available data, and analyzes three factors: data composition, training objective, and base encoder initialization.

Result: SEA-Embedding achieves state-of-the-art performance on SEA-BED and provides a reproducible framework for analyzing robust text embedding design in Southeast Asian languages.

Conclusion: SEA-Embedding demonstrates that a fully open, reproducible embedding pipeline trained only on public data can achieve state-of-the-art robustness for Southeast Asian languages, while enabling systematic study of design choices.

Abstract: Text embeddings are fundamental to many downstream applications, making robustness important for real-world NLP. However, most recent state-of-the-art embedding models are not reproducible because they rely on closed or undisclosed training data, and they remain insufficiently robust for Southeast Asian languages. We present SEA-Embedding, a fully open and reproducible text-embedding pipeline for Southeast Asian languages trained only on publicly available data, and use it to study three core factors of robust embedding design: data composition, training objective, and base encoder initialization. SEA-Embedding achieves state-of-the-art results on SEA-BED while enabling systematic and reproducible analysis of robust text embeddings for the region.

</details>


### [67] [Conditional Hypothesis Generation for LLM-Based Text Analysis with Researcher-Specified Covariates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03029)
*Paiheng Xu,Jing Liu,Wei Ai*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper improves LLM hypothesis generation by conditioning on researcher-chosen covariates, reducing confounds and better surfacing subgroup-consistent language differences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard LLM-based hypothesis generation finds globally discriminative language patterns but ignores domain-relevant covariates, so discovered patterns may be confounded rather than substantively meaningful within relevant subgroups.

Method: The paper proposes conditional hypothesis generation with two econometrics-inspired techniques: feature-covariate interactions to capture sign reversals across subgroups, and within-stratum demeaning plus inverse-frequency reweighting to handle underrepresented strata.

Result: On synthetic data, each proposed method outperforms global baselines in the setting it is designed for, and expert evaluation on two real-world datasets shows covariate-aware generation yields more useful hypotheses within relevant subgroups.

Conclusion: Incorporating researcher-specified covariates into LLM-based hypothesis generation produces more interpretable and useful subgroup-valid language differences than global pattern selection, especially under stratum imbalance or sign reversal.

Abstract: A core goal of computational social science is to discover interpretable differences in how language varies across outcomes of interest, such as political affiliation or instructional quality. Recent LLM-based hypothesis generation methods describe such differences in natural language, but select for globally discriminative patterns without accounting for covariates that shape the data based on researchers' domain knowledge. When covariates are ignored, selected patterns can reflect confounds rather than differences of substantive interest. We introduce conditional hypothesis generation, a framework that incorporates researcher-specified covariates to steer hypothesis discovery toward differences that hold within relevant subgroups. Two challenges arise: the target subgroup may be underrepresented (stratum imbalance), and the direction of a difference may reverse across subgroups (sign reversal). We propose two econometrics-inspired methods: one introduces feature--covariate interactions to detect sign reversals, and the other applies within-stratum demeaning and inverse-frequency reweighting to equalize underrepresented strata. Synthetic experiments show each method outperforms global baselines in its targeted setting, and expert evaluation on two real-world datasets confirms that covariate-aware generation surfaces more useful hypotheses within relevant subgroups.

</details>


### [68] [The Deliberative Illusion: Diagnosing Factual Attrition and Stance Homogenization in Multi-Agent LLM Deliberation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03032)
*Herun Wan,Jiaying Wu,Minnan Luo,Fanxiao Li,Ningnan Wang,Nancy F. Chen,Min-Yen Kan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: 论文指出多智能体LLM审议存在“审议幻觉”：越讨论越趋同，但关键事实不断流失。作者用DelibTrace量化这一过程，发现多轮讨论可抹去大量关键事实，并削弱多样立场与抗错误信息能力。 / The paper identifies a 'deliberative illusion' in multi-agent LLMs: more discussion yields more consensus but less preserved knowledge. Using DelibTrace, the authors show that iterative discussion removes many critical facts, reduces viewpoint diversity, and increases vulnerability to misinformation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 现有多智能体LLM系统常把“达成共识”当作成功交互的标志，但对审议类问题而言，真正的可靠性取决于讨论是否保留了解释议题所需的事实与多元观点。作者因此要检验讨论是否会制造一种“审议幻觉”。 / Existing multi-agent LLM systems often treat consensus as evidence of successful interaction, but for deliberative problems reliability depends on preserving the facts and viewpoints needed to interpret the issue. The paper investigates whether discussion creates a 'deliberative illusion.'

Method: 论文提出DelibTrace框架：将议题分解为原子事实，标注其中的关键事实，将这些事实分配给不同智能体，并在多轮讨论中跟踪其保留、丢失与传播情况；随后在伦理与新闻审议任务上，对三类代表性LLM家族进行实验。 / The paper introduces DelibTrace: it decomposes an issue into atomic facts, labels issue-critical facts, distributes them across agents, and tracks their survival across discussion rounds; it is then applied to ethical and news-based deliberation across three representative LLM families.

Result: 实验发现，在多轮讨论中，系统最多会丢失72%的议题关键事实；保留下来的证据可能足以重构一个误导性的议题图景；最终立场仍然受基础模型先验影响；而且单个恶意智能体可以利用不断收缩的共享上下文注入错误信息。 / Experiments show that multi-agent discussion can erase up to 72% of issue-critical facts; the retained evidence may misleadingly reconstruct the issue; final stances remain anchored in base-model priors; and a single malicious agent can inject misinformation into the shrinking shared context.

Conclusion: 多智能体LLM讨论并不必然提升审议质量；相反，讨论过程中常出现关键事实流失与立场同质化，导致系统在“更一致”的同时“知道得更少”。因此，评估多智能体审议应关注哪些事实、不确定性与合理分歧得以保留。 / Multi-agent LLM discussion does not necessarily improve deliberative quality; instead, it often causes loss of critical facts and stance homogenization, making agents 'agree more while knowing less.' Evaluation should therefore track which facts, uncertainties, and legitimate disagreements survive interaction.

Abstract: Multi-agent LLM systems often treat consensus as evidence of successful interaction. For deliberative problems, however, reliability depends on whether agents preserve the facts and viewpoints needed to interpret an issue. We identify the deliberative illusion: discussion produces (1) factual attrition, the progressive loss of issue-critical facts, alongside (2) stance homogenization, the collapse of diverse positions toward consensus. To measure this process, we introduce DelibTrace, a framework that decomposes each issue into atomic facts, labels issue-critical ones, distributes them across agents, and tracks their survival across discussion rounds. Across ethical and news-based deliberation with three representative LLM families, multi-agent discussion erases up to 72% of issue-critical facts. This loss is consequential: retained evidence can reconstruct the issue misleadingly, final stances remain anchored in base-model priors, and a single malicious agent can inject misinformation into the shrinking shared context. These results reveal a sharper risk: agents can agree more while knowing less. We call for evaluations that measure which facts, uncertainties, and legitimate disagreements survive interaction.

</details>


### [69] [The Geometry of LLM-as-Judge: Why Inter-LLM Consensus Is Not Human Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03043)
*Sourabrata Mukherjee,Hamna Hamna,Kalika Bali,Sunayana Sitaram*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLM judges can strongly agree with each other while still being misaligned with humans. The paper shows this using geometric diagnostics on Indic benchmarks: for subjective tasks, LLM judges score in a compressed, nearly human-orthogonal subspace. Standard training helps little; small human-anchored calibration helps most, but not enough to match humans.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM judges are widely used, but they often agree more with each other than with humans. The paper investigates whether that agreement reflects shared valid signal or instead a shared bias/collapsed evaluation direction, especially in multilingual and subjective evaluation settings.

Method: The paper evaluates 41 LLM judges on four community-built Indic datasets covering eight Indic languages. It measures four geometric diagnostics on the LLM-as-judge stack: score spread, effective rank, principal angle to the human subspace, and stacked judge-human/judge-judge correlations, with bootstrap confidence intervals. It also studies the effects of fine-tuning, preference optimization, and post-hoc calibration.

Result: On subjective rubrics, LLM judges use much less of the human score range (sigma_J/sigma_H about 0.3-0.5), their scoring axis is nearly orthogonal to the human axis (87-89 degrees), and inter-LLM agreement exceeds LLM-human agreement (about 0.35 vs 0.27-0.32). On a verifiable factual rubric, alignment improves substantially (axis 58.5 degrees; r_LH = 0.519). Fine-tuning and preference optimization recover score spread but do not fix the human-misaligned axis. Post-hoc calibration on a small human-anchored set improves all rubrics and lets a calibrated 24B Indic judge outperform GPT-5.5 on correlation, but it still remains below human reliability.

Conclusion: Inter-LLM agreement is not sufficient evidence that LLM judges are aligned with human judgment. For subjective Indic evaluation rubrics, LLM judges largely agree within a compressed and human-misaligned scoring subspace; only direct geometric checks and human-anchored calibration meaningfully improve alignment, though performance still remains below human reliability.

Abstract: LMs-as-judges are now standard, yet judges agree strongly with one another while agreeing only weakly with humans. We test whether this reflects shared signal or shared bias by measuring four geometric quantities on the standard LLM-as-judge stack across four community-built Indic datasets, eight Indic languages, and 41 LLM judges: score spread, effective rank, principal angle to the human subspace, and stacked correlations among judges and humans, all with bootstrap confidence intervals.
  On subjective rubrics, judges use less than half the human score range ($σ_J / σ_H \approx 0.3$--$0.5$). Their evaluation axis is nearly orthogonal to the human one and noticeably further from humans than humans are from each other ($87^\circ$--$89^\circ$ versus $78^\circ$--$81^\circ$). Inter-LLM agreement exceeds LLM--human agreement ($r_{LL} \approx 0.35$ versus $r_{LH} \approx 0.27$--$0.32$).
  On a rubric with a verifiable factual answer, the same diagnostics fall back into the human range (axis $58.5^\circ$; $r_{LH} = 0.519$). Fine-tuning and preference optimization recover spread ($0.32 \rightarrow 1.08$) but barely move the axis (still $87^\circ$--$88^\circ$). Only post-hoc calibration on a small human-anchored set improves all four community-health rubrics together, placing a calibrated 24B Indic judge ($r = 0.184$) ahead of GPT-5.5 ($r = 0.123$), yet still short of human reliability (human-human $r = 0.474$ on the verifiable rubric).
  We argue that inter-LLM agreement should be considered evidence of human alignment only when a direct geometric check on the judge's score subspace passes; otherwise, the consensus reflects agreement within a collapsed subspace.

</details>


### [70] [G^2C-MT: Graph-Guided Context Selection for Document-Level Machine Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03078)
*Baijun Ji,Zixuan Zhou,Xiangyu Duan,Yu Liu,Longbo Sun,Rupu Wei,Bohong Zhao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Treat DocMT context selection as graph path discovery: build a lightweight paragraph discourse graph, sample context paths with depth-biased random walks, prompt the LLM with those paths, and achieve better translation than strong baselines on several LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing DocMT methods use retrieval-based or discourse-aware context selection, but they do not explicitly model structured discourse dependencies between distant paragraphs, and some discourse modeling approaches are too expensive when based on LLMs.

Method: The paper builds a lightweight discourse graph where paragraphs are nodes and pairwise edges encode semantic similarity, adjacency, and keyword overlap. It then uses a depth-biased random walk to sample backward context paths for each target paragraph, and feeds these paths as prompts to an LLM for translation. It also supports multi-path sampling and candidate aggregation for robustness.

Result: Across multiple domains, G^2C-MT outperforms strong baselines on several LLMs, including DeepSeek-V3, Gemini-2.5-Flash-lite, and Qwen-2.5/3 series, showing better handling of long-range discourse context and improved robustness under discourse ambiguity.

Conclusion: G^2C-MT demonstrates that modeling document-level MT context as structured path discovery on a lightweight discourse graph is effective and outperforms strong baselines across domains and multiple LLM backbones.

Abstract: Effective document-level machine translation (DocMT) requires capturing long-range discourse dependencies. Recent work has explored retrieval-based and discourse-aware context selection. However, these approaches often lack an explicit mechanism for modeling structured discourse dependencies between distant paragraphs in a document. In this paper, we propose G^2C-MT (Graph-Guided Context for Machine Translation), which views DocMT context selection as a structured path discovery problem on a lightweight discourse graph, rather than retrieving unstructured context sets or relying on expensive LLM-based discourse modeling. In detail, we represent each paragraph as a node and model the relationship between each pair of nodes, considering their semantic similarity, adjacency, and keyword overlap. Furthermore, we propose a depth-biased random walk over the graph to sample a backward context path for each target paragraph. The context path will be used to prompt a large language model (LLM) for translation. This framework naturally supports multi-path context sampling, which can improve robustness by aggregating diverse translation candidates for discourse-ambiguous inputs. Experiments conducted across various domains show that G^2C-MT outperforms strong baselines on multiple LLMs, including DeepSeek-V3, Gemini-2.5-Flash-lite, and the Qwen-2.5/3 series.

</details>


### [71] [Regret Pre-training: Bridging Prior and Posterior Views for Enhanced Knowledge Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03080)
*Mingkuan Zhao,Xiayu Sun,Wentao Hu,Suquan Chen,Jiaxuan Li,Xiaoyan Zhu,Xin Lai,Jiayin Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Use future context only during training to teach a causal LM better representations. Regret Pre-training adds a KL-based teacher-student loss, improves downstream accuracy without extra parameters, and GlobalRegret performs best.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard causal language model training only uses left context when predicting tokens, even though future context is available during training. This wastes potentially useful supervisory information. The paper aims to exploit this privileged future information during training while preserving causal inference at test time.

Method: The paper proposes a self-supervised pre-training framework under the LUPI paradigm. A single model produces both a causal Student distribution and a future-conditioned Teacher distribution. Training combines the standard language modeling objective with a regret loss that minimizes KL divergence from teacher to student. Two teacher variants are studied: LocalRegret, which allows attention to one future token, and GlobalRegret, which uses bidirectional context with the target token masked.

Result: After training on 4 billion tokens and evaluating on nine downstream tasks with the OLMoE-1B-7B architecture, GlobalRegret achieves 33.9% average accuracy and LocalRegret 32.2%, both outperforming the 30.2% baseline. The largest reported improvement is on BoolQ, where GlobalRegret reaches 61.0% versus 42.9% for the baseline.

Conclusion: Regret Pre-training can effectively transfer future-aware information into causal language models without adding parameters, and both GlobalRegret and LocalRegret consistently improve downstream task performance over the baseline, with GlobalRegret showing the strongest gains.

Abstract: Causal language models factorize sequence probabilities using only preceding context, leaving future information unexploited during training despite its availability in the training data. This paper introduces Regret Pre-training, a self-supervised framework grounded in the Learning Using Privileged Information (LUPI) paradigm. The framework employs a dual-view architecture in which a single model generates both a causal Student distribution and a future-conditioned Teacher distribution. The training objective augments standard language modeling with a regret loss that minimizes the KL divergence from teacher to student, transferring future-aware signals to the causal representations. We investigate two teacher configurations on the OLMoE-1B-7B architecture:LocalRegret, which extends attention by one future token, andGlobalRegret, which conditions on bidirectional context with the target position masked. Experiments on nine downstream tasks following 4 billion tokens of training demonstrate that both configurations consistently outperform the baseline. On average,GlobalRegret andLocalRegret achieve 33.9% and 32.2% accuracy respectively, surpassing the baseline's 30.2%. Most notably,GlobalRegret improves BoolQ performance by 18.1 percentage points (61.0% vs 42.9%). The framework introduces no additional parameters and requires only one extra inference-mode forward pass per training step.

</details>


### [72] [Can Factual Opinions Be Edited (Manipulated) in Large Language Models?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03096)
*Yuanpu Cao,Ziyi Yin,Fenglong Ma,Jinghui Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper studies the overlooked risk of editing factual opinions in LLMs, introduces the FOE benchmark to measure it, finds current editing methods are weak, and proposes a simple method that better aligns edited opinions with supporting evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior knowledge editing work focuses mainly on atomic facts, but manipulating factual opinions about public figures can distort public image, influence elections, and shift social views. The paper is motivated by the need to systematically assess and mitigate this underexplored risk.
