Method: The authors introduce the FOE benchmark for factual opinion editing, covering 261 public figures, 19 issue categories, and 2,178 opinion records, and evaluate current editing methods on it. They also propose a Self-Generated Evidence-Aligned approach to improve consistency between edited opinions and supporting evidence without explicit instructions.

Result: Experiments show that existing editing techniques struggle with factual opinions, often making only surface-level edits and failing to maintain consistency with generated evidence. The proposed method improves opinion-evidence alignment more effectively than prior approaches.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that factual opinion editing in LLMs poses a meaningful security risk, existing knowledge editing methods perform poorly on this setting, and the proposed Self-Generated Evidence-Aligned method better preserves alignment between edited opinions and generated supporting evidence.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly integrated into various domains, making knowledge editing techniques crucial yet potentially hazardous. Current editing methods primarily target atomic facts, overlooking the significant risks associated with manipulating factual opinions, e.g., documented stances of public figures on societal issues. Such manipulation could reshape public images, influence elections, and alter societal views. To systematically assess this threat, we introduce the Factual Opinion Editing with Evidence (FOE) benchmark, which encompasses 261 public figures, 19 issue categories, and 2,178 complete opinion records. Our evaluations demonstrate that current editing techniques struggle significantly with factual opinions, often achieving only superficial changes while failing to preserve consistency between the edited opinion and the supporting evidence generated by the model. To address this limitation, we further propose a simple yet effective Self-Generated Evidence-Aligned method that achieves opinion-evidence alignment without relying on explicit instructions. Together, our benchmark and method provide a foundation for understanding the emerging security implications of factual opinion editing in LLMs.

</details>


### [73] [PhotoCraft: Agentic Reasoning with Hierarchical Self-Evolving Memory for Deep Image Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03099)
*Kailin Lyu,Zhiqiang Yuan,Jianwei He,Qiwei Yan,Xuanbo Su,Nanxing Hu,Yang Liu,Ce Hao,Shengqian Qin,Lianyu Hu,Jinchao Zhang,Jie Zhou*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PhotoCraft adds working, episodic, and semantic memory to training-free photo-search agents, enabling better multi-step reasoning and boosting deep image search performance by up to 18.5% on DISBench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM/MLLM-based deep image search agents are largely stateless and reactive, so they struggle to retain long-horizon contextual information such as time, location, and event relations. This causes execution drift and prevents effective reuse of prior experience across tasks.

Method: The paper proposes PhotoCraft, a hierarchical memory framework for MLLM-based photo-search agents with three human-cognition-inspired components: working memory, episodic memory, and semantic memory. These memories are dynamically invoked during multi-step reasoning and answer generation to preserve context and transfer experience across tasks.

Result: On DISBench, PhotoCraft consistently improves context-aware retrieval across multiple MLLM backbones, with performance gains of up to 18.5%, and reduces key failure modes associated with memoryless deep image search.

Conclusion: PhotoCraft is a practical, training-free hierarchical memory system that makes deep image search agents more reliable, context-aware, and generalizable by addressing long-horizon reasoning and knowledge transfer limitations.

Abstract: Deep Image Search requires multi-step reasoning over rich contextual cues, such as time, location, and event relations. However, most existing LLM-based agents are stateless and reactive, lacking persistent memory to maintain long-horizon context or transfer experience across tasks, which often leads to execution drift and experience isolation. To address these limitations, we propose PhotoCraft, a training-free, hierarchical memory system for photo-search agents. Inspired by human cognition, PhotoCraft equips MLLMs with working, episodic, and semantic memory, which are dynamically invoked during reasoning to preserve logical consistency and knowledge transferability throughout multi-step reasoning and answer generation. Extensive experiments on DISBench demonstrate that PhotoCraft consistently improves context-aware retrieval across diverse MLLM backbones, achieving gains of up to 18.5\% and effectively mitigating key bottlenecks in memoryless deep image search, offering a practical path toward reliable and generalizable multimodal search agents.

</details>


### [74] [Small RL Controller, Large Language Model: RL-Guided Adaptive Sampling for Test-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03102)
*Runpeng Dai,Tong Zheng,Rui Liu,Chengsong Huang,Hongtu Zhu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper uses RL to train a lightweight controller that adaptively decides whether an LLM should keep sampling at test time or stop, improving the balance between reasoning accuracy and inference cost/latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Test-time scaling can improve LLM reasoning, but it significantly increases latency and computation cost. Existing adaptive sampling approaches reduce some cost, but they typically depend on heuristics or distributional assumptions. The paper aims to provide a principled and lightweight decision framework for balancing quality and efficiency.

Method: The method models adaptive sampling as a Markov decision process. A lightweight controller is trained with reinforcement learning to decide at each sampling round whether to stop or request more samples, using only statistics of final answers. The framework is also interpreted as a Lagrangian relaxation of a constrained optimization problem with explicit budget constraints.

Result: Experiments against strong baselines such as ASC and ESC show that the proposed controller achieves improved trade-offs among answer correctness, number of sampling rounds, and total number of samples required.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that framing adaptive test-time sampling as an MDP and learning a lightweight RL-based controller yields better trade-offs between answer correctness, latency, and computation cost than prior heuristic or assumption-based methods, while remaining practical for CPU training and deployment.

Abstract: Test-time scaling improves the reasoning performance of large language models but incurs substantial cost in both total computation and latency. Existing adaptive sampling methods partially mitigate this issue by dynamically deciding when to stop sampling, yet they typically rely on heuristic rules or rely on distribution assumptions. In this work, we formulate adaptive sampling as a Markov decision process (MDP). We train a lightweight sampling controller with reinforcement learning (RL) to jointly balance answer correctness, latency, and computation cost. At each round, the controller decides to stop sampling or to acquire additional samples. Our method is lightweight which only relies on statistics of final answers, and can be trained and deployed on CPU. We further show that the resulting framework admits an interpretation as the Lagrangian relaxation of a constrained optimization problem with explicit budget constraints. Experiments against strong baselines such as ASC and ESC show that our method achieves improved trade-offs among answer correctness, sampling rounds, and total samples required.

</details>


### [75] [Coherence Maximization Improves Pluralistic Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03110)
*Taslim Mahbub,Yiding Pei,Shi Feng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: 论文提出用 ICM 在无监督条件下生成符合特定人群价值观的示例，效果可媲美人工金标；核心发现是：示例之间的“内部一致性”比单点标签准确更能决定泛化能力。


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI 对齐需要基于具体示例的价值规范，但在缺乏大规模人工监督的前提下，如何生成有效示例仍是开放问题。论文旨在找出什么样的示例更有效，尤其关注示例之间的一致性是否是关键因素。

Method: 论文使用 Internal Coherence Maximization (ICM) 方法，通过最大化标签之间的相互可预测性来推断标签，并据此构造 persona-specific 的 in-context examples，以引导模型对齐目标群体的价值观；同时比较一致与不一致示例在相同标签准确率下的泛化表现，并研究针对低置信度问题的人类反馈策略。

Result: 在覆盖分类、偏好判断和开放式生成的四个基准上，ICM 推断得到的上下文示例达到了与 gold labels 相当的性能。并且在控制标签准确率不变时，更高一致性的示例具有明显更强的泛化能力。对于预训练中代表性不足的人群，对模型最不确定的问题进行定向人工标注，比对任意问题做同等数量标注效果更好。

Conclusion: 论文认为，一致性（coherence）是可扩展价值规范设计的关键原则。通过最大化标签之间的内部一致性，可以在几乎无需人工监督的情况下生成有效的、面向特定人格/群体价值观的示例，并显著提升泛化能力。

Abstract: Aligning AI systems with diverse human values requires value specifications grounded in concrete examples, but generating such examples without extensive human supervision remains an open challenge. We investigate what makes these examples effective, using Internal Coherence Maximization (ICM) -- which infers labels by maximizing their mutual predictability -- to generate persona-specific examples that steer a model toward a target group's values, without human supervision. Across four benchmarks spanning classification, preference, and open-ended generation, ICM-inferred in-context examples match the performance of gold labels. Crucially, coherence matters beyond individual label accuracy: with accuracy held constant, more coherent examples generalize substantially better than incoherent ones. For personas underrepresented in pretraining data, targeted human feedback on the questions where the model is least certain about a persona's values yields better generalization than the same number of labels on arbitrary questions. These results identify coherence as a key design principle for scalable value specification, leveraging the diverse human perspectives already encoded in pretrained language models.

</details>


### [76] [Experience-Driven Dynamic Exits for LLMs with Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03113)
*Yanyu Zhu,Hoilam Pao,Niu Hu,Wei Guo,Shaoxiong Zhan,Boyu Lai,Zitai Wang,Yongqin Zeng,Hai-Tao Zheng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LEDE uses offline RL to dynamically tune self-speculative decoding per token, making LLM inference notably faster than both standard decoding and static speculative methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-speculative decoding speeds up large language model inference, but static settings such as fixed exit layers and fixed speculation lengths are suboptimal across varying contexts, limiting achievable acceleration.

Method: The paper models decoding-time configuration selection as an MDP and trains LEDE with offline reinforcement learning to decide, at each generation step, the optimal early-exit layer and speculation length from local sequence context, balancing computation cost against draft quality.

Result: On Llama-2 and Llama-3, LEDE reaches about 2.0x to 2.7x speedup over standard autoregressive decoding and adds roughly 17% further speedup compared with static speculative decoding baselines.

Conclusion: LEDE formulates dynamic self-speculative decoding as a Markov Decision Process and shows that an offline reinforcement learning policy can adaptively choose exit layers and speculation lengths to improve inference efficiency over both autoregressive decoding and static speculative baselines.

Abstract: Large Language Models suffer from slow autoregressive inference. While self-speculative decoding accelerates this process, its efficiency is hampered by static configurations like fixed exit layers and speculation lengths. We reframe this optimization as a \textbf{Markov Decision Process} and propose \textbf{LEDE}, a framework that uses offline reinforcement learning. LEDE learns a policy to dynamically select the optimal exit layer and speculation length based on the local context of the generated sequence at each step, balancing computational cost and draft quality. Comprehensive evaluations on Llama-2 and Llama-3 models show LEDE achieves up to a $2.0\times$$\sim$$2.7\times$ speedup over autoregressive decoding and and provides an additional 17\% speedup over the static speculative baselines.

</details>


### [77] [DMT-CBT: Longitudinal Therapeutic State Modeling for CBT Counseling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03132)
*Chang Liu,Shuyi Zhang,Changsheng Ma,Yongfeng Tao,Minqiang Yang,Bin Hu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Model CBT as a multi-session evolving state-tracking problem, not just next-response generation; DMT-CBT and its synthetic multimodal dataset improve longitudinal counseling quality and state consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior LLM-based CBT systems treat counseling as short-horizon local response generation, which fails to reflect real clinical CBT as a longitudinal, partially observable, multimodal process with delayed intervention effects across sessions.

Method: The paper proposes DMT-CBT, a dynamic framework that maintains structured therapeutic states over multiple sessions, incorporates multimodal behavioral grounding, and uses tool-augmented interventions for adaptive therapeutic reasoning. It also introduces DMTCorpus, a synthetic multi-session multimodal CBT dataset with evolving states and cross-session continuity.

Result: Experiments show improved counseling fidelity, therapeutic alliance, longitudinal affective trajectories, and therapeutic state preservation compared with approaches relying on post-hoc state extraction.

Conclusion: DMT-CBT better matches real CBT by explicitly modeling evolving therapeutic states across sessions, leading to higher counseling fidelity, stronger therapeutic alliance, more favorable longitudinal affective trends, and more faithful state tracking than post-hoc extraction baselines.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have shown growing potential for Cognitive Behavioral Therapy (CBT) counseling. However, most existing approaches still formulate counseling as a local response generation problem, focusing on empathetic replies within short, text-only, or single-session interactions. We argue that this formulation fundamentally mismatches the nature of real psychotherapy. In clinical CBT, therapy is a longitudinal process in which therapists continuously infer, update, and intervene on evolving therapeutic states across sessions. Realistic CBT further involves multimodal inference and delayed cross-session intervention effects, requiring models to capture longitudinal therapeutic state evolution under partial observability. We propose DMT-CBT, a framework for Dynamic Modeling of evolving Therapeutic states in CBT counseling. DMT-CBT maintains structured therapeutic states across sessions while incorporating multimodal behavioral grounding and tool-augmented intervention to support adaptive therapeutic reasoning. Based on this framework, we construct DMTCorpus, a synthetic multi-session multimodal CBT counseling dataset featuring evolving therapeutic states, image-grounded client behaviors, and cross-session intervention continuity. Experimental results show that DMT-CBT improves counseling fidelity and therapeutic alliance, produces more favorable longitudinal affective trajectories, and preserves therapeutic states more faithfully than post-hoc extraction approaches.

</details>


### [78] [A cross-domain tropical species dataset with Chinese vernacular names and CITES source links](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03156)
*Jeff Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A large tropical-species dataset unifies six biodiversity taxonomic sources and adds ontology, Chinese common names with provenance, and CITES links; it achieves 99.5% Chinese-name coverage, but name-quality validation is still incomplete.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is that commercially and regulatorily related tropical species are fragmented across biodiversity infrastructures organized by biological kingdom, making cross-domain use difficult. A unified, versioned dataset is needed to support applied use cases spanning trade, husbandry, regulation, and multilingual access, especially with traceable Chinese vernacular names and CITES linkage.

Method: The authors constructed a harmonized dataset of 410,499 active tropical species across tropical plants, aquatic species, and pets by joining stable taxonomic identifiers from GBIF, POWO, iNaturalist, NCBI Taxonomy, Catalogue of Life, and EOL. They then added three original layers: a trade- and husbandry-oriented cross-domain ontology, a provenance-typed Chinese vernacular naming layer excluding unverified machine-generated names, and a CITES Species+ source-linkage layer. Coverage statistics and an internal preliminary review were used to characterize the current dataset state.

Result: The main result is a released dataset snapshot covering 410,499 active tropical species, with 99.50% Chinese vernacular coverage (408,456 taxa) and integrated identifier mappings across major biodiversity databases. The dataset is published on Zenodo under a CC-BY 4.0-compatible referencing approach for original layers, while the paper also reports that translation accuracy is not fully established and that blind external audit remains the key unresolved validation item.

Conclusion: The paper presents a versioned cross-domain tropical species dataset integrating multiple biodiversity infrastructures and adding three original contribution layers, with near-complete Chinese vernacular coverage and CITES linkage. It argues the dataset is reusable and practically valuable, but its current release still requires external validation of name quality and further release-engineering before a future formal Data Descriptor submission.

Abstract: We describe a versioned cross-domain dataset of 410,499 active tropical species (working snapshot 2026-04-20) spanning three applied subdomains -- tropical_plants, tropical_aquatic, and tropical_pets -- that share a commercial and regulatory life cycle but are distributed across kingdom-organised biodiversity infrastructures. The resource joins taxonomic identifiers from GBIF, Plants of the World Online, iNaturalist, NCBI Taxonomy, the Catalogue of Life and the Encyclopedia of Life, and adds three original layers: a cross-domain ontology that re-segments taxa along trade and husbandry contexts; a Chinese vernacular layer with explicit per-name provenance under a typology that excludes unverified machine-generated proposals; and a CITES source-linkage layer connecting each taxon to its Species+ entry. Chinese vernacular coverage -- the proportion of taxa carrying a CJK Chinese name distinct from the scientific binomial -- reaches 99.50 percent (408,456 of 410,499; full-population count). Coverage characterises completeness, not name-translation accuracy; the latter is bounded by the four-level provenance typology and is the subject of a preliminary internal review reported here, with a blind external audit identified as the principal open item. Upstream content is referenced by stable identifier only for the original-contribution layers, supporting CC-BY 4.0 reuse. The dataset is deposited on Zenodo (10.5281/zenodo.20377811). This preprint is the canonical v1.0 description of the dataset's current state; future Data Descriptor submission is anticipated but is contingent on the validation and release-engineering items listed in the Limitations.

</details>


### [79] [Fully Automated Identification of Lexical Alignment and Preference-Stage Shifts in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03165)
*Thomas Stephan Juzek,Xiaoyang Ming,Jose A. Hernandez*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces two automatic metrics to detect words that chat assistants overuse compared with human scientific writing and to estimate whether this comes from preference tuning. Tested on PubMed continuations from six LLM families, the method finds stable, repeatable signs of lexical misalignment and scales beyond English scientific text.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing studies of LLM-human lexical divergence mainly focus on Scientific English and depend on manual curation. The paper aims to provide scalable, curation-free, assumption-light metrics for systematically measuring lexical misalignment and tracing its possible origin in preference learning.

Method: The authors propose two automatic metrics: Lexical Alignment Score (to detect lexical overuse) and Triangulated Preference Shift (to estimate the contribution of human preference learning). They generate continuations from PubMed abstracts and measure windowed document prevalence across six model families: Falcon, Gemma, Llama, Mistral, OLMo, and Yi.

Result: The approach automatically identifies overused lexical items such as 'suggest', 'additionally', and 'strategy'. It also reproduces prior findings and shows robustness across parameter choices, random seeds, and extra evaluation datasets, while linking part of the observed shift to preference learning.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that two curation-free, assumption-light metrics can reliably detect lexical misalignment in LLM-generated scientific text and estimate how much of that shift is associated with human preference learning. The method is stable across models, settings, seeds, and additional data, and is scalable to other domains and languages.

Abstract: The language used by digital chat assistants such as ChatGPT can diverge from human expectations (misalignment). Research, mostly on Scientific English, has described both what divergences occur and, to some extent, why, linking them to the training stage of human preference learning. Yet, existing approaches rely on manual curation. This paper introduces two curation-free, assumption-light evaluation metrics: the Lexical Alignment Score, which identifies lexical overuse, and the Triangulated Preference Shift, which quantifies how much of such shifts can be attributed to human preference learning. Using PubMed abstracts, continuations were generated and measured using windowed document prevalence across six model families (Falcon, Gemma, Llama, Mistral, OLMo, Yi). The procedure identifies, without manual intervention, overused items such as 'suggest', 'additionally', and 'strategy', and estimates their link to preference learning. Our findings replicate prior work and remain stable across parameter settings, random seeds, and evaluation on further data. The approach scales readily and enables systematic study of lexical (mis)alignment beyond Scientific English and across languages, and as such, the metrics have the potential to contribute to improved alignment for future models and understanding of its origins.

</details>


### [80] [HyperPatch: Sequential Knowledge Editing Under n-ary Structural Drift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03179)
*Yu-Kai Chan,Wen-Sheng Lien,Dong-Ting Yao,Bo-Kai Ruan,Kwan-Yeung Lin,Hong-Han Shuai,Meng-Fen Chiang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: HyperPatch treats sequential knowledge editing for n-ary facts as a hypergraph stability problem, preserving relational structure and delivering much better robustness and multi-hop accuracy than triple-based baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing knowledge editing methods assume binary triples, but real-world knowledge is often n-ary. Sequential updates in changing environments break the atomic structure of events, causing structural drift and retriever grounding failures that are often mistaken for hallucinations.

Method: The paper proposes HyperPatch, a parameter-preserving framework with three phases: Structural Prior Initialization using contrastive learning on an HGNN, Sequential Topology Editing via SimHash-based topological alignment plus Topological LoRA adaptation, and Structure-Conditioned Reasoning over fused linguistic and structural manifolds.

Result: On MQuAKE-CF and MQuAKE-T, HyperPatch improves Hop-wise Accuracy by 96.24% and 21.06% over the strongest baseline. Ablation studies show better reliability under continuous n-ary updates, while standard KG-based variants can suffer up to 88.3% H-Acc collapse from structural misalignment.

Conclusion: HyperPatch effectively addresses sequential knowledge editing for n-ary facts in non-stationary environments by preserving event integrity on hypergraph manifolds, substantially improving multi-hop factual accuracy and robustness over standard KG-based methods.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) rely on Knowledge Editing (KE) to maintain temporal validity, yet real-world knowledge is inherently n-ary. We demonstrate that in non-stationary environments, sequential updates to complex relations induce N-ary Structural Drift, a phenomenon where the binary reification of n-ary events into triples fractures relational atomicity. This precipitates Structure-Conditioned Knowledge Transfer Failure, a systematic mis-grounding of the retriever frequently misdiagnosed as parametric hallucination. To tackle this, we propose HyperPatch, a parameter-preserving framework that reformulates sequential KE as a stability problem over hypergraph manifolds. HyperPatch preserves event integrity through three phases: (i) Structural Prior Initialization, establishing a topology-aware embedding space via contrastive learning on a Hypergraph Neural Network (HGNN) to capture high-order correlations; (ii) Sequential Topology Editing, utilizing a dual-stage mechanism that employs SimHash-based Topological Alignment for rapid conflict resolution and Topological LoRA Adaptation to track drift without backbone retraining; and (iii) Structure-Conditioned Reasoning, which integrates globally consistent evidence from fused linguistic and structural manifolds. On the MQuAKE-CF and MQuAKE-T benchmarks, HyperPatch achieves relative gains in Hop-wise Accuracy (H-Acc) of 96.24% and 21.06% over the strongest baseline, respectively. Further ablations demonstrate superior reliability under continuous n-ary update streams, whereas the standard KG-based variant suffers H-Acc collapses of up to 88.3% due to structural misalignment.

</details>


### [81] [SenseJudge: Human-Centric Preference-Driven Judgment Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03189)
*Rui Li,Junfeng Liu,Xiangwen Kong,Linhai Xu,Zhifang Sui*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces SenseJudge and SenseBench to make LLM judges more customizable and human-aligned; experiments show better personalized judging and more human-consistent model ranking than existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-as-judge methods usually depend on fixed preference data and trained judges, which limits their ability to reflect diverse user preferences and adapt to realistic human-AI dialogue settings.

Method: The paper proposes SenseJudge, a human-preference-driven customizable judgment framework, and SenseBench, a challenging benchmark built from real-world multi-turn instruction-following interactions. It evaluates the framework on personalized judging and model ranking, with additional analyses of position bias, consistency, and ablations.

Result: Experiments show SenseJudge outperforms competing judgment methods and models on personalized judging, yields rankings aligned with human sense, and remains robust under bias, consistency, and ablation analyses.

Conclusion: SenseJudge is a robust and customizable LLM-judging framework that better matches diverse human preferences than prior fixed-preference judges, and it produces model rankings more consistent with human judgment.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) as judges across various scenarios such as assessing model responses is becoming an increasingly accepted paradigm. However, existing judgment approaches often rely on trained judgers using fixed preference data, which tend to overlook diverse user preferences and struggle to adapt to real-world human-AI dialogue scenarios. To address these limitations, we propose SenseJudge, a customizable judgment framework driven by human preferences and SenseBench, a diverse and challenging instruction-following benchmark derived from real-world multi-turn interactions. We applied the automatic judgment framework and benchmark to two tasks: (1) LLMs as personalized judges, and (2) model ranking. We conducted extensive experiments, and the results demonstrate that the SenseJudge framework surpasses other judgment methods and models in the LLMs-as-personalized-judges task and achieves model ranking that aligns with real human sense. Additionally, we conducted analyses on position bias and consistency, alongside ablation studies, which affirmed the robustness of SenseJudge.

</details>


### [82] [MemTrain: Self-Supervised Context Memory Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03197)
*Ziheng Li,Xingrun Xing,Haoqing Wang,Zhi-Hong Deng,Yehui Tang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MemTrain teaches LLM agents to better maintain and recall memory through self-supervised reconstruction tasks on Wikipedia, yielding substantial downstream gains on memory-heavy QA tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory-agent training relies on expensive, limited, end-to-end reinforcement learning data for downstream tasks, which lacks diversity and does not adequately cover general memory behaviors.

Method: The paper proposes MemTrain, a self-supervised framework trained on unlabeled Wikipedia using two coupled proxy tasks: an end-to-end masked reconstruction objective and an intermediate memory recall objective, jointly optimized with GRPO.

Result: Across long-text QA and search-based QA benchmarks, MemTrain consistently improves memory-intensive reasoning performance for different models, with gains up to 17.67 points.

Conclusion: MemTrain can generally improve the context-memory ability of long-horizon LLM agents and leads to stronger downstream memory-intensive reasoning, outperforming direct task-specific post-training by up to 17.67 points.

Abstract: Memory is an indispensable capability for long-horizon LLM agents, enabling them to preserve and utilize information accumulated across extended interactions. Existing memory-agent approaches are typically trained end-to-end with reinforcement learning on downstream tasks. However, collecting high-quality annotated problems for memory-intensive scenarios is costly, and the resulting training data often lack sufficient diversity to cover general memory behaviors. In this work, we propose MemTrain, a self-supervised training framework for generally enhancing the context-memory capability of LLM agents for more effective downstream post-training. MemTrain introduces two coupled proxy tasks over unlabeled Wikipedia corpora: (1) an end-to-end masked reconstruction objective, which requires the model to recover masked entities after multiple rounds of memory updates, thereby encouraging memory maintenance from the final outcome perspective; and (2) an intermediate memory recall objective, which requires the model to reconstruct masked historical information using intermediate memory states, encouraging faithful compression and memory completeness throughout the interaction process. The two objectives are jointly optimized using GRPO. Extensive experiments on long-text QA and search-based QA benchmarks demonstrate that MemTrain consistently improves downstream memory-intensive reasoning performance across different models, achieving gains of up to 17.67 points over direct task-specific post-training.

</details>


### [83] [AI Rater Discrimination Depends on Scoring Protocol in Complex Clinical Decision-Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03198)
*Sangwon Baek,Kyu Yeon Hur,Kyunga Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLM raters look overly generous and less informative without a rubric. Patient-specific rubric anchoring makes scores more spread out, better separates stronger from weaker clinical AI outputs, and exposes differences between rater models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used as AI raters in clinical AI evaluation, but their scoring behavior across evaluation settings has not been quantitatively characterized. The study aims to determine how scoring protocol and design choices affect rating behavior, discrimination, and reliability in a complex clinical decision-making task.

Method: The paper conducts a factorial study in adult type 2 diabetes pharmacotherapy at 12-month outpatient follow-up using four open-source LLMs as both CDSS models and AI raters. CDSS outputs were evaluated on seven questions under two scoring protocols (GR vs Non-GR). Linear mixed-effects models estimated main effects and interactions between scoring protocol and five design factors: CDSS model, prompt configuration (DRG vs Baseline), rater model, prompt character, and prompt type.

Result: Compared with GR, Non-GR produced consistently higher scores in a narrow range, with mean scores lower by 7.69 to 49.64 points under GR and interquartile ranges 1.68 to 3.67 times wider. GR increased discrimination between DRG and Baseline CDSS outputs by 1.76 to 5.10 times across questions and revealed substantial variation across rater models that Non-GR obscured.

Conclusion: Rubric-anchored scoring (Gold Rubric, GR) better preserves discriminative power and exposes meaningful differences among AI raters in clinical AI evaluation, while rubric-free scoring (Non-GR) systematically inflates scores and masks variability; therefore, Non-GR should not replace GR for patient-specific or jurisdiction-specific clinical evaluation tasks.

Abstract: Clinical AI evaluation increasingly delegates scoring to large language models (LLMs) acting as AI raters, yet their scoring behavior across evaluation conditions has not been quantitatively characterized. We address this gap through a factorial study of AI rater behavior in adult type 2 diabetes (T2D) pharmacotherapy at 12-month outpatient follow-up, a clinical task involving complex decision-making operationalized across seven evaluation questions. Four open-source LLMs served simultaneously as clinical decision support system (CDSS) models and AI raters. Each CDSS output was scored under two scoring protocols: a rubric-anchored Gold Rubric (GR) protocol incorporating a patient-specific rubric, and a rubric-free Non Gold Rubric (Non-GR) protocol. Linear mixed effects models crossed the scoring protocol factor with five design factors -- CDSS model, CDSS prompt configuration (document-referenced generation [DRG] vs.\ Baseline), rater model, prompt character, and prompt type -- and estimated main effects together with their protocol interactions. Across all questions, AI raters yielded consistently higher scores within a very narrow range (74--78 points on average) under Non-GR compared to those under GR (7.69 to 49.64 points lower mean scores; 1.68 to 3.67 times wider interquartile ranges). Within each question, GR amplified the AI rater's discrimination between DRG and Baseline CDSS outputs by factors of 1.76 to 5.10, while also revealing substantial behavioral variation across rater models that Non-GR suppressed. These findings support rubric anchoring as the scoring protocol that preserves discriminative power in clinical AI evaluation; rubric-free scoring cannot substitute when questions require patient-specific or jurisdiction-specific criteria that rater models cannot infer from parametric knowledge alone.

</details>


<div id='cs.GR'></div>

# cs.GR [[Back]](#toc)

### [84] [Fewer, Better Frames: A Compute-Normalized Proof of Concept for Coherence-First World-Model Rendering with Model-Guided FSR4 Frame Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02586)
*Paweł Katarzyński*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: Instead of maximizing native FPS, the paper shows that spending compute on fewer, more coherent anchor frames and reconstructing intermediate frames can yield more stable long-horizon world-model videos under the same approximate compute budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is that evaluating world models by native frame cadence alone can be misleading because higher nominal FPS may sacrifice long-horizon scene coherence and scene stability.

Method: The method compares two branches implemented with Overworld's Waypoint-1.5 and WorldEngine on a Windows fallback stack using ONNX Runtime + DirectML and an FSR4 DX12 bridge: a coherence-first branch generating higher-context anchor frames at 15 FPS and reconstructing 30 FPS via latent-delta motion guidance and synthesized depth, versus a cadence-first baseline generating about 30 FPS natively under matched seed, route, control script, duration, and local time-scaling conditions.

Result: Across forest, sword, desert, and snow scenes, the coherence-first branch better preserves path geometry, object identity, large silhouettes, and depth layering, while the baseline degrades earlier with brightness drift and geometric distortion. LPIPS favors the coherence-first branch in all tested scenes. A heavier sword-scene probe also shows non-monotonicity, meaning more context and denoising do not always improve quality.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that, under a limited inference budget, a coherence-first world-model rendering strategy that generates lower native FPS anchor frames with higher context and reconstructs presentation FPS can preserve long-horizon scene stability better than a native higher-FPS baseline, though it is only a proof of concept and not a finished realtime renderer.

Abstract: World models are often evaluated by native frame cadence, but higher nominal frame rate can trade away long-horizon scene stability. This article reports an independent proof of concept implemented using Overworld's Waypoint-1.5 family and WorldEngine runtime on a Windows fallback stack with ONNX Runtime + DirectML and an FSR4 DX12 bridge. The tested coherence-first branch generates higher-context anchor frames at a 15 FPS presentation-timeline cadence and reconstructs presentation to 30 FPS using latent-delta motion guidance and synthesized depth. It is compared against a lower-context cadence-first baseline that generates about 30 FPS natively under the same seed, route, control script, target presentation duration, and local time-scaling regime. Across forest, sword, desert, and snow scenes, the coherence-first branch preserves path geometry, object identity, large silhouettes, and depth layering longer, while the baseline degrades earlier into brightness drift and geometric distortion. Lightweight temporal metrics and paired videos support the visual comparison, with LPIPS favoring the coherence-first branch across all tested scenes. Here compute-normalized means approximately matched same-GPU, same-timescale operating points, not exact FLOP parity or measured realtime throughput. A separate heavier sword-scene probe suggests local non-monotonicity: more context and denoising did not automatically improve quality. These results support coherence-first allocation as a practical proof-of-concept strategy under limited inference budget, not as a finished realtime renderer.

</details>


### [85] [A Novel Procedural Generation for Level Design of Mansions and Dungeons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03857)
*Isaac Fiuza Vieira,Kathya Silvia Collazos Linares,Esteban Walter Gonzalez Clua,Érick Oliveira Rodrigues*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: The paper presents a BSP-plus-graph PCG method for indoor maps that is controllable, reproducible, and usually produces navigable layouts; in 100,000 tests, over 91% of maps were fully connected.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Purely procedural generation can reduce development cost and increase variety, but without level design guidance it often produces incoherent layouts and poor gameplay. The paper aims to retain PCG efficiency while improving architectural coherence and navigability.

Method: The method uses three stages: Binary Space Partitioning for spatial segmentation, graph-traversal-based room connection to avoid redundant links, and post-processing to remove structural artifacts and improve visual cohesion; room size/shape are parameterized and randomness is seed-controlled.

Result: Experiments show the approach is flexible across seeds and parameter settings, and BFS-based evaluation on 100,000 generated maps found that more than 91% were fully connected when parameters were chosen appropriately.

Conclusion: A level-design-guided PCG pipeline can generate indoor environments that are structurally coherent and largely navigable, with connectivity exceeding 91% under suitable parameter settings.

Abstract: Procedural Content Generation (PCG) has become an essential technique in game development due to its ability to reduce production time and cost while increasing replayability and variety. However, when not aligned with level design principles, PCG can lead to incoherent spatial structures and poor gameplay experiences. Objective: This work proposes a PCG method guided by level design principles to generate structured indoor environments - such as houses, mansions, and dungeons - aiming to ensure both architectural coherence and navigability. Methodology: The method is divided into three main stages: segmentation of the space using Binary Space Partitioning (BSP); logical connection of rooms based on graph traversal to prevent redundant links; and a post-processing stage responsible for cleaning structural artifacts and improving visual cohesion. The methodology allows parameterization of room area and shape, with randomness controlled via seeds for reproducibility. Results: Two experiments were conducted. The first demonstrated the flexibility of the methodology under different seeds and parameter configurations. The second evaluated the navigability of generated maps by verifying connectivity using Breadth-First Search (BFS). In this test, 100,000 maps were generated, and with suitable parameters, over 91% of them achieved complete connectivity.

</details>


<div id='cs.DC'></div>

# cs.DC [[Back]](#toc)

### [86] [GreenGNN: Energy-Aware Windowed Communication Optimization for Distributed GNN Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02916)
*Arefin Niam,Tevfik Kosar,M. S. Q. Zulkar Nine*

Main category: cs.DC

TL;DR: GreenGNN cuts energy in distributed GNN training by batching remote feature fetches and caching hot features across short windows of mini-batches, yielding major energy savings and faster training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Distributed large-scale GNN training incurs high energy waste because mini-batch neighbor sampling causes many small remote feature fetches across partitions; each RPC has fixed overhead, and GPUs consume considerable idle power while waiting for remote data.

Method: The paper proposes GreenGNN, an energy-aware distributed GNN training system that groups training into windows of consecutive mini-batches, caches hot features locally for each window, merges remote feature requests into bulk transfers, preserves an on-demand path for cache misses, and chooses the window size offline using a discrete-event simulator with a hybrid energy model based on a deterministic one-epoch access trace.

Result: Implemented on DGL and evaluated on a 4-node GPU cluster, GreenGNN reduces total system energy by 27–43% over the baseline, improves end-to-end throughput by up to 3.9x, and lowers GPU energy by 36–71%.

Conclusion: GreenGNN demonstrates that exploiting short-term temporal locality in distributed sampling-based GNN training can substantially reduce communication-related energy use while also increasing throughput.

Abstract: Large-scale graph neural network (GNN) training often requires distributed clusters because graph structure and feature tensors no longer fit in a single node's memory. In sampling-based training, each mini-batch expands into a receptive field that spans partitions and triggers thousands of remote feature fetches per epoch. This wastes energy for two main reasons: each small RPC pays a fixed initiation and protocol cost, and GPUs continue drawing substantial baseline power while waiting for remote features. We present GreenGNN, an energy-aware distributed GNN training system that reduces communication energy by exploiting the bursty, short-lived temporal locality of neighbor sampling. GreenGNN groups training into windows of W consecutive mini-batches, stages each window's hot features in a local cache, and merges remote requests from each partition owner into a small number of bulk transfers. This amortizes RPC overhead across many features while preserving an on-demand path for cache misses. Because window size controls the trade-off between communication amortization and hot-set staleness, GreenGNN selects W offline using a discrete-event simulator that replays a deterministic one-epoch access trace with a hybrid energy model. We implement GreenGNN on DGL and evaluate it on a 4-node GPU cluster with benchmark datasets. Across datasets and batch sizes, GreenGNN reduces total system energy by 27--43% relative to baseline while improving end-to-end throughput by up to 3.9x. GPU energy drops by 36--71%, driven by fewer RPC initiations and lower GPU stall time.

</details>


### [87] [FOLD: Fuzzy Online Deduplication for Very Large Evolving Datasets via Approximate Nearest Neighbor Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03001)
*Nelson Bore,Pritish Mishra,Constantin Adam,Eyal de Lara,Oana Balmau*

Main category: cs.DC

TL;DR: FOLD is an online fuzzy deduplication system for continuously growing LLM corpora. It uses an incremental HNSW index plus a bitmap-based similarity signal to efficiently find likely duplicates, delivering much higher recall and better throughput than prior scalable baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing fuzzy deduplication methods, especially classic Locality-Sensitive Hashing pipelines, do not scale well as training corpora grow and are poorly suited for continuously ingested, evolving datasets. The paper is motivated by the need for a high-recall, high-throughput online deduplication system for large language model data pipelines.

Method: The paper proposes FOLD, an online fuzzy deduplication system that incrementally updates an HNSW index over accepted documents. For each new document, it retrieves a small candidate neighborhood from the index and then performs fuzzy duplicate detection, avoiding repeated global bucket rebuilds or full rescans. To improve HNSW traversal, it replaces raw Jaccard-based search with a bitmap representation that preserves Jaccard alignment while providing better score discrimination.

Result: Across LM1B, C4, RealNews, and Common Crawl, FOLD remains fast and accurate as dataset size increases. At the largest tested scales, it achieves 93–97% recall and up to 2.09x higher throughput than competing methods, while the best alternative reaches only 76% recall.

Conclusion: FOLD demonstrates that online fuzzy deduplication can remain both accurate and efficient at LLM-corpus scale by using an incrementally maintained HNSW index and a more discriminative bitmap-based search signal aligned with Jaccard similarity.

Abstract: Fuzzy deduplication is key to constructing large language model training corpora. However, classic Locality-Sensitive Hashing pipelines scale poorly as corpora grow and are ill-suited to continuous ingestion. We present FOLD (Fuzzy Online Deduplication), an online fuzzy deduplication system that delivers high recall and throughput for evolving datasets. FOLD maintains an incrementally updated HNSW index over admitted documents, retrieving a small, high-quality candidate neighborhood for each incoming document instead of repeatedly rebuilding global buckets or rescanning the accumulated corpus. To our knowledge, FOLD is the first online fuzzy deduplication system to use HNSW. However, applying Jaccard similarity out of the box causes score crowding, making graph traversal unreliable within a small number of steps. FOLD addresses this with a bitmap representation that provides a more discriminative, Jaccard-aligned signal during HNSW search. Across four LLM-scale datasets (LM1B, C4, RealNews, and Common Crawl), FOLD stays fast and accurate as the corpus grows: at the largest evaluated scales, it maintains 93-97% recall and achieves up to 2.09x higher throughput than competing alternatives, whose best recall reaches only 76%.

</details>


### [88] [Brief Announcement: Generative Markov Model for Distributed Computing Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03061)
*Alfreds Lapkovskis,Ali Beikmohammadi,Sindri Magnússon,Praveen Kumar Donta*

Main category: cs.DC

TL;DR: 论文提出一种面向分布式计算系统的结构化生成式马尔可夫模型，通过状态因子化把复杂系统变得可分析、可仿真、可优化；案例研究表明，把AI推理任务分散到用户设备比集中调度更具扩展性。 / TL;DR: The paper introduces a structured generative Markov model for distributed computing systems that makes complex system states tractable for analysis, simulation, and optimization; a case study shows distributed AI inference across user devices scales better than centralized scheduling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 动机在于新兴分布式计算范式（如 computing continuum）具有异构性、随机性和复杂性，现有方法缺乏统一的形式化模型来高效利用全域资源，因此需要一个既能表达系统复杂状态、又能支持优化和决策的统一框架。 / The motivation is that emerging distributed computing paradigms such as the computing continuum are heterogeneous, stochastic, and complex, yet lack a unified formal model that can capture system-wide behavior and support efficient resource utilization, optimization, and decision-making.

Method: 方法上，作者将分布式计算系统建模为一个基于结构化系统状态分解的生成式马尔可夫模型。系统状态先分解为高维变量，再按元素进一步因子化，以利用分布式系统中的稀疏依赖结构，从而支持可 tractable 的仿真、推断与策略学习，并连接马尔可夫链理论与强化学习。 / Methodologically, the authors model distributed computing systems as a generative Markov model factorized over a structured system state. The state is decomposed into high-dimensional variables and further factorized over elements to exploit sparse dependencies, enabling tractable simulation, inference, and policy learning while linking distributed computing with Markov chain theory and reinforcement learning.

Result: 结果表明，该框架能够在原本难以处理的系统状态上实现建模、仿真、推断和策略学习。在协作式AI推理实验中，分布式利用用户设备资源相比集中式调度表现出更低的延迟和更低的服务器资源占用。 / The results show that the framework makes modeling, simulation, inference, and policy learning feasible over otherwise intractable system states. In the collaborative AI inference case study, distributed use of user devices achieves lower latency and lower server resource consumption than centralized scheduling.

Conclusion: 该论文提出了一个用于分布式计算系统的生成式马尔可夫建模框架，并通过协作式AI推理案例表明：在大规模场景下，集中式调度会成为瓶颈，而将计算分散到用户设备能够降低时延并减少服务器资源消耗。 / The paper concludes that a generative Markov modeling framework can effectively represent distributed computing systems, and its collaborative AI inference case study shows that centralized scheduling becomes a bottleneck at scale, while distributing computation to user devices reduces latency and server resource usage.

Abstract: Emerging distributed computing paradigms, such as the computing continuum, are inherently heterogeneous, stochastic, and complex. Efficiently and effectively utilizing all available resources across the continuum demands a unified formal model of the system. To address this gap, we propose a general framework for modeling distributed computing systems as a generative Markov model, factorized over a structured system state. In our model, the state decomposes into high-dimensional variables, each further factorized over its elements, reflecting the sparse dependency structure inherent to distributed systems. This yields a tractable model enabling simulation, inference, and policy learning over otherwise intractable system states, bridging distributed computing with Markov chain theory and reinforcement learning (RL). We demonstrate our framework through a case study of collaborative AI inference, in which a dedicated server combines resources with those volunteered by service users. Our results show that centralized scheduling becomes a bottleneck at scale, while distributing computation across user devices reduces both latency and server resource consumption. These findings highlight the value of adaptive decision-making in distributed computing systems and demonstrate the framework's utility for modeling, simulation, and optimization.

</details>


### [89] [BlobShuffle: Cost-Effective Repartitioning in Stream Processing Systems via Object Storage Exemplified with Kafka Streams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03364)
*Sören Henning,Otmar Ertl,Adriano Vogel*

Main category: cs.DC

TL;DR: BlobShuffle replaces direct record-by-record shuffle with object-storage-backed batched exchange plus lightweight notifications, cutting cloud shuffle cost by 40x+ while maintaining low latency and Kafka Streams guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Native stream shuffling in cloud deployments is expensive because it generates heavy cross-availability-zone network traffic and requires operating a high-throughput, strongly consistent messaging backbone. The work is motivated by the need to lower shuffle cost without sacrificing correctness or requiring major infrastructure changes.

Method: The paper proposes BlobShuffle, which batches shuffled records, stores the batches in cloud object storage, sends compact notifications through Kafka, and lets downstream operators fetch and extract the needed records. It also uses configurable batching and distributed caching, and is implemented as an add-on to Kafka Streams with minimal application changes.

Result: In a large-scale Kubernetes-based AWS evaluation, BlobShuffle reduces shuffling cost by more than 40x versus native Kafka Streams, keeps 95th percentile shuffle latency below 2 seconds, and scales beyond 2 GiB/s without hitting a scalability limit in the reported experiments.

Conclusion: BlobShuffle demonstrates that using cloud object storage as an intermediate exchange layer can dramatically reduce cross-AZ shuffle cost while preserving Kafka Streams consistency and correctness, making large-scale shuffle-intensive stream processing economically viable.

Abstract: Shuffling or repartitioning data streams is an essential operation of state-of-the-art stream processing frameworks to support stateful workloads in a large-scale, distributed setting. In today's cloud deployments, however, shuffling can become a major cost driver due to substantial network traffic across multiple availability zones (AZs) as well as an operational burden when operating a high-throughput, strongly consistent messaging backbone at scale. We present BlobShuffle, a novel approach to cost-effective shuffling for stream processing systems that leverages cloud object storage as an intermediate exchange layer. Instead of sending all shuffled records directly, BlobShuffle groups records into batches, stores these batches in cloud object storage, and forwards only compact notifications. Downstream operators use these notifications to retrieve the relevant batches and extract the corresponding records. BlobShuffle balances cost efficiency and latency through configurable batching and a distributed caching mechanism. BlobShuffle is implemented as an add-on for Kafka Streams that requires only minimal code changes to existing applications, leaves Kafka and the underlying infrastructure unmodified, and preserves Kafka Streams' consistency and correctness guarantees. In a large-scale experimental evaluation on a Kubernetes-based AWS deployment, we show that BlobShuffle can reduce shuffling costs by more than 40x compared to native Kafka Streams shuffling while keeping the 95th percentile shuffle latency below 2 seconds. Moreover, it scales to processing more than 2 GiB/s without encountering a scalability limit in our experiments, indicating that BlobShuffle can economically support shuffle-intensive workloads at large scale.

</details>


### [90] [Predicting Lakehouse Performance in Clouds: An Empirical Exploration of Query Runtime Variance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03464)
*James Nurdin,Wei Liu,Richard Mccreadie,Lauritz Thamsen*

Main category: cs.DC

TL;DR: The paper shows that lakehouse queries have high runtime variability, identifies why, and proves that modeling or mitigating this variability makes performance prediction much more accurate and carbon-aware scheduling more effective.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Distributed lakehouse operators need accurate Query Performance Prediction to optimize monetary, resource, and environmental costs, but runtime variance in lakehouses has not been systematically characterized, limiting reliable workload management.

Method: The paper empirically measures runtime variance for distributed analytical queries on Kubernetes-based lakehouse deployments across three public clouds and one private cloud, multiple data scales, and three benchmarks; then performs factor analysis on sources such as data locality, co-tenant load, and caching; and evaluates the effect of variance mitigation on state-of-the-art QPP models and low-carbon scheduling.

Result: Repeated executions of the same query can differ in runtime by nearly 2x. Key variance factors include data locality, co-tenant interference, and caching. Addressing these sources reduces QPP prediction error by up to 80% and improves low-carbon scheduling outcomes with significant carbon cost reductions.

Conclusion: Lakehouse query runtimes exhibit substantial run-to-run variance across cloud environments, and explicitly accounting for major variance sources can greatly improve QPP accuracy and reduce downstream carbon costs in workload scheduling.

Abstract: Data analytics increasingly runs on distributed lakehouse systems, where platform operators must optimise monetary, resource, and environmental costs. Query Performance Prediction (QPP) helps to balance these costs and supports workload management techniques, such as adaptive resource scaling and low-carbon scheduling. However, runtimes in lakehouses can vary substantially, and the impact of runtime variance on QPP accuracy and workload orchestration has not previously been systematically studied for lakehouse systems.
  This paper addresses this gap by investigating the runtime variance observed for distributed lakehouse analytical queries and its impact on QPP. First, we quantify the run-to-run variance using Kubernetes deployments across three public clouds and one private cloud, spanning multiple database scales and three analytical benchmarks. Our results demonstrate that repeated executions of the same query can vary in runtime by nearly twofold. Second, we conduct a factor analysis study assessing key sources of this runtime variance such as data locality, co-tenant load, and caching effects. Third, we examine how variance influences state-of-the-art QPP models, revealing that addressing key sources of variance can reduce prediction error up to 80%. Finally, we demonstrate the downstream implications for low-carbon scheduling as an example of a workload management technique that relies on performance prediction, showing that accounting for runtime variance can lead to a significant reduction in carbon costs.

</details>


### [91] [Fast TetraBFT: Optimizing Latency Where It Matters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03754)
*Antonio J. Fernández-Pinto,Manuel Bravo,Gregory Chockler,Alexey Gotsman*

Main category: cs.DC

TL;DR: A simpler wrapper around TetraBFT is enough to match the best known 3-delay good-case latency for unauthenticated Byzantine consensus, without losing its space or communication efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to improve the practicality of unauthenticated Byzantine consensus, which is valuable for blockchains without symmetric trust assumptions and for post-quantum settings. Existing optimal-latency protocol ForgetIT reaches 3 message delays but is highly complex, motivating a simpler protocol with the same optimal good-case latency.

Method: The authors design Fast TetraBFT by augmenting the existing TetraBFT unauthenticated Byzantine consensus protocol with a simple fast-path wrapper tailored for partially synchronous networks. They analyze how this wrapper reduces the protocol’s good-case decision latency from 5 to 3 message delays without sacrificing core efficiency properties.

Result: Fast TetraBFT achieves optimal good-case latency of 3 message delays in partially synchronous networks, improving TetraBFT’s latency from 5 to 3 message delays. At the same time, it retains bounded space requirements and low communication complexity, showing that optimal latency can be obtained with a simpler design than prior work.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that the complexity of prior optimal-latency unauthenticated Byzantine consensus protocols is not necessary: by adding a simple fast-path wrapper to TetraBFT, Fast TetraBFT achieves the optimal good-case latency of 3 message delays while preserving bounded space and low communication complexity.

Abstract: Unauthenticated Byzantine consensus protocols achieve optimal failure resilience while relying only on authenticated point-to-point channels, not authenticated messages. They are an attractive building block for blockchains that do not mandate symmetric trust assumptions as well as for future post-quantum settings. We consider unauthenticated Byzantine consensus in partially synchronous networks and focus on optimizing its good-case latency - the worst-case time for correct processes to reach a decision under favorable conditions. A recently proposed ForgetIT protocol achieves an optimal good-case latency of 3 message delays but employs a highly complex design. We show that this complexity is unnecessary. To this end, we present Fast TetraBFT - an unauthenticated Byzantine consensus protocol that achieves optimal good-case latency by augmenting an existing TetraBFT protocol with a simple fast-path wrapper. Our solution lowers the good-case latency of TetraBFT from 5 to 3 message delays while preserving its bounded space requirements and low communication complexity.

</details>


### [92] [E2LLM: Towards Efficient LLM Serving in Heterogeneous Edge/Fog Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03770)
*Truong-Thanh Le,Amir Taherkordi,Hoang-Loc La,Frank Eliassen,Phuong Hoai Ha,Peiyuan Guan*

Main category: cs.DC

TL;DR: E2LLM deploys LLMs on limited Edge/Fog devices by grouping devices into specialized PREFILL/DECODER replicas and optimally partitioning models within each group, cutting waiting time by over 50% versus Splitwise at high load.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying LLMs in real-world Edge and Fog settings is difficult because devices are resource-constrained, so the standard assumption that a whole model fits on one device often fails. Practical deployment also requires balancing latency, cost, and resource utilization.

Method: The paper proposes E2LLM, which replicates the full LLM across multiple device groups, assigns each replica a specialized PREFILL or DECODER role based on token-processing efficiency, uses a Genetic Algorithm to cluster devices for performance, and applies Dynamic Programming within each cluster to find an optimal model partitioning strategy that minimizes bottlenecks.

Result: Experiments show that E2LLM adapts well to varying workloads, including highly variable input and output token lengths, and reduces average waiting time by more than 50% compared with the Splitwise baseline under high-demand conditions.

Conclusion: E2LLM shows that efficient LLM deployment in resource-constrained Edge/Fog environments is improved by combining replica-level role specialization with intra-replica model parallelism, substantially reducing waiting time under high demand.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have become integral to modern applications, yet their deployment remains challenging. Beyond executing the models themselves, practical deployment must address cost efficiency, low latency, and optimal resource utilization. Conventional approaches typically assume that an entire model can be hosted on a single device, which does not hold in many real-world scenarios, particularly in Edge and Fog environments where device resources are constrained. In this paper, we introduce E2LLM, a framework designed to enable efficient LLM deployment in such resource limited settings. Rather than simply partitioning a single model across all available devices, E2LLM replicates the full model across multiple groups of devices (replicas) and applies model parallelism within each replica. Each replica is assigned a specialized role PREFILL or DECODER based on its efficiency in handling input and output tokens. This separation leverages the inherent differences between these two phases of LLM inference. To effectively organize devices, we utilize a Genetic Algorithm to form clusters that maximize system performance. Within each cluster, we apply Dynamic Programming to determine an optimal partitioning strategy that minimizes bottlenecks in model-parallel execution. Experimental results demonstrate that our approach adapts robustly to varying workloads, including scenarios with significant variation in input and output token lengths. Compared to the Splitwise baseline, E2LLM reduces average waiting time by over 50% under high-demand conditions

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [93] [Human-in-the-Loop Contextual Bandits for Short-Term Rental Dynamic Pricing: Structural Equivalence of Historical Warm-Up and Approval-Gated Live Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02595)
*Oleg Miroshnichenko*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A human-gated contextual bandit for STR pricing can safely reuse historical deterministic-policy data to warm-start learning, cutting cold-start dramatically and suggesting that mandatory human oversight can improve, rather than hinder, deployment in regulated domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Short-term rental dynamic pricing is difficult for pure online learning because each pricing decision is financially consequential, operators need explainability and oversight, and feedback is sparse with only one booking outcome per night. These constraints make naive bandit cold-start periods operationally unacceptable.

Method: The paper proposes the Human-in-the-Loop Gated Bandit (HITL-GB) framework, where a contextual bandit recommends prices and a human decides to accept, modify, or reject them. It formalizes an approval-gated reward signal and derives a regularized ridge-regression warm-up procedure using historical deterministic-policy episodes to initialize Hierarchical Factored Thompson Sampling (HF-TS) agents.

Result: On real anonymized STR production data covering 1,461 nightly pricing episodes, the historical warm-up procedure reduced effective cold-start from about 150 episodes to about 30 episodes for HF-TS agents, making deployment more practical in sparse-feedback settings.

Conclusion: Human approval requirements in sparse-feedback, high-stakes markets can be leveraged as a statistical advantage: historical data collected under a deterministic policy can initialize a contextual bandit's posterior as if it were on-policy warm-up data, substantially reducing cold-start while preserving human control.

Abstract: Dynamic pricing in short-term rental (STR) markets presents a distinctive challenge for online learning algorithms: pricing decisions carry significant financial risk, operators require explainability, and market feedback is sparse (one booking outcome per listed night). We introduce the Human-in-the-Loop Gated Bandit (HITL-GB) framework, in which a contextual bandit algorithm generates price recommendations but a human agent retains authority to accept, modify, or reject each recommendation before it is applied. We show that under this approval constraint, historical pricing data -- collected under a prior deterministic policy -- is structurally equivalent to on-policy warm-up data for initialising the bandit's posterior, bypassing the weeks-to-months cold-start period that renders pure online bandit learning impractical in sparse-feedback markets. We formalise the approval-gated reward signal, derive a regularised ridge-regression warm-up procedure from historical episodes, and validate the approach on real STR production data (anonymised urban market, 2 rooms, April 2022 -- April 2026, 1,461 nightly pricing episodes). Our warm-up procedure compresses effective cold-start from ~150 episodes to ~30 episodes when initialising agents from the Hierarchical Factored Thompson Sampling (HF-TS) family. We further argue that the structural equivalence result is domain-agnostic: any high-stakes domain where human approval is legally or operationally required -- including clinical drug dosing, credit origination, content moderation, and radiological diagnosis -- satisfies the same conditions and benefits from the same warm-up strategy. In regulated industries, mandatory human oversight is thus a statistical asset rather than a deployment constraint.

</details>


### [94] [Scalable Uncertainty Quantification for Extreme Weather Forecasting via Empirical Neural Tangent Kernels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02886)
*Jose Marie Antonio Miñoza,Rex Gregor Laylo,Sebastian C. Ibañez*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A fast post-hoc uncertainty method for neural weather models works well if you avoid variance collapse with proper truncation and use ICA instead of SVD when extreme-weather features are heavy-tailed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning weather models are fast and accurate, but they produce deterministic forecasts without uncertainty estimates, which is a major limitation for decision-making during extreme weather events.

Method: The paper introduces Neural Tangent Kernel-based uncertainty quantification using last-layer empirical features, analyzes variance collapse via eigenvalue truncation relative to feature-space effective rank, compares ICA and SVD for feature decomposition, and proposes a data-driven rule based on eigenspectrum concentration ratio to choose between them.

Result: NTK-UQ achieves 31–37% sharper 90% prediction intervals than split conformal prediction, produces adaptive intervals that increase with extreme event severity, and the proposed selection rule correctly identifies the better decomposition method for all four tested architectures.

Conclusion: NTK-UQ can provide accurate, adaptive, and computationally cheap uncertainty estimates for deep learning weather models without retraining, but its success depends strongly on architecture-aware eigenvalue truncation and choosing the right feature decomposition method.

Abstract: Deep learning weather models now match numerical weather prediction accuracy while running orders of magnitude faster, but produce deterministic forecasts without uncertainty estimates, a critical gap for high-stakes decisions during extreme weather events. This paper proposes Neural Tangent Kernel-based uncertainty quantification (NTK-UQ) using last-layer empirical features. Theoretical analysis predicts that UQ quality is architecture-dependent through two mechanisms. First, a variance collapse mechanism explains when UQ fails: when the eigenvalue truncation rank approaches the effective rank of the feature space, the GP correction term consumes nearly all prior variance, destroying discrimination between tropical cyclones and routine conditions; architectures with concentrated spectra (spectral operators) require aggressive truncation ($k \leq 10$), while attention-based models tolerate full-rank computation. Second, decomposition performance depends on the non-Gaussian, heavy-tailed structure of extreme weather: Independent Component Analysis exploits higher-order statistics (kurtosis, negentropy) to isolate heavy-tailed extreme-event features, achieving higher discrimination than singular value decomposition, which captures only second-order variance. A data-driven selection rule chooses ICA or SVD from the feature eigenspectrum concentration ratio, correctly prescribing the superior decomposition for all four evaluated architectures. Compared to split conformal prediction (the natural post-hoc baseline), NTK-UQ achieves 31--37\% sharper prediction intervals at 90\% coverage, and uniquely produces \emph{adaptive} intervals that scale with extreme event severity, which conformal prediction cannot achieve by construction. The framework requires no retraining; inference-time uncertainty requires only a single matrix-vector product per sample.

</details>


### [95] [Spectral Asymptotics of Neural Network Loss Landscapes: An Exact Decomposition of the Curvature Exponent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02596)
*Anherutowa Calvo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A theory explains layer-wise differences in curvature scaling by how gradient directions align with layer geometry, predicts Hessian decay from independently measured gradient and curvature exponents, and yields a new optimizer that beats AdamW on some vision tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to explain why the curvature exponent α in the scaling law h_k ∝ σ_k^α differs systematically across neural network layer types, and to connect this variation to geometric structure in gradients and curvature rather than treating it as an unexplained empirical fact.

Method: The method derives a Spectral Alignment Decomposition, α = 2 + d log Φ_k / d log σ_k, where Φ_k quantifies alignment between Kronecker-factor eigenbases and gradient singular directions. It then proves a spectral transfer identity s = αγ, establishes a zeta-function participation-ratio bound, fits α and γ from independent empirical measurements, and uses the theory to design the preconditioner T(σ; α) implemented in the gradient singular basis.

Result: The results show that α is near 2 for convolutions, near 1 for transformer attention, and below 1 for MLP up-projections; that the identity s = αγ predicts Hessian spectral decay with about 2% median error across 93 layers, five architectures, and three datasets without free parameters; that curvature effectively concentrates in roughly one direction per layer; and that the proposed Spectral Newton optimizer outperforms AdamW on vision benchmarks where α ≈ 2.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that variation in the curvature exponent across neural layer types is explained by spectral alignment geometry via the Spectral Alignment Decomposition, and that this insight leads to an architecture-adaptive preconditioner (Spectral Newton) that can outperform AdamW when curvature behaves quadratically, especially in vision models.

Abstract: The curvature exponent $α$ in $h_k \propto σ_k^α$ -- governing how Hessian eigenvalues scale with gradient singular values -- varies systematically across layer types ($α\approx 2$ for convolutions, $\approx 1$ for transformer attention, $< 1$ for MLP up-projections). Why? We prove the Spectral Alignment Decomposition: $α= 2 + d\logΦ_k / d\logσ_k$, where $Φ_k$ measures alignment between Kronecker factor eigenbases and gradient singular directions. This reduces "why does $α$ vary?" to a geometric question we answer for LayerNorm, residual connections, and softmax heads. The decomposition implies a spectral transfer identity $s = αγ$ linking curvature exponent, effective gradient rank-decay $γ$, and Hessian decay exponent $s$. The identity is algebraic; its empirical content is that $α$ and $γ$, fit on independent data (HVPs vs. SVD), recover $s$ to ~2% median error across 93 layers, five architectures, and three datasets -- with no free parameters. A zeta-function bound on participation ratio shows curvature concentrates onto effectively one direction per layer. As a proof of concept, we derive the architecture-adaptive preconditioner $T(σ;α)$ and show that Spectral Newton -- implementing $T$ in the gradient singular basis -- outperforms AdamW on vision benchmarks where $α\approx 2$.

</details>


### [96] [Correcting Neural Operator Spectral Bias via Diffusion Posterior Sampling with Sparse Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03936)
*Niccolò Perrone,Fanny Lehmann,Stefania Fresca,Filippo Gatti*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Use a diffusion posterior sampler with frequency-aware guidance from a frozen neural operator so sparse sensors and fast surrogate predictions can be fused without inheriting the surrogate's high-frequency bias.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural operator surrogates are fast but systematically suppress high-frequency PDE solution content, while sparse sensors provide accurate local values but poor spatial coverage. The work aims to combine both sources without reintroducing surrogate spectral bias.

Method: The paper combines a frozen neural operator surrogate with an unconditional score-based diffusion prior and diffusion posterior sampling, introducing a closed-form frequency-shaped guidance score that weights surrogate information according to frequency-dependent accuracy without requiring denoiser backpropagation.

Result: On 3D elastic wavefield prediction with 5% and 2% sensor coverage, FreqNO-DPS attains near-zero spectral bias across all bands, outperforming both the neural operator alone and sensor-only DPS, while isotropic guidance improves pointwise accuracy but largely preserves the surrogate's high-frequency bias.

Conclusion: FreqNO-DPS effectively removes the neural operator's high-frequency spectral bias by using spectrally calibrated guidance inside diffusion posterior sampling, achieving near-zero spectral bias across frequency bands even with very sparse sensors.

Abstract: Neural operator surrogates (NO) approximate PDE solutions orders of magnitude faster than numerical solvers, but suffer from spectral bias: high-frequency content is systematically attenuated, limiting reliability where fine-scale structure matters. Sparse sensor measurements of the field are often available too, offering pointwise accuracy without spectral distortion but covering only a small fraction of the domain. We address this by treating NO predictions as auxiliary observations in a diffusion posterior sampling framework. Our method, FreqNO-DPS (https://github.com/niccoloperrone/FreqNO-DPS), combines an unconditional score-based diffusion prior, trained on high-fidelity simulations, with diffusion posterior sampling (DPS) conditioned on sparse observations and guided by a frozen neural operator. Naive integration reintroduces the surrogate's spectral bias; we resolve this with a closed-form, spectrally shaped guidance score that weights the surrogate by its frequency-dependent accuracy and needs no denoiser backpropagation. A distribution-free analysis bounds the approximation error across the frequency-diffusion-time plane and shows the guidance's frequency dependence is preserved regardless of distributional assumptions. On 3D elastic wavefield prediction at 5% and 2% sensor coverage, the method reaches near-zero spectral bias across all bands, where both the surrogate and sensor-only DPS show systematic high-frequency attenuation. Isotropic guidance, the natural baseline, improves pointwise accuracy but carries the bias into the posterior nearly intact, confirming that frequency-dependent calibration is essential, not merely beneficial. The framework needs only paired surrogate/reference data and exploits no problem-specific structure beyond the residual's approximate spectral diagonality, verifiable for new surrogates via the coherence diagnostic we provide.

</details>
