We then show that the standard Johnson-Lindenstrauss lemma greatly underestimates the packing efficiency of trained representations, and derive an adjusted capacity formula in which the number of near-orthogonal directions depends on the ratio of vectors to dimensions ($k/d$) rather than the raw count - a single modification that cuts prediction error by two orders of magnitude with no extra parameters. Combining these results, we define representational capacity as an upper bound on the number of distinguishable directions available for features and embeddings in a model's latent space. Capacity is exponentially sensitive to $\varepsilon$, and larger models favor tighter orthogonality constraints over maximizing raw capacity - a pattern compatible with several explanations (a stability-capacity trade-off, a ceiling on usable concepts, or confounds with model scale) that we leave to future work.

</details>


### [121] [QUIVER: Quantum-Informed Views for Enhanced Representations in Large ML Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02785)
*Aritra Bal,Michael Binder,Markus Klute,Benedikt Maier,Michael Spannowsky*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: QUIVER adds a quantum Fisher-based feature view from a trained variational quantum circuit to classical ML models, giving complementary geometric information and improving results on QM9 and JetClass.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is that multimodal inputs often improve large machine learning models when each modality offers complementary information. The authors argue that classical feature augmentation or scaling model capacity may miss intrinsic higher-order statistical structure, while a quantum Fisher view may expose this structure in a non-redundant way.

Method: The paper introduces QUIVER, which augments classical data-driven representations with a quantum Fisher information matrix-based view. A variational quantum circuit is trained on the same task, and the resulting basis-independent quantum Fisher view, summarizing higher-order correlations and the geometry of the learned quantum state manifold, is fused into existing model architectures through targeted architectural modifications.

Result: On two very different benchmarks, QM9 for molecular property prediction and JetClass for jet flavor classification, QUIVER improves standard evaluation metrics over baseline approaches. The results suggest that simulated quantum-geometric features can already offer measurable practical value before fault-tolerant quantum hardware is available.

Conclusion: QUIVER shows that quantum-geometric information, represented by the quantum Fisher view from a task-trained variational quantum circuit, can provide a genuinely complementary modality to classical features and improve machine learning performance on standard tasks even with simulated quantum circuits.

Abstract: Large machine learning models benefit substantially from multimodal inputs that provide a complementary view of the same example. We introduce QUIVER (QUantum-Informed Views for Enhanced Representations, a paradigm that enriches classical data-driven features with a quantum Fisher view: a geometrically motivated, basis-independent summary of higher-order correlations captured by a variational quantum circuit (VQC) trained to perform the same task. Unlike classical feature augmentation, the quantum Fisher information matrix encodes the intrinsic geometry of the learned quantum state manifold. While this feature map, motivated by quantum information theory, is ordinarily non-trivial to model classically, it can surface statistical structure that additional classical data or model capacity finds difficult to learn. This makes the quantum Fisher view a genuinely complementary modality rather than a redundant one. We demonstrate that QUIVER improves standard performance metrics on two benchmark datasets from very different fields: QM9 for predicting molecule properties, and JetClass for predicting jet flavor at the Large Hadron Collider (LHC). The core contribution, however, is domain-agnostic: the quantum Fisher view can be fused into a broad class of model architectures via targeted modifications to the base architecture, to incorporate information about the quantum geometry of the problem. These results demonstrate that quantum-geometric features, extracted from simulated variational circuits, can deliver measurable value for standard machine learning tasks, well before the advent of fault-tolerant quantum hardware.

</details>


### [122] [Qift: Shift-Friendly No-Zero W2 Post-Training Quantization for Rotated W2A4/KV4 LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02823)
*Chi-Wei Huang,Chia-Chi Tsai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper argues that standard 2-bit weight quantization fails partly because its fixed reconstruction levels are poorly chosen. After showing Hadamard rotation makes LLM weights close to a zero-centered Gaussian, it proposes Qift, a simple training-free no-zero 2-bit level set (roughly +/-1 and +/-3 after rescaling). This consistently beats the usual {-2,-1,0,+1} levels on LLaMA models under W2A4/KV4 inference and gets much closer to 3-bit performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard two-bit weights with level set {-2,-1,0,+1} often fail in aggressive W2A4/KV4 settings. The authors aim to show this is not only a bit-width issue but also a poor reconstruction-level design issue, and to find a simple deployment-friendly alternative.

Method: The paper analyzes the geometry of two-bit scalar quantization levels under Hadamard rotation, observes that pretrained weights are nearly zero-centered while rotation makes them more Gaussian-like, and then proposes Qift: a fixed no-zero W2 level set such as {+/-0.5, +/-1.5} (or reparameterized variants) with standard per-channel scaling. It also uses scale-invariant ratio analysis to identify an effective inner/outer centroid ratio range of 0.25 to 0.33.

Result: Across all 224 linear modules in LLaMA-2-7B and LLaMA-3.1-8B, no-zero level sets consistently outperform the standard W2 level set in pure W2A4 perplexity, mixed W2/W4 perplexity, downstream accuracy, and GPTQ residual behavior. At L=16 mixed precision, the proposed design significantly reduces the gap to W3A4 while preserving half the transformer layers at 2-bit precision.

Conclusion: A fixed no-zero two-bit weight level set is better suited than the standard {-2,-1,0,+1} for Hadamard-rotated W2A4/KV4 LLM inference, substantially improving perplexity, downstream accuracy, and narrowing the gap to W3A4 without adding training or learned codebooks.

Abstract: Two-bit weight quantization is attractive for memory-efficient LLM inference, but the standard W2 level set {-2,-1,0,+1} often collapses under aggressive W2A4/KV4 settings. We study the scalar level-set geometry of two-bit weights in a Hadamard-rotated quantization pipeline. Conventional asymmetric W2 substantially improves over the standard level set, indicating that W2A4 failure is not only a bit-width problem but also a reconstruction-level problem. Across all 224 linear modules in each of LLaMA-2-7B and LLaMA-3.1-8B, pretrained weights are already nearly zero-centered, while Hadamard rotation primarily Gaussianizes their standardized shape: excess kurtosis and Q-Q error drop by orders of magnitude. Based on this approximate zero-centered Gaussian-like source model, we propose Qift, a fixed no-zero W2 level set for rotated W2A4/KV4 inference. The main level set is {+/-0.5, +/-1.5}, equivalently {+/-1, +/-3} under a half-scale reparameterization; a power-of-two variant uses {+/-1, +/-4} for sign-and-shift decoded weight application. Qift redesigns the fixed two-bit code-to-level mapping and is training-free, learned-codebook-free, group-grid-free, and zero-point-free, retaining the standard per-channel scale. A scale-invariant ratio analysis identifies an effective inner/outer centroid ratio range of 0.25 to 0.33, explaining why mirror no-zero (MNZ), Lloyd, NF2, and PoT-MNZ perform well while {+/-1, +/-2} does not. On both models, the no-zero level sets consistently improve pure W2A4 perplexity, L-layer mixed W2/W4 perplexity, downstream accuracy, and GPTQ residual behavior over the standard W2 level set. At L=16 mixed precision, they substantially narrow the gap to W3A4 while keeping half of the transformer layers at two-bit precision, giving a simple, source-aware, and deployment-friendly alternative to more complex learned W2 codebooks.

</details>


### [123] [Mitigating Spurious Correlations with Memorization-Guided Dataset De-Biasing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02830)
*Arda Fazla,Abolfazl Hashemi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper shows that common sample selection scores are misled by spurious features, and introduces a two-stage scoring method that isolates core vs. spurious feature difficulty to pick better training samples; this lets plain ERM beat strong debiasing baselines with only a small data subset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing models often overfit spurious correlations that dominate training data, harming minority-group generalization. Prior subset selection or scoring methods also tend to rely on spurious features and usually need unknown group labels, making them ineffective in realistic settings.

Method: The paper proposes a two-stage sample scoring function that separates the learning dynamics of core causal features from spurious features, evaluates their difficulty independently, and then uses this metric to prioritize informative samples with and without spurious correlations.

Result: Extensive experiments show that training a standard ERM model on the selected subset achieves better performance than state-of-the-art debiasing approaches, despite using as little as 10% of the original training data.

Conclusion: Selecting a small but carefully scored subset of training data can substantially mitigate spurious correlation, allowing a standard ERM model to outperform existing debiasing methods while using only about 10% of the full dataset.

Abstract: Real-world datasets often contain spurious correlations that are not causally related to the target label. When such correlations dominate the majority of training samples, models tend to rely on them, leading to misclassification of minority samples that do not exhibit the same spurious patterns. While a potential approach is to select subsets of data to better represent the minority samples, this may require access to group labels, which are typically unknown. Furthermore, as we demonstrate, widely used sample scoring functions in the invariant subset or coreset selection literature largely depend on spurious features and therefore fail to accurately capture the importance or difficulty of core, causally relevant features. Accordingly, we propose to mitigate spurious correlations by developing a two-stage sample scoring function that disentangles the learning dynamics of core and spurious features and evaluates their difficulty separately. Based on our proposed metric, we introduce a new algorithm to find and prioritize informative samples both with and without spurious correlations. Extensive experiments demonstrate that a standard ERM model trained on our selected samples achieves superior performance compared to state-of-the-art debiasing techniques, while requiring as little as 10\% of the original training data.

</details>


### [124] [Learning Coherent Representations: A Topological Approach to Interpretability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02841)
*Sigurd Gaukstad,Melvin Vaupel,Valdemar Kargård Olsen,Erik Hermansen,Benjamin Dunn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduce coherence, a geometric interpretability constraint for neural representations, prove it preserves shared topology between data and features, and implement it with a differentiable training objective that improves interpretability on several datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is that standard deep neural representations are often hard to interpret because individual neurons respond to scattered, unrelated inputs; the authors seek a geometric constraint inspired by biological neural coding so that learned features correspond to contiguous, meaningful regions of state space.

Method: The authors define coherence for non-negative matrices as a bidirectional geometric clustering condition between samples and features, prove a bounded interleaving result between the Vietoris-Rips filtrations of the two spaces, and introduce Coh, a differentiable Fréchet-variance-based objective to enforce this property during training in models such as autoencoders and token embeddings.

Result: The paper shows theoretically that coherent matrices induce compatible topological structure between samples and features, and empirically demonstrates on synthetic data, rotated MNIST, and BERT token embeddings that the Coh objective produces more interpretable feature activations and feature spaces.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that enforcing coherence in learned representations yields features that are geometrically contiguous and jointly topologically aligned with the sample space, improving interpretability beyond what sparsity alone provides.

Abstract: Deep neural networks learn representations where individual features often lack interpretable meaning; a single neuron may activate for scattered, unrelated inputs. We introduce coherence, a geometric property inspired by neural coding in the brain, where neurons like grid cells and head direction cells respond to contiguous regions of state space. A non-negative matrix is coherent if each row (sample) attends to geometrically clustered columns (features) and vice versa, and in addition every sample is well described by some feature and every feature is needed by some sample. We prove that coherent matrices induce a bounded interleaving between the Vietoris-Rips filtrations of samples and features, guaranteeing that both spaces share compatible topological structure. This geometric constraint facilitates interpretability. For example, if data lies on a circle, coherent features must tile that circle into contiguous arcs. We introduce Coh, a differentiable objective function based on Fréchet variance that enforces coherence during training. Unlike sparsity, which bounds how many samples a feature activates on, coherence bounds which samples, requiring geometric connectivity rather than only rarity. This yields not just interpretable features but an interpretable feature space. We validate Coh in an auto-encoder using synthetic and rotated MNIST datasets and in a token embedding of BERT using language data.

</details>


### [125] [Spectral-Progressive Thought Flow for Lightweight Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02842)
*Yixian Shen,Zhiheng Yang,Qi Bi,Changshuo Wang,Shuai Wang,Jia-Hong Huang,George Floros,Prayag Tiwari,Anuj Pathania*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SpecFlow is a lightweight multimodal spatial reasoning framework that compresses visual intermediate states into a fixed DCT space and progressively refines detail under text guidance, achieving strong reasoning accuracy with much lower compute and memory cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal spatial reasoning methods depend on long chains of textual and visual intermediate thoughts, causing heavy computation and memory overhead due to accumulating visual tokens and dense cross-modal attention.

Method: The paper proposes Spectral-Progressive Thought Flow (SpecFlow), which encodes intermediate visual thoughts in a fixed-size discrete cosine transform space, progressively adds higher-frequency details when needed, and uses classifier-free guidance so autoregressive textual thoughts steer flow-based updates of the visual state without enlarging the context.

Result: Empirically, SpecFlow attains competitive or superior multimodal spatial reasoning performance while lowering computation and KV cache costs by as much as 2.1 times, with latency and memory remaining independent of reasoning depth.

Conclusion: SpecFlow enables long-horizon multimodal spatial reasoning with stable latency and memory usage by keeping a bounded visual workspace in fixed-size discrete cosine space, while achieving competitive or better performance and reducing computation and KV-cache cost by up to 2.1x.

Abstract: Multimodal spatial reasoning often relies on long chains of intermediate textual and visual thoughts, where accumulating visual tokens and dense cross-modal attention incur substantial computation and memory overhead. To address this challenge, we propose Spectral-Progressive Thought Flow (SpecFlow), a novel lightweight multimodal spatial reasoning framework that represents intermediate visual thoughts in a fixed-size discrete cosine space. By exploiting strong energy compaction, SpecFlow preserves global layout and relational structure while introducing high-frequency details only when increased spatial precision is required. To align visual state evolution with linguistic intent, classifier-free guidance enables autoregressive textual thoughts to steer flow-based updates of the visual workspace/state without expanding the context. As a result, SpecFlow maintains a bounded visual workspace whose updates depend only on the current visual state and accumulated textual trace, enabling long-horizon inference with stable latency and memory usage independent of reasoning depth. Empirical results show that SpecFlow achieves competitive or superior reasoning performance while reducing computation and KV cache costs by up to 2.1 times.

</details>


### [126] [A Systematic Evaluation of Current Architectures in Wind Power Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02849)
*Vinicius Bortolini,Gilson Adamczuk Oliveira,Erick Oliveira Rodrigues,Matheus Henrique Dal Molin Ribeiro*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A review of wind interval forecasting papers shows that hybrid models using decomposition plus deep learning usually work better, but the field still lacks standard evaluation and stronger real-world validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is that wind resources are inherently uncertain, and interval forecasting is more suitable than point forecasting for supporting efficient wind power integration and operational decision-making in power systems.

Method: This paper uses a systematic literature review methodology and applies Latent Dirichlet Allocation (LDA) topic modeling to guide paper selection, identify themes, and analyze research trends in hybrid interval forecasting approaches for wind energy.

Result: The main findings are that decomposition-based hybrid models are dominant, dual-model strategies for lower and upper bounds are commonly used, interval quality is assessed by balancing coverage and width, and major open issues include non-standardized metrics, computational burden, and insufficient real-world validation.

Conclusion: The review concludes that hybrid interval forecasting methods, especially those combining decomposition techniques such as VMD/EEMD with deep learning or statistical models, generally improve wind speed or wind generation interval forecast reliability and accuracy by producing narrower intervals while maintaining adequate coverage.

Abstract: Interval wind speed forecasting is essential for the efficient integration of wind energy into power systems, as it accounts for the inherent uncertainty of wind resources. This study presents a systematic literature review focused on hybrid approaches to interval forecasting of wind generation, exploring the combination of deep learning, modal decomposition, and statistical methods. To guide the paper selection, Latent Dirichlet Allocation (LDA) was applied for topic modeling, enabling the identification of patterns and research trends. The findings emphasize that integrating hybrid models with decomposition techniques-such as Variational Mode Decomposition (VMD) and Ensemble Empirical Mode Decomposition (EEMD)-enhances forecast accuracy and reliability by narrowing prediction intervals without compromising coverage. Regarding interval construction, most studies adopt a dual-model strategy, independently forecasting the lower and upper bounds. Input data are commonly decomposed using techniques like EMD, EEMD, or VMD, which extract frequency-based components. These components serve as inputs to models such as LSTM or ELM, trained separately for each bound. This approach allows for targeted modeling of uncertainty, improving flexibility and precision, Interval quality is typically evaluated through metrics that balance coverage and interval width. The review also highlights challenges, including the lack of standardized evaluation metrics, computational complexity, and limited real-world validation. Overall, the study reinforces the value of interval forecasting for wind energy operations and offers insights for advancing model robustness and decision-making.

</details>


### [127] [RESCAST-100K: A Comprehensive Dataset for Cross-Domain Residential Load and Indoor Temperature Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02852)
*Jainam Dhruva,Yousaf Raza,A. B. Siddique,Simone Silvestri*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces RESCAST-100K, a 100K-home residential forecasting benchmark for load and indoor temperature prediction across controlled domain shifts, and shows that cross-attention and MLP-mixer models generalize best across domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Residential forecasting suffers from data heterogeneity and scarcity, while existing datasets are too narrow and do not support structured cross-domain transfer, domain adaptation, or zero-shot evaluation. A broader benchmark is needed to study generalization under controlled domain shifts.

Method: The paper builds RESCAST-100K from about 100,000 EnergyPlus-simulated U.S. homes derived from ResStock, with 15-minute multivariate time series for total load, HVAC load, and indoor temperature, plus weather, setpoints, and 40+ static covariates. It defines source-target domains along interpretable axes such as geography, climate zone, wall construction, and heating equipment, integrates five real-world datasets under a unified schema, and benchmarks recurrent, attention-based, and MLP-mixer models on zero-shot, missing-input, and sim-to-real forecasting settings.

Result: RESCAST-100K supports systematic transfer-learning and domain-adaptation experiments across simulated and real residential datasets. In the reported benchmarks, cross-attention and MLP-mixer architectures consistently outperform recurrent models and classical transformers in zero-shot cross-domain settings and under missing-input conditions.

Conclusion: RESCAST-100K is a large-scale, configuration-driven benchmark that enables rigorous cross-domain evaluation for residential short-term forecasting, and results show cross-attention and MLP-mixer models are stronger than recurrent and classical transformer baselines under domain shift.

Abstract: Accurate short-term forecasting of residential energy load and indoor temperature is essential for home energy management systems, grid-level demand response, and community energy efficiency efforts. Domain adaptation and transfer learning have shown promise for improving forecasting accuracy under data heterogeneity and scarcity commonly seen in residential settings. However, progress is limited by the lack of comprehensive residential datasets: existing benchmarks are narrow in target coverage and rarely support structured cross-domain evaluation. We introduce RESCAST-100K, a large-scale residential forecasting benchmark for studying cross-domain generalization. It provides a configuration-driven interface that instantiates source and target domains along interpretable axes, including geography, climate zone, wall construction, and heating equipment, enabling systematic evaluation of transfer learning, domain adaptation, and zero-shot generalization under controlled domain shifts. The benchmark covers approximately 100,000 EnergyPlus-simulated U.S. homes derived from ResStock, with 15-minute time series for three coupled targets per home: total load, HVAC load, and indoor temperature. These are paired with weather channels, HVAC setpoints, and over 40 static building covariates. RESCAST-100K also integrates five real-world residential datasets under a unified schema, supporting sim-to-real evaluation on the same tasks. We benchmark recurrent, attention-based, and MLP-mixer architectures for zero-shot performance across domains, missing-input conditions, and forecasting tasks. Cross-attention and MLP-mixer models consistently outperform recurrent and classical transformer baselines under domain shift. RESCAST-100K is intended to aid the machine learning and building analytics communities advance cross-domain residential forecasting at home, community, and grid scale.

</details>


### [128] [GRZO: Group-Relative Zeroth-Order Optimization for Large Language Model Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02857)
*Liyan Tan,Yequan Zhao,Yifan Yang,Ruijie Zhang,Xinling Yu,Zheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GRZO makes zeroth-order LLM fine-tuning more practical by using per-example perturbations plus group-relative normalization to cut variance, improve convergence, save memory, and outperform MeZO and its variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing zeroth-order optimization is attractive for memory-efficient fine-tuning of large language models, but its practical adoption is constrained by the high variance of gradient estimation, which hurts optimization quality and performance.

Method: The paper proposes Group-Relative Zeroth-Order (GRZO): for each mini-batch example, it samples one pseudo-independent perturbation and computes per-example losses, then applies group-relative normalization to aggregate them. This increases the effective number of gradient directions from 1 to the batch size without extra forward-pass cost, while keeping memory usage at inference level.

Result: The authors prove GRZO is directionally unbiased and that its variance decreases proportionally with batch size, giving a tighter nonconvex convergence bound than MeZO. Empirically, across RoBERTa-large, Llama3-8B, and OPT-13B on multiple tasks, GRZO improves average accuracy on Llama3-8B by +3.0 over MeZO with 23% lower peak GPU memory, and boosts sparse, low-rank, and quantized ZO variants by +6.0 on average when used as a drop-in replacement.

Conclusion: GRZO is an effective drop-in zeroth-order optimizer for LLM fine-tuning that preserves inference-level memory while reducing gradient-estimation variance, improving both theoretical convergence and practical downstream accuracy over MeZO and related ZO variants.

Abstract: Zeroth-order (ZO) optimization is a memory-efficient alternative to backpropagation for fine-tuning large language models, but its deployment is limited by the high variance of gradient estimation. We propose GRZO, a Group-Relative Zeroth-Order optimizer that draws one pseudo-independent perturbation per mini-batch example and aggregates the per-example losses through group-relative normalization, raising the effective gradient-direction count from one to the batch size at no additional forward cost while preserving inference-level memory. We prove that GRZO is directionally unbiased with variance shrinking proportionally to the batch size, yielding a tighter nonconvex convergence bound than MeZO. Across RoBERTa-large, Llama3-8B, and OPT-13B over multiple tasks, GRZO improves average accuracy on Llama3-8B by $+3.0$ over MeZO at $23\%$ lower peak GPU memory; as a drop-in replacement for the MeZO core, it lifts sparse, low-rank, and quantized ZO variants by $+6.0$ on average.

</details>


### [129] [Forgetting is Not Erasure: Recovering Latent Knowledge via Transport Keys](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02860)
*Archie Chaudhury*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Forgetting in continual learning is often partly an interface-alignment problem rather than true representational erasure; simple transport keys plus model stitching can recover much of old-task performance after sequential training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work questions the strong view that catastrophic forgetting mainly reflects permanent loss of task-relevant representations after sequential training, and investigates whether performance drops instead arise from mismatched internal interfaces.

Method: The paper proposes a stitched evaluation protocol that combines early layers from the post-update model with late layers from the pre-update model, optionally linked by a compact task-specific transport key. Transport keys are compact interface-alignment operators estimated from a small set of paired anchor activations and assessed via model stitching.

Result: In controlled continual-learning experiments on Split CIFAR-100, transport keys recover most of the original Task A accuracy after training on Task B in a ResNet-style model, and a similar recovery trend is observed in a compact vision transformer.

Conclusion: Continual learning forgetting is not solely caused by erasing earlier task representations; a substantial part comes from interface drift between internal network stages, and restoring alignment can recover prior-task performance.

Abstract: Catastrophic forgetting is often framed as a representational problem: after sequential training, a model appears to lose the features that supported performance on earlier tasks. We challenge the stronger form of this view. Across controlled continual-learning settings, we find that a significant portion of apparent forgetting can be attributed to interface drift between internal stages rather than permanent erasure of task-relevant computation. We study this phenomenon through a stitched evaluation protocol that combines early computation from a post-update network with late computation from its predecessor, optionally mediated by a compact, task-specific transport key. We describe transport keys at a systems level as compact interface-alignment operators estimated from a small set of paired anchor activations and evaluated through model stitching. On split CIFAR-100 with a ResNet-style network, transport keys recover most of the original Task A performance after sequential training on Task B. On a compact vision transformer, we observe a similar recovery pattern. These results suggest that continual learning may require better mechanisms for indexing and re-accessing latent computations, not only methods that prevent weight change.

</details>


### [130] [RRISE: Robust Radius Inference via a Surrogate Estimator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02876)
*Jong-Ik Park,Shreyas Chaudhari,Carlee Joe-Wong,José M. F. Moura*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RRISE accelerates randomized smoothing certification by learning a surrogate model plus conformal calibration, achieving near-Monte-Carlo certified accuracy with one forward pass instead of thousands of noisy evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard randomized smoothing provides architecture-agnostic L2 robustness certificates, but its heavy per-input Monte Carlo sampling cost makes it impractical for real-time or repeated-deployment systems. The authors aim to remove this deployment-time bottleneck by sharing information across the deployment stream.

Method: The paper proposes RRISE, which trains a learned surrogate on precomputed Monte Carlo class-count soft targets using soft-label cross-entropy, then applies a one-time conformal calibration procedure to convert surrogate outputs into conservative certified radii that are verifiable at deployment.

Result: Across image classification benchmarks, RRISE stays within 0.84 percentage points of fixed-budget Monte Carlo certified accuracy while replacing up to 10^4 noisy base-model evaluations per query with one surrogate pass. It recovers training cost after about 10^5 queries and outperforms the prior offline-surrogate method by 1.23 to 1.91x certified accuracy on CIFAR-100 and Tiny ImageNet.

Conclusion: RRISE makes randomized smoothing practical for repeated deployment by replacing per-input Monte Carlo certification with a single surrogate forward pass while preserving provably conservative certified radii after conformal calibration.

Abstract: Randomized smoothing (RS) uses a smoothed classifier to provide architecture-agnostic certificates of $\ell_2$ classification robustness, but its dependence on per-input Monte Carlo (MC) sampling undermines its use in real-time systems. We argue that this cost is structural rather than fundamental, such that it can be significantly reduced by sharing information across the deployment stream. We introduce RRISE, an RS framework that compresses certification into a single forward pass through a learned surrogate. RRISE trains the surrogate against precomputed MC class-count targets via a soft-label cross-entropy loss and converts surrogate predictions into provably conservative certified radii through a one-time conformal calibration step. The resulting certificate is deployment-verifiable: whenever the calibrated radius is positive, the surrogate's prediction provably matches the smoothed classifier's and the smoothed classifier is constant on a ball of that radius around the input. Across image classification benchmarks, RRISE matches fixed-budget MC certified accuracy within $0.84$ percentage points while replacing up to $10^4$ noisy base-model evaluations per query with a single surrogate forward pass, recouping MC training cost after $\approx 10^5$ deployment queries. On CIFAR-100 and Tiny ImageNet, where the only prior offline-surrogate method collapses, RRISE achieves $1.23$ to $1.91\times$ higher certified accuracy, establishing efficient randomized smoothing as a practical path to certified robustness in repeated-deployment settings.

</details>


### [131] [Are we really tilting? The mechanics of reward guidance in flow and diffusion models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02884)
*Sanjit Dandapanthula,Nicholas M. Boffi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Reward hacking in reward-guided diffusion is largely caused by a standard approximation of the Doob h-function. The paper pinpoints two specific errors, fixes one with a cheap damping schedule, and explains why best-of-n sampling helps with the other.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although reward guidance is effective for steering generative models at inference time, it often causes reward hacking, where reward is optimized at the expense of fidelity to the learned data distribution. The paper aims to identify the fundamental source of this problem rather than attributing it only to neural reward complexity or diffusion training artifacts.

Method: The paper studies reward-guided diffusion in analytically tractable settings, especially Gaussian and Gaussian mixture targets with quadratic rewards. It derives the effect of finite-particle plug-in estimation of the Doob h-function in closed form, identifies distinct failure modes, proposes a closed-form reward damping schedule, and validates the theory on synthetic data and FLUX.1 text-to-image generation.

Result: In closed form, the authors show two failure modes of the plug-in estimator: over-optimization within each mode and failure to choose high-reward modes. Their damping schedule corrects the within-mode bias without extra computation, and experiments on Gaussian mixtures, a 2D checkerboard, and FLUX.1 show that these theoretical insights transfer to practical settings.

Conclusion: Reward hacking in reward-guided diffusion fundamentally arises from the common finite-particle plug-in approximation to the Doob h-function, not only from complex reward models or training biases. A closed-form reward damping schedule reduces within-mode reward hacking at no extra compute, while best-of-n sampling mainly helps with high-reward mode selection.

Abstract: Reward guidance algorithms steer a learned generative process toward the reward-tilted measure at inference time. While empirically powerful, these methods are prone to reward hacking: the guided model over-optimizes the reward at the cost of fidelity to the learned distribution. Prior work has attributed this to the complexity of neural reward functions or implicit biases in diffusion training, but its fundamental origins remain poorly understood. We show that reward hacking arises from an approximation made in most practical implementations of reward-guided diffusion -- finite-particle plug-in estimation of the Doob h-function -- even in the simplest non-trivial settings of Gaussian and Gaussian mixture targets with quadratic rewards. In closed form, we isolate two distinct failure modes of the plug-in estimator: it leads to reward hacking within each mode and it cannot select high-reward modes. We propose a closed-form reward damping schedule that corrects the within-mode bias with no additional compute, and clarify the role of best-of-n sampling in compensating for the mode selection failure. Experiments on Gaussian mixture targets, a 2D checkerboard, and FLUX.1 text-to-image generation confirm that our theoretical insights carry over to practical settings.

</details>


### [132] [A Nonmonotone Gradient-Based Algorithm for Symmetric Nonnegative Matrix Factorization and Graph Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02887)
*Ryan Swart,Johannes Brust*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 作者提出SNMPBB系列算法，将非单调投影BB方法用于Symmetric NMF，并扩展到图聚类和大规模低秩近似；理论上保证全局收敛，实验上在速度、残差和聚类精度上整体优于现有基线。 / The authors propose the SNMPBB family, applying a nonmonotone projected BB method to Symmetric NMF and extending it to graph clustering and large-scale low-rank approximation; the methods have global convergence guarantees and empirically outperform strong baselines in speed, residual quality, and clustering accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 现有Symmetric NMF的投影梯度方法通常被认为收敛缓慢，限制了梯度算法的实用性。作者旨在打破这一认知，设计更快、更可扩展且适用于图聚类和大规模问题的算法。 / Existing projected gradient methods for Symmetric NMF were widely regarded as slow, limiting the perceived usefulness of gradient algorithms. The authors aim to overturn this view by designing faster, more scalable methods suitable for graph clustering and large-scale problems.

Method: 论文提出将非单调投影Barzilai-Borwein方法引入对称非负矩阵分解，形成SNMPBB；进一步结合图拉普拉斯正则化得到Graph-SNMPBB，并结合随机低秩近似得到LAI-SNMPBB；同时证明这些方法收敛到一阶驻点，并证明BB曲率信息在随机近似下仍被保留。 / The paper adapts the nonmonotone projected Barzilai-Borwein method to Symmetric NMF as SNMPBB; it further incorporates graph Laplacian regularization for Graph-SNMPBB and randomized low-rank approximation for LAI-SNMPBB. The authors prove convergence to first-order stationary points and show that BB curvature information is preserved under randomized approximations.

Result: 在合成数据上，SNMPBB在获得相近残差时比SymANLS快约6倍，且秩越高优势越明显；在6个真实聚类基准上，Graph-SNMPBB的准确率与SymANLS持平或更优；在34个SuiteSparse矩阵上，LAI-SNMPBB在运行时间和残差质量上均优于LAI-SymPGNCG。 / On synthetic data, SNMPBB is about 6x faster than SymANLS for similar residuals, with larger gains at higher ranks. On six real-world clustering benchmarks, Graph-SNMPBB matches or exceeds the accuracy of SymANLS. On 34 SuiteSparse matrices, LAI-SNMPBB outperforms LAI-SymPGNCG in both runtime and residual quality.

Conclusion: SNMPBB及其扩展（Graph-SNMPBB、LAI-SNMPBB）为Symmetric NMF提供了高效且具有全局收敛保证的梯度型求解框架，并在聚类与大规模低秩近似场景中优于或不逊于现有方法。 / SNMPBB and its extensions (Graph-SNMPBB, LAI-SNMPBB) provide an efficient gradient-based framework for Symmetric NMF with global convergence guarantees, outperforming or matching existing methods in clustering and large-scale low-rank settings.

Abstract: Symmetric nonnegative matrix factorization (Symmetric NMF) approximates a matrix as $WW^T$ with nonnegative rectangular factor $W$. It has broad applications in graph clustering and machine learning. In contrast to the NMF, projected gradient methods for the symmetric problem had been associated with slow convergence. To address this, we introduce SNMPBB, the first adaptation of nonmonotone projected Barzilai-Borwein methods to Symmetric NMF, demonstrating that gradient algorithms are significantly more effective than previously understood. We further extend SNMPBB to graph clustering using the graph Laplacian regularization (Graph-SNMPBB) and to large problems with low-rank approximations (LAI-SNMPBB). For all variants we prove global convergence to first-order stationary points and also that Barzilai-Borwein curvature information is preserved with randomized approximations. On synthetic data, SNMPBB achieves 6 times speedup over the alternative SymANLS for similar residuals, with advantages growing at higher ranks. Across six real-world clustering benchmarks, Graph-SNMPBB matches or exceeds SymANLS accuracy. Lastly, LAI-SNMPBB outperforms state-of-the-art LAI-SymPGNCG on 34 SuiteSparse matrices in both runtime and residual quality.

</details>


### [133] [Multi-Modal Machine Learning for Breast Cancer Recurrence Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02892)
*Jiahao Shao,Xudong Wang,Anam Nawaz Khan,Christopher Brett,Xueping Li,Bing Yao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Use structured data plus extracted pathology and note information to predict breast cancer recurrence; this multi-modal approach performs better than using any single data source alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional recurrence prediction models that rely only on structured or unstructured data miss important clinical context, limiting timely and accurate risk assessment for follow-up care and treatment planning.

Method: The study combines structured EHR data with information extracted from free-text pathology reports and clinician notes using regular expressions and a precedence-based conflict reconciliation strategy, then evaluates single-modal and multi-modal feature sets across multiple machine learning models.

Result: Multi-modal models consistently achieve better predictive accuracy than single-modal models, and the enriched feature set adds value beyond commonly used baseline features from prior breast cancer studies.

Conclusion: Integrating multi-modal clinical data with rule-based extraction and conflict reconciliation improves breast cancer recurrence prediction over single-source approaches.

Abstract: Breast cancer recurrence, a leading cause of long-term mortality among survivors, requires timely and accurate risk assessment to guide follow-up care and treatment planning. Traditional predictive models, often limited to either structured or unstructured data alone, struggle to capture the full clinical context. This study examines the impact of integrating multi-modal clinical data, including treatment records, pathology reports, and clinician notes, on recurrence prediction. By integrating a rule-based regular expression extraction mechanism with a rigorous precedence-based conflict reconciliation strategy, our approach effectively recovers definitive tumor characteristics from free-text pathology narratives to augment structured records. We also benchmark performance against commonly used feature sets from prior breast cancer studies to assess the added value of multi-modal integration. Single-source and multi-modal inputs are evaluated across a range of machine learning models. Results show that multi-modal integration consistently improves predictive accuracy compared to single-modal methods.

</details>


### [134] [Fast Unlearning at Scale via Margin Self-Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02920)
*Federico Di Gennaro,Alexander Shevchenko,Fanny Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MASC is a cheaper LM unlearning method that monitors token-level logit margins to decide when to stop, avoiding costly checkpoint evaluation while keeping strong forget/retain performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing unlearning methods waste computation by training with a fixed budget even after the desired forget-retain balance has been reached, and by selecting checkpoints through repeated downstream evaluation and storage. The authors aim to reduce both inefficiencies while preserving model utility and avoiding retraining.

Method: The paper proposes MArgin Self-Correction (MASC). For each sequence to forget, it actively shrinks the logit margin between the original next token and the strongest alternative tokens. It stops online when this margin becomes sufficiently small on average over enough token positions across all forget examples, producing the final unlearned model without extra validation-based checkpoint selection.

Result: On TOFU, MUSE News, and MUSE Books, MASC achieves competitive forgetting-retention trade-offs at a fraction of the compute cost of prior baselines. The study also finds that increasing model size improves the trade-off for both MASC and SimNPO: forgetting metrics stay similar while retention utility increases.

Conclusion: MASC provides an efficient language-model unlearning approach with an online stopping rule that avoids downstream checkpoint evaluation, achieving competitive forget-retain trade-offs at much lower computational cost; larger models further improve utility retention while maintaining similar forgetting performance.

Abstract: Language-model unlearning updates a trained model to behave as if it had not seen selected training examples, while preserving utility and avoiding costly retraining. Existing approaches typically fine-tune the pretrained model with a fixed training budget and select the final model afterwards by evaluating several saved checkpoints on downstream validation data. Two sources of unnecessary computation limit scalability: training beyond the desired forget-retain trade-off, and checkpoint selection that requires extra storage and repeated evaluations. To address these limitations, we introduce MArgin Self-Correction (MASC), an efficient unlearning method with an online stopping rule that does not require downstream evaluation. Given a text sequence to be forgotten, MASC actively reduces the logit gap between the original next token and the most likely alternatives. It outputs a final model once this gap is small on average over a sufficiently large proportion of token positions across all forget sequences. On TOFU, MUSE News, and MUSE Books, MASC achieves a competitive forget-retain trade-off at a fraction of the computational cost of existing baselines. We further observe that as we increase model size (a.k.a. number of parameters), the trade-offs improve for both MASC and SimNPO -- the forget metrics remain comparable while retain utility increases.

</details>


<div id='physics.geo-ph'></div>

# physics.geo-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [135] [GeoVolDiff: Taming 3D Geological Volumes with Latent Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03572)
*Qi Pang,Hongling Chen,Jinghuai Gao*

Main category: physics.geo-ph

TL;DR: GeoVolDiff uses physics-based simulations plus a latent diffusion model to generate realistic 3D geological volumes, and those synthetic volumes are good enough to train seismic impedance inversion models that perform competitively even on real field data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior geophysical deep learning work focuses heavily on model architecture and loss design, but a core bottleneck is the lack of abundant, representative, high-quality labeled training data. In geophysics, field data are expensive and usually lack ground-truth labels, making data scarcity much more severe than in domains like computer vision.

Method: The paper proposes a three-stage generative pipeline: first, build a base corpus using physics-based forward simulations; second, train a Latent Diffusion Model to learn the distribution of 3D geological structures in latent space; third, sample diverse synthetic geological volumes from the trained model and use them to pre-train downstream inversion networks.

Result: Synthetic data generated by GeoVolDiff improve downstream usability: inversion networks trained exclusively on generated samples achieve competitive results on both synthetic benchmarks and field datasets, demonstrating that generative geological volumes can substitute for expensive field-acquired labels to a meaningful extent.

Conclusion: GeoVolDiff can generate large-scale, geologically plausible 3D volumes that are useful as surrogate labeled data; models pre-trained only on these synthesized data achieve competitive seismic impedance inversion performance on both synthetic and real field data, even without extra physical or geological priors.

Abstract: Deep learning has become a prevailing paradigm across a wide range of geophysical applications. Yet most existing studies concentrate on methodological refinements -- novel network architectures, physics-informed constraints, or taskspecific loss functions -- while paying comparatively little attention to a more fundamental challenge of any data-driven approach: the availability and representativeness of high-quality training data. This limitation is especially pronounced in geophysics. Unlike computer vision, which benefits from large-scale, well-curated benchmarks such as ImageNet, comparably abundant and reliably labelled geophysical data are prohibitively expensive to acquire and, in most field settings, lack accessible ground-truth supervision. To alleviate this data deficiency, we propose GeoVolDiff, a generative framework for three-dimensional geological volumes. It comprises three coupled stages: (i) constructing a foundational training corpus through physics-based forward simulation; (ii) training a Latent Diffusion Model (LDM) to capture the statistical distribution of 3D geological structures; and (iii) synthesizing diverse, structurally plausible volumes at scale for downstream geophysical tasks. We examine the utility of the synthesized data on a representative downstream task, seismic impedance inversion. Without incorporating any additional physical or geological prior, inversion networks pre-trained exclusively on synthesized data attain competitive performance on both synthetic and field datasets, indicating that data synthesised by the generative model can serve as an effective surrogate for costly field-acquired labels.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [136] [Visual Graph Scaffolds for Structural Reasoning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02673)
*Runlin Lei,Xiaokui Xiao,Zhewei Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that graphs help LLMs most when used visually to structure reasoning, not merely as text-converted or external knowledge aids.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work mostly uses graphs as external knowledge at test time. The paper is motivated by the idea that graphs may also help organize reasoning internally, similar to how humans use mind maps for branching and converging thoughts.

Method: The paper studies multi-hop QA by rewriting teacher-provided reasoning traces into graph-structured mind maps and using them to guide a student model. It compares visual graph guidance against text-flattened graph guidance, including settings without direct answer hints, and evaluates effects after supervised fine-tuning and KL-based distillation.

Result: A clear modality gap is observed: when graph structures are flattened into text and direct answer hints are removed, reasoning efficiency and answer quality drop substantially. Visual graph guidance remains effective without direct answer clues and retains its advantage after supervised fine-tuning and KL-based distillation.

Conclusion: Graphs are valuable for LLMs not only as external knowledge sources but also as visual reasoning scaffolds; visual graph guidance remains effective under abstract guidance and after fine-tuning/distillation, while flattened text graphs lose much of their benefit.

Abstract: Graphs have been used to enhance large language models (LLMs) for structured reasoning, mostly as external knowledge sources are provided to models at test time. In this paper, we take a different view: the value of graphs for LLMs lie not only in supplying information, but also in organizing reasoning. Inspired by how humans use graph-structured mind maps to organize branching and converging thoughts, we ask whether graphs can serve as an internal form of reasoning assistance. We study this question on multi-hop question answering tasks, where teacher-provided reasoning traces are rewritten as graph mind maps and used to guide a student model. Our experiments reveal a clear modality gap. When graph structures are flattened into text, their benefits become limited once direct answer hints are removed. Under this abstract guidance setting, both reasoning efficiency and answer quality degrade substantially. In contrast, visual graph guidance remains effective without direct answer clues, and its advantage persists after supervised fine-tuning and KL-based distillation. The above findings support the claim that graphs should be studied not only as external knowledge structures for LLMs, but also as visual scaffolds for organizing reasoning.

</details>


### [137] [AURA: Action-Gated Memory for Robot Policies at Constant VRAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02775)
*Josef Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AURA-Mem replaces growing KV-cache with a tiny recurrent memory that writes only when an observation matters for the next action, achieving constant memory and far fewer writes without hurting robotic policy performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard KV-cache works well for datacenter inference with many short, resettable requests, but is poorly matched to robots and embodied agents that run one long, non-resetting episode on bandwidth-limited edge hardware where memory bandwidth, storage capacity, and write endurance are critical constraints.

Method: The paper wraps a frozen vision-language-action backbone with a fixed-size recurrent memory and a learned write gate. The gate is trained using a closed-loop action-error/action-surprise signal so memory is updated only when the current observation is expected to change the next action. The method is evaluated on a synthetic benchmark and on OpenVLA-OFT 7B in LIBERO-Long.

Result: AURA-Mem keeps inference state fixed at 4,224 bytes independent of horizon, whereas KV-cache grows dramatically with time. On the synthetic benchmark it matches the best O(1) baseline accuracy while using 5.19-6.13x fewer writes, up to 9.19x fewer in easier settings. On LIBERO-Long with OpenVLA-OFT 7B, it matches the ungated base policy success rate (0.233) and slightly exceeds an always-write KV arm (0.217), while using 7.0x fewer writes.

Conclusion: AURA-Mem is a constant-size recurrent memory with an action-utility gate that preserves task success while drastically reducing memory writes versus KV-cache-style always-write memory, making it better suited for long-horizon embodied inference on edge hardware.

Abstract: The KV-cache is the right memory for datacenters but the wrong memory for robots. Datacenter inference batches many short requests and resets them, amortizing an attention cache across a crowd. Embodied agents instead run one long, non-resetting episode on bandwidth-limited edge hardware, where high-bandwidth memory and flash are scarce, flash has finite write endurance, and memory writes rather than compute can become the binding constraint.
  AURA-Mem (Action-Utility Recurrent Adaptive Memory) targets this regime. It wraps a frozen vision-language-action backbone with a constant-size recurrent memory and a learned gate that writes only when the current observation would change the next action: memory that knows when to stay silent. Unlike reconstruction-based memory, the gate is trained directly against a closed-loop action-error signal. Its inference state is fixed at 4,224 bytes regardless of horizon, while a KV-cache grows to 6,061 times larger at 100,000 steps.
  On a controlled synthetic benchmark, AURA-Mem matches the best O(1) baseline in accuracy while using 5.19-6.13 times fewer writes, and up to 9.19 times fewer writes on easier configurations. Budget-matched random and periodic schedules do not recover this gain, isolating the benefit to the action-surprise signal. On a trained closed-loop OpenVLA-OFT 7B panel on LIBERO-Long (n=60 episodes per arm), the gate does not hurt success: AURA-Mem matches the ungated base policy (0.233) and slightly exceeds an always-write KV arm (0.217), while using 7.0 times fewer writes and constant memory. We also instantiate an approximate-information-state value-loss bound as a methodology demonstration; at this scale, the bound is vacuous rather than a guarantee.

</details>


### [138] [Evaluating Transformer and LSTM Frameworks for Prediction in Ungauged Basins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02791)
*Taye Akinrele,James Halgren,Noorbakhsh Amiri Golilarz,Sudip Mittal,Shahram Rahimi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Using NOAA NWM retrospective data, the paper tests whether an encoder-only Transformer beats an LSTM for upstream streamflow inference in ungauged basins. It does not: the LSTM performs better in both tested settings, and adding downstream information greatly boosts accuracy for all models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ungauged basins lack direct observations, which increases uncertainty in hydrologic prediction and limits anticipation of extreme events. Because watershed networks have convergent topologies that integrate upstream processes, the paper investigates whether Transformer-style sequence modeling offers an architectural advantage over LSTM for inferring upstream streamflow under sparse information.

Method: The study compares an encoder-only Transformer and an LSTM for upstream streamflow inference using retrospective simulations from the NOAA National Water Model (NWM). Experiments are conducted under two input configurations: upstream-only and combined upstream-plus-downstream information.

Result: Across both upstream-only and combined configurations, the LSTM achieves stronger overall performance than the encoder-only Transformer. Including downstream information improves all models, raising median NNSE by more than 60%, showing that downstream context is a strong auxiliary constraint.

Conclusion: The encoder-only Transformer does not outperform the LSTM for upstream streamflow inference in ungauged basins under limited hydrologic information. Recurrent memory appears better suited to this reconstruction task, while adding downstream hydrologic context substantially improves prediction skill for all architectures.

Abstract: Watershed networks exhibit convergent topologies in which multiple tributaries merge into downstream channels,integrating diverse upstream hydrological processes. In ungauged basins, the absence of direct observations increases uncertainty and limits the ability to anticipate extreme events. This study evaluates whether an encoder-only Transformer provides an advantage over an LSTM for upstream streamflow inference under limited hydrologic information, using retrospective simulations from the NOAA National Water Model (NWM). Across both upstream-only and combined configurations, the LSTM showed stronger overall performance than the Transformer model across the two configurations. Incorporating downstream information further boosted performance for all models, increasing median NNSE by more than 60%. Rather than treating this as a leaderboard-style comparison, we interpret the experiments as a test of architectural inductive bias for hydrologic sequence inference. The results indicate that recurrent memory remains better aligned with this upstream reconstruction task than an encoder-only Transformer, while downstream hydrologic context provides a strong auxiliary constraint that substantially improves prediction skill across architectures

</details>


### [139] [BehaviorBench: Modeling Real-World User Decisions from Behavioral Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02798)
*Liangwei Yang,Jielin Qiu,Zixiang Chen,Ming Zhu,Juntao Tan,Zhiwei Liu,Wenting Zhao,Zhujun Lan,Akshara Prabhakar,Silvio Savarese,Huan Wang,Shelby Heinecke*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BehaviorBench is a new benchmark built from real prediction-market and on-chain user behavior to test personalized decision models. It shows personalization helps predict users’ beliefs more than their exact trades, and benchmark design choices strongly affect model performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation data for personalized decision-support systems are limited, and many existing benchmarks depend on simulated or model-generated users, which may systematically differ from real human behavior. The paper aims to provide a benchmark grounded in real-world behavioral evidence.

Method: The paper constructs BehaviorBench from public prediction-market and on-chain records by reconstructing wallet-level decision histories. It defines two task layers: Belief prediction and Trade prediction. The authors evaluate frontier and open-weight generative models under four history interfaces: no personalization, direct recent history, generated user profiles, and retrieved support-wallet evidence.

Result: Across 2,000 evaluation wallets, BehaviorBench includes 141,445 Belief instances and 1,485,972 Trade instances with disjoint support pools for retrieval-based evaluation. Experiments show that personalization improves Belief prediction more consistently than Trade prediction, model rankings differ by task and metric, and different history interfaces reveal different failure modes.

Conclusion: BehaviorBench offers a real-world benchmark for evaluating personalized decision modeling from behavioral traces, showing that personalization helps belief prediction more reliably than trade prediction and that evaluation outcomes vary across task layers, metrics, and history interfaces.

Abstract: Many decision-support settings require systems that adapt to individual users, but evaluation data for this problem remain limited. Existing benchmarks for user understanding often rely on simulated users or model-generated behavior, even though recent work cautions that model-based simulations can diverge systematically from human behavior. We introduce \textsc{BehaviorBench}, a benchmark for evaluating personalized decision modeling from real-world behavioral traces. \textsc{BehaviorBench} reconstructs wallet-level decision histories from observed public prediction-market and on-chain records, and organizes them into two complementary task layers: \emph{Belief prediction}, which predicts a user's final revealed stance and confidence in a market, and \emph{Trade prediction}, which predicts the direction and amount of individual transactions. Across 2,000 evaluation wallets, the benchmark contains 141,445 Belief instances and 1,485,972 Trade instances, with disjoint support pools for retrieval-based evaluation. We evaluate frontier and open-weight generative models under four history interfaces: no personalization, direct recent history, generated user profiles, and retrieved support-wallet evidence. Personalization improves Belief prediction more consistently than Trade prediction, model rankings change across task layers and metrics, and different history interfaces expose different failure modes. \textsc{BehaviorBench} provides an evaluation setting for studying whether personalized methods can use real-world behavioral evidence rather than simulated users alone.

</details>


### [140] [ChatHealthAI: Aligning Electronic Health Record Representations with Large Language Models for Grounded Clinical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02802)
*Bo-Hong Wang,Baicheng Peng,Ruilin Wang,Jun Bai,Ziyang Song,Yue Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ChatHealthAI connects an EHR foundation model to a frozen LLM using a task-aware resampler, enabling interpretable clinical reasoning from structured longitudinal EHR data without sacrificing much predictive performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are strong at natural-language reasoning for clinical decision support but weak at modeling structured longitudinal EHR data, while EHR foundation models capture patient trajectories well but lack interpretable language-based reasoning. The work aims to bridge these complementary limitations.

Method: The paper proposes ChatHealthAI, a multimodal reasoning framework that uses a task-aware resampler to align patient representations from a pretrained EHR foundation model with the semantic space of a frozen LLM. It integrates longitudinal structured EHR features with refined clinical event descriptions to support natural-language reasoning and prediction.

Result: On three clinical prediction tasks from the EHRSHOT benchmark, ChatHealthAI improves reasoning quality and interpretability and preserves competitive predictive accuracy compared with existing approaches.

Conclusion: ChatHealthAI demonstrates that aligning structured longitudinal EHR representations from a pretrained EHR foundation model with a frozen LLM can improve clinical reasoning quality and interpretability while maintaining competitive predictive performance on benchmark clinical tasks.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) exhibit strong natural-language reasoning abilities for clinical decision support, but struggle to effectively model structured longitudinal electronic health records (EHRs). In contrast, EHR foundation models can learn predictive patient representations, yet lack interpretable language-based reasoning. To bridge this gap, we propose ChatHealthAI, a multimodal reasoning framework that aligns structured EHR representations from a pretrained EHR foundation model with the semantic space of a frozen LLM through a task-aware resampler. By integrating longitudinal patient representations with refined clinical event descriptions, ChatHealthAI enables clinically grounded natural-language reasoning while maintaining accurate patient prediction. We evaluated ChatHealthAI on three clinical predictive tasks from the EHRSHOT benchmark. Results show that ChatHealthAI improves reasoning quality and interpretability while preserving competitive predictive performance. These findings highlight the potential of integrating EHR foundation models with pretrained LLMs for interpretable clinical prediction.

</details>


### [141] [Traj-Evolve: A Self-Evolving Multi-Agent System for Patient Trajectory Modeling in Lung Cancer Early Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02812)
*Sihang Zeng,Matthew Thompson,Ruth Etzioni,Meliha Yetisgen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Traj-Evolve teaches a multi-agent LLM system to predict from long EHR timelines by both remembering similar past patients and improving agent coordination through reinforcement learning, leading to better lung cancer risk prediction than prior methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based multi-agent methods can handle long EHR contexts, but they treat each patient independently and do not exploit accumulated experience from similar past cases the way clinicians do. Longitudinal EHR modeling also remains difficult because the data are sparse, noisy, multimodal, and span long time horizons.

Method: The paper proposes Traj-Evolve, a self-evolving multi-agent system with two mechanisms: (1) an Experience Pool that stores rejection-sampled reasoning traces and retrieves similar prior patients as few-shot context, and (2) multi-agent reinforcement learning using reward-ranked fine-tuning to optimize collaboration among agents and between agents and memory. A leave-one-out cross-retrieval strategy aligns retrieval-augmented training and inference.

Result: On a lung cancer prediction task using up to five years of multimodal EHR data, Traj-Evolve outperforms 9 strong baselines on both the full cohort and the difficult never-smoker subgroup. The analysis shows that a larger Experience Pool makes more specific retrieval preferable, MARL rapidly improves the manager agent while worker agents keep gaining in temporal reasoning from more verified patients, and the two mechanisms provide complementary gains: ExPool improves specificity and MARL improves sensitivity.

Conclusion: Traj-Evolve improves longitudinal EHR patient trajectory modeling by combining retrieval-based experiential memory with parametric multi-agent reinforcement learning, achieving stronger lung cancer risk prediction than multiple baselines across both overall and never-smoker populations.

Abstract: Modeling patient trajectories from longitudinal electronic health records (EHRs) requires reasoning over sparse, noisy, and long-context multimodal sequences. Existing LLM-based multi-agent systems address context length but process patients in isolation, failing to mirror how clinicians leverage accumulated experience from similar prior cases. We present Traj-Evolve, a self-evolving multi-agent system with two complementary evolving mechanisms. First, an Experience Pool (ExPool) acts as a non-parametric memory, indexing rejection-sampled reasoning traces to retrieve similar patients as few-shot contexts. Second, multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) via reward-ranked fine-tuning parametrically optimizes inter-agent and agent-memory collaboration. A leave-one-out cross-retrieval strategy unifies the two, aligning training- and inference-time behavior under retrieval augmentation. On a lung cancer prediction task utilizing up to five years of multimodal EHRs, Traj-Evolve outperforms 9 strong baselines on the overall population and a challenging never-smoker population. Analysis of the evolving dynamics highlights three key findings: (1) expanding the ExPool shifts optimal retrieval from diverse to specific samples; (2) under MARL, the manager agent's prediction loss converges quickly while the worker agents' temporal reasoning continues to benefit from more verified patients; and (3) the two mechanisms are complementary on the predicted risk, where ExPool improves specificity while MARL improves sensitivity.

</details>


### [142] [An Exploration of Collision-based Enemy Morphology Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02832)
*Johor Jara Gonzalez,Matthew Guzdial*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies automatic generation of video-game enemy body shapes from player collision data, proposing three methods that all match or outperform a robotics-inspired evolutionary baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enemy morphology generation is underexplored in procedural content generation for games, even though enemy body plans and collision structures strongly affect gameplay and related morphology research exists in robotics.

Method: The paper proposes and compares three new approaches for procedural generation of enemy morphologies in video games, using player collision information as the driving signal, and evaluates them against an evolutionary baseline adapted from robotics morphology generation.

Result: All three proposed approaches demonstrate different strengths and weaknesses, but each performs at least as well as, and in some cases better than, the adapted evolutionary baseline.

Conclusion: Three novel player-collision-driven methods for generating enemy morphologies each show distinct tradeoffs, and all achieve performance comparable to or better than an adapted evolutionary robotics baseline.

Abstract: Despite a great deal of prior research into Procedural Content Generation (PCG), relatively little prior work has explored generating enemies for video games. In particular, there is almost no work on generating enemy morphologies, the basic body plan or collision information for in-game enemies, despite the existence of related morphology generation work in robotics. In this paper, we explore three different novel approaches to generate enemy morphologies based on player collision information. We found that each approach provides different strengths and weaknesses, but all had equivalent or better performance than an evolutionary baseline adapted from prior robotics morphology work.

</details>


### [143] [Thinking Past the Answer: Evaluating Harmful Overthinking in Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02835)
*Simone Caldarella,Davide Talon,Rahaf Aljundi,Elisa Ricci,Massimiliano Mancini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that for reasoning models, more thinking is not always better: once the answer is already correct, continued reasoning often hurts rather than helps. Using a new prefix-level evaluation protocol, the authors find that stopping at the first correct point can significantly improve accuracy and reveal harmful overthinking as an important reliability problem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although LRMs often improve through longer test-time reasoning, it is unclear whether more reasoning remains beneficial after the model has already found the correct answer. Recent observations of overthinking motivate a finer-grained study of whether continued reasoning refines or corrupts already-correct solutions.

Method: The paper introduces a prefix-level trajectory evaluation protocol based on reasoning sufficiency. It identifies the minimum reasoning budget at which a model first reaches the correct answer, then compares continued reasoning beyond that point to distinguish verbose overthinking from harmful overthinking. The analysis is conducted on multimodal benchmarks and validated on language-only reasoning benchmarks, with additional study of efficiency methods such as early stopping.

Result: Many supposedly reasoning-heavy cases require much less reasoning than expected. Stopping at the first correct prefix improves accuracy by up to 21% compared with standard full reasoning. Early stopping cuts verbose overthinking by up to 50% but does not solve harmful overthinking. Error analysis shows correctness loss is mainly caused by logical drift and visual reinterpretation, and the phenomenon generalizes beyond multimodal tasks to language-only benchmarks.

Conclusion: Current Large Reasoning Models are limited not only by reasoning ability but by failing to stop once the correct answer is reached; extra reasoning can destabilize correct trajectories, making harmful overthinking a general reliability risk across multimodal and language-only tasks.

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) improve performance by generating explicit intermediate reasoning traces through increased test-time compute, yet the assumption that longer reasoning is consistently beneficial remains under-examined. While recent evidence shows that additional reasoning can lead models to overthink, we ask: "Once a model has reached the correct answer, does further reasoning refine the solution, or deviate from it?" To study the dynamics after correctness, we introduce a prefix-level trajectory evaluation protocol grounded in reasoning sufficiency, defining the minimum reasoning budget required for a model to first generate the correct answer. This allows us to disentangle verbose overthinking, where additional reasoning is redundant but harmless, from harmful overthinking, where continued reasoning destabilizes an already-correct trajectory. Starting from multimodal benchmarks, we find that many instances considered reasoning-intensive require surprisingly little reasoning. Moreover, stopping at the first correct prefix improves accuracy over standard reasoning up to 21%, revealing that current models are limited not only by their ability to reason, but also by their inability to stop at the right time. Furthermore, while common efficiency strategies like early stopping substantially reduce verbose overthinking (up to 50%), they fail to mitigate harmful overthinking. Failure analysis reveals that correctness deviations are mainly driven by logical drift and visual reinterpretation. Finally, we show that our findings generalize to language-only reasoning benchmarks, highlighting harmful overthinking as a broader reliability risk. Code available at https://simonecaldarella.github.io/thinking-past-the-answer.

</details>


### [144] [Toward a Modular Architecture for Embedded AI Agent Systems at the Edge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02862)
*Marcus Rüb,Michael Gerhards*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A reference architecture for embedded AI agents: keep fast, privacy-critical intelligence on-device, offload complex reasoning to the cloud, and enforce safety and observability through a governance layer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agentic AI frameworks assume server-class hardware or constant connectivity, which does not fit embedded microcontrollers with strict memory and energy limits in pervasive computing environments.

Method: The paper proposes a modular reference architecture for Embedded Agent Systems with a tiered design: on-device agents use compressed neural networks and rule-based logic, while cloud-augmented agents use SLMs for higher-level reasoning; it then analyzes architectural trade-offs instead of focusing on benchmarks.

Result: The result is a conceptual architecture that bridges deterministic real-time control and agentic intelligence, highlighting how tiered deployment and a governance layer can support safe, observable, and reliable autonomous device fleets under resource constraints.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that embedded autonomous agents are feasible when intelligence is split across on-device and cloud-augmented tiers, with governance built in as a system-wide concern to balance latency, energy, privacy, and reliability.

Abstract: The rise of Large Language Models (LLMs) has enabled agentic AI capable of complex reasoning and tool use; however, deploying such autonomy in pervasive computing environments remains challenging due to the strict memory and energy constraints of embedded microcontrollers. Existing frameworks typically assume server-class resources or continuous connectivity, leaving a gap for deeply embedded systems. This paper proposes a modular reference architecture for Embedded Agent Systems that bridges the divide between deterministic real-time control and agentic intelligence.
  We introduce a tiered design that decouples On-Device Agents - executing highly compressed neural networks and rule-based logic for low-latency, privacy-critical tasks - from Cloud-Augmented Agents that leverage Small Language Models (SLMs) for higher-level reasoning and planning. A key contribution is the integration of a cross-cutting Governance Layer, ensuring observability, policy enforcement, and safety across distributed fleets of autonomous devices. Rather than presenting purely empirical benchmarks, we analyze architectural design principles and trade-offs regarding latency, energy, and reliable execution in resource-constrained environments.

</details>


### [145] [Don't Gamble, GAMBLe: An Analytical Framework for AI-Driven Research Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02863)
*Marquita Ellis,Paul Castro*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces GAMBLe, a framework for understanding how LLM-based research systems behave. It shows that ADRS performance depends on how generators, evaluators, and search methods interact, not on any single best model, and that smart component selection yields large gains under tight budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
