Motivation: ADRS are increasingly used to discover algorithms, proofs, and designs, but their internal behavior is expensive to explore and poorly understood. Existing analytical tools and convergence guarantees are insufficient because they rely on structural assumptions that fail under realistic ADRS processes.

Method: The paper formalizes ADRS and introduces GAMBLe, which decomposes system behavior into four parameters: generator (G), assessor (A), discovery mechanism (M), and budget (B), plus the effective landscape Leff = A o G. The framework is then evaluated through more than 760 replicated runs and over 46,000 iterations across multiple generators, search mechanisms, and three NP-hard benchmark problems.

Result: Experiments show there is no total ordering among generators or discovery mechanisms: frontier LLMs can lose to open-source models, and simple greedy search can outperform advanced meta-search. With only 60 iterations per run, appropriate component choices improve final performance by 13 to 67 percent and search efficiency by 6 to 39 times.

Conclusion: GAMBLe provides a practical framework for analyzing AI-Driven Research Systems by modeling them through generator, assessor, mechanism, budget, and an effective landscape. Standard convergence assumptions do not reliably describe ADRS, and system performance is highly dependent on component interactions rather than any universally best model or search method.

Abstract: AI-Driven Research Systems (ADRS) -- systems coupling LLMs with automated evaluation to discover algorithms, proofs, and designs -- are being optimized and adopted across domains, but the tools to analyze them have not kept pace. ADRS performance depends on component interactions that are poorly understood, expensive to explore, and (as we show) not well captured by standard convergence guarantees. These guarantees rely on structural assumptions that do not hold under the ADRS process we formalize. We introduce GAMBLe, a framework that decomposes ADRS behavior into four parameters (generator $G$, assessor $\mathcal{A}$, discovery mechanism $\mathcal{M}$, budget $B$) and one compositional object, the effective landscape $L_{\text{eff}} = \mathcal{A} \circ G$, which reveals that distinct generator-assessor pairs induce structurally different per-problem optimization landscapes. We exercise the framework on 760+ replicated runs (>46,000 iterations) spanning generators from single LLMs to dynamically-adaptive ensembles, mechanisms from greedy selection to co-evolutionary meta-search, and three NP-hard problems whose assessors range from continuous scoring to cliff functions. The experiments reveal no total ordering of generators or mechanisms: frontier models can underperform open-source alternatives and the simplest mechanism sometimes outperforms state-of-the-art meta-search. Results show that even under limited budgets (60 iterations per run), the right component choices can improve performance by 13-67% and search efficiency by 6-39x.

</details>


### [146] [When Helping Hurts and How to Fix It: Multi-Agent Debate for Data Cleaning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02866)
*Chirag Parmar,Akshat Mehta,Henglin Wu,Jagadish Ramamurthy,Shweta Medhekar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TL;DR: Multi-agent debate is useful for finding errors, but usually harmful for generating cleaned outputs unless the Critic can reliably verify mistakes and avoid overturning correct answers. The key is adversarial separation plus grounded evidence; without that, critique often confuses the Generator and reduces quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation: Prior work often assumes that multi-agent debate improves LLM-based data cleaning, but its effects appear inconsistent across tasks. The paper aims to explain when debate helps and when it hurts, especially distinguishing between generation tasks, where critique may corrupt correct answers, and detection tasks, where critique may expose errors. The goal is to move from anecdotal success claims to a predictive condition that explains debate effectiveness.

Method: Method: The paper evaluates multi-agent debate across three benchmarks, four model families, and more than 6,000 task-condition pairs. It combines broad empirical comparison with a derived theoretical condition for debate benefit, then validates that condition through a factorial experiment. The factorial study compares self-verification versus a separately instantiated Critic, and tests mechanisms such as code-execution grounding and evidence-gated generation. The authors also assess external validity by checking whether the proposed condition predicts outcomes in 19 published comparisons spanning seven domains.

Result: Result: Debate decreases generation performance for all four model families by 1.6 to 15.5 percentage points due to critique-induced confusion, but improves error detection by 27.4 F1 points with a large effect size (d=1.0). The proposed benefit condition correctly predicts all nine task types studied and generalizes to 19 published comparisons in seven domains with zero false positives. A specially designed debate setup with a separate grounded Critic and evidence-gated Generator is the first to significantly outperform a single-agent baseline on a generative task by 5.3 percentage points (p<0.05).

Conclusion: Conclusion: Multi-agent debate is not uniformly beneficial for data cleaning. It harms generative correction tasks across all tested model families because critique-induced confusion can cause a Generator to accept hallucinated or misleading Critic feedback, but it substantially improves error detection. Debate is beneficial only when the expected probability of rescuing incorrect outputs exceeds the probability of damaging correct outputs. Adversarial role separation with grounded verification and evidence-gated generation is necessary for debate to outperform a single agent on generative tasks.

Abstract: When does multi-agent debate help data cleaning, and when does it hurt? Across three benchmarks, four model families, and over 6,000 task-condition pairs, we find debate's effect reverses sign: it degrades generation across all four models (-1.6 to -15.5pp) through critique-induced confusion (CIC), hallucinated Critic feedback that the Generator accepts uncritically, yet improves error detection (+27.4pp F1, d=1.0). We derive a debate benefit condition: debate helps when the probability of rescuing a wrong output (Critic verification odds weighted by fixability) exceeds the probability of destroying a correct one. A factorial experiment proves adversarial separation is essential: self-verification with identical tools fails, while a separate Critic with code-execution grounding and evidence-gated generation produces the first debate configuration to significantly exceed single-agent on a generative task (+5.3pp, p<0.05). The condition correctly predicts all nine task types and generalizes with zero false positives across 19 published comparisons in seven domains.

</details>


### [147] [Handoff Debt: The Rediscovery Cost When Coding Agents Take Over Interrupted Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02875)
*Dipesh KC,Anjila Budathoki*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how much time and token cost a new coding agent wastes when taking over another agent's unfinished work. A controlled handoff benchmark shows that giving successors traces or well-structured notes makes takeover much cheaper, even if final solve rates improve only modestly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing coding-agent benchmarks assume a single uninterrupted agent solves a repository issue, but real software work involves interruption, reassignment, review, and resumption from partial states. The paper addresses the missing evaluation dimension of 'handoff debt,' the rediscovery cost caused by opaque or incomplete predecessor work.

Method: The paper introduces a takeover protocol that interrupts a coding agent at deterministic handoff points, freezes the repository, and then evaluates successor agents under four handoff conditions: repository state only, raw trace, summary notes, and structured notes. The study uses 75 source tasks, producing 181 handoff-point tasks and 724 takeover runs per successor model across three successor models.

Result: Across three successor models, context-bearing handoffs reduce median agent events by 20–59% and cumulative prompt tokens by 42–63% compared with repository-only takeover. Improvements in solved rate are smaller and depend on the model, but efficiency gains are consistent.

Conclusion: Coding-agent evaluation should measure not only whether a task is eventually solved, but also the resumption cost imposed on a successor agent; better handoff context consistently lowers takeover cost.

Abstract: Coding-agent benchmarks evaluate whether a single uninterrupted agent can resolve a repository issue. Real software work is messier: tasks are interrupted, reassigned, reviewed, and resumed from partial states left by another agent or engineer. We study this missing dimension through \emph{handoff debt}: the rediscovery cost imposed when a predecessor's work is opaque or incomplete. Our takeover protocol interrupts a coding agent at deterministic handoff points, freezes the repository, and evaluates successor agents under four handoff views: repository state only, raw trace, summary notes, and structured notes. Across 75 source tasks, the protocol generates 181 handoff-point tasks and 724 takeover runs per successor model. Across three successor models, context-bearing handoffs reduce median agent events by 20--59\% and cumulative prompt tokens by 42--63\% relative to repository-only takeover. Solved-rate effects are smaller and model-dependent, but efficiency gains are consistent. These findings suggest that coding-agent evaluation should report not only whether a task is solved, but also how costly that work is for another agent to resume.

</details>


### [148] [Large AI Models in Dental Healthcare: From General-Purpose Systems to Domain-Specific Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02914)
*Sema Helali,Lina Abu Nadab,Sausan Alqawas,Alaa Abd-Alrazaq,Faleh Tamimi,Rafat Damseh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This review of 97 dentistry AI studies shows that LLMs are strong for text tasks, vision foundation models are strong for image tasks, and dental-specific multimodal models perform best overall; the most effective systems combine these models, but clinical deployment is still limited by hallucinations, scarce annotated data, and missing evaluation standards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although oral diseases affect nearly 3.5 billion people globally, the comparative clinical value of emerging large-scale AI models in dentistry remains unclear. Prior work lacked a unified review spanning language-generative models, discriminative vision foundation models, and dental-specific foundation models, as well as their interrelationships and shared limitations.

Method: The paper is a PRISMA-ScR-guided scoping review. The authors systematically searched PubMed, Google Scholar, Scopus, and arXiv, used two independent reviewers for screening, included 97 studies published from 2020 to 2026, and proposed a two-dimensional framework classifying models by architectural paradigm and degree of dental specialization.

Result: Language-generative models perform well on text-centric tasks such as clinical reasoning, exam performance, and patient communication, but are inconsistent for image-reliant diagnosis. Adapted SAM and CLIP variants show strong results in segmentation and lesion detection. Dental-specific models such as DentVFM, DentVLM, and OralGPT perform best on complex multimodal tasks. Combined pipelines consistently outperform single-model systems, and pretraining resources are heavily skewed toward dental vision data because large-scale dental text corpora are scarce.

Conclusion: General-purpose and dental-specific foundation models in dentistry are complementary rather than mutually exclusive; the best-performing clinical systems combine them in structured multimodal pipelines. However, safe autonomous deployment still depends on reducing hallucinations, expanding annotated dental datasets, and establishing standardized clinical benchmarks.

Abstract: Background: Oral diseases affect nearly 3.5 billion people worldwide, yet the comparative clinical potential of large-scale AI models in dentistry remains poorly understood. Three distinct model categories have emerged: language-generative models, discriminative vision foundation models, and dental-specific foundation models, with no unified review examining their relationships and collective limitations.
  Methods: Following PRISMA-ScR guidelines, we systematically searched four databases (PubMed, Google Scholar, Scopus, arXiv), screened independently by two reviewers. After applying inclusion/exclusion criteria, 97 studies (2020-2026) were included. We propose a two-dimensional classification framework organizing models by architectural paradigm and dental specialization degree.
  Results: Language-generative models excel at text-based tasks (clinical reasoning, licensing exams, patient communication) but show inconsistent performance on image-dependent diagnostics. Adapted SAM and CLIP variants achieve strong tooth segmentation and lesion detection results. Dental-specific models (DentVFM, DentVLM, OralGPT) demonstrate strongest performance on complex multimodal tasks. Integrated pipelines consistently outperform single-model approaches. A data asymmetry is observed: dental-specific pretraining concentrates almost entirely in the vision domain, reflecting scarce large-scale dental text corpora.
  Conclusions: General-purpose and dental-specific models play complementary roles; the most effective systems combine both within structured pipelines. Safe autonomous deployment requires resolving three persistent barriers: hallucination in generative models, limited annotated dental datasets, and absent standardized clinical evaluation benchmarks.

</details>


### [149] [What Benchmarks Don't Measure: The Case for Evaluating Abstention Competence in Autonomous Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02965)
*Victor Ojewale,Suresh Venkatasubramanian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: 本文批评现有智能体基准只奖励“做事”而不评估“该不该做”，这会诱导智能体在信息不足、无法验证或未经授权时仍继续行动，形成“合规偏置”。作者提出三种应当弃权的缺口类型，以及一套面向弃权的评测指标，并用 144 个企业场景的初步实验说明：通过运行时弃权机制，可以较高比例阻止危险行为，同时保持较好的任务可用性。 / TL;DR: The paper argues that existing agent benchmarks reward acting but not deciding whether action is appropriate, which induces a compliance bias. It proposes a three-gap taxonomy for when agents should abstain and introduces abstention-aware metrics. Preliminary experiments on 144 enterprise scenarios show that a runtime abstention mechanism can block many hazardous actions while retaining strong usability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 动机在于：传统基准只关注任务是否完成，却没有评估智能体在缺少必要信息、证据或授权时是否本应暂停或拒绝执行。这会系统性鼓励“继续行动”这一默认行为，从而掩盖潜在不安全决策。作者希望建立一套能够评估“合理弃权”的基准与指标。 / The motivation is that standard benchmarks focus on task completion and fail to evaluate whether an agent should have paused or refused when required information, evidence, or authorization is missing. This systematically rewards proceeding by default and masks unsafe behavior. The paper aims to build benchmark concepts and metrics that can properly evaluate principled abstention.

Method: 方法上，作者先从机制层面分析合规偏置的来源，指出其来自人类反馈训练中的奖励黑客，并被主流基准的评分与架构进一步固化。随后提出三类应当弃权的情境：规范缺口、验证缺口与权限缺口；在此基础上设计弃权评测协议与复合指标，包括 Safety Rate、Usability Rate 和 Informed Refusal Rate，并在 144 个企业级场景、5 个模型家族上评估一种运行时强制弃权机制。 / Methodologically, the paper first analyzes the origin of compliance bias, arguing that it emerges from reward hacking in human-feedback pipelines and is reinforced by benchmark designs. It then proposes a three-gap taxonomy of abstention-warranted situations: specification gaps, verification gaps, and authority gaps. Based on this, it defines abstention-aware protocols and metrics, including Safety Rate, Usability Rate, and Informed Refusal Rate, and evaluates a runtime-enforced abstention mechanism across 144 enterprise scenarios and five model families.

Result: 初步结果显示，所提出的运行时强制弃权机制在实验中可实现最高 89.2% 的危险操作拦截率，并在已授权场景中达到 87.5% 的可用性。结果还表明，不同模型家族的安全-可用性权衡曲线差异明显。 / Preliminary results show that the runtime-enforced abstention mechanism can block up to 89.2% of hazardous actions while preserving 87.5% usability on authorized scenarios. The experiments also indicate that the safety-usability tradeoff differs substantially across model families.

Conclusion: 本文认为现有自主智能体基准普遍忽视“何时不应继续执行”的问题，导致由人类反馈训练出来的智能体形成“合规偏置”。作者提出弃权场景三分法与一组弃权评测指标，并通过初步实验说明安全性与可用性的权衡并非固定不变，而是可调且因模型家族而异。 / The paper concludes that current autonomous-agent benchmarks systematically ignore when an agent should refrain from acting, producing a compliance bias in agents trained with human-feedback objectives. It introduces a three-gap taxonomy and abstention-aware evaluation metrics, and presents preliminary evidence that the safety-usability tradeoff is tunable rather than intrinsic and differs across model families.

Abstract: Benchmarks for autonomous agents measure whether agents complete tasks, yet this framing is systematically blind to whether an agent should have proceeded at all. Agents trained under human-feedback objectives develop a structural tendency to proceed even when they lack the inputs, evidence, or authorization to act safely, a disposition we term compliance bias, because both the reward signal and the benchmark scoring regime treat proceeding as the correct default regardless of whether the preconditions for safe action are present. We make three contributions. We first show that compliance bias originates in reward hacking within human-feedback pipelines and is entrenched by prominent agent benchmarks, which either penalize agents for pausing or are architecturally unable to distinguish a principled pause from a silent failure. We then introduce a three-gap taxonomy of abstention-warranted scenarios, covering specification gaps where required information is absent, verification gaps where world state cannot be confirmed, and authority gaps where explicit authorization has not been given, which together provide a principled basis for constructing abstention-aware agent benchmarks. Finally, we propose abstention evaluation protocols (Safety Rate, Usability Rate, and Informed Refusal Rate) and report preliminary results across 144 enterprise agent scenarios and five model families, in which a runtime-enforced abstention mechanism achieves up to 89.2% hazardous-action blocking and 87.5% usability on authorized scenarios, demonstrating that the safety--usability tradeoff is tunable rather than inherent and that its shape varies substantially across model families. We treat this as preliminary work and offer the taxonomy and composite metrics as a starting point for further conversations.

</details>


### [150] [WISE-HAR: A Generalizable Ensemble Deep Learning Framework for WiFi-Based Human Activity Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02974)
*Maheen Arshad,Qindeel E Zahra,Muhammad Khuram Shahzad*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A WiFi-based HAR system using CNN ensembles and strong augmentation recognizes three activities with high accuracy and generalizes well across scenarios and antenna types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to improve WiFi-based HAR as a privacy-preserving and non-intrusive alternative to cameras and wearables, while addressing key challenges such as model performance variance, limited dataset size, and weak real-world generalization.

Method: The method uses the Wallhack1.8k WiFi spectrogram dataset to classify three activities: No Presence, Walking, and Walking + Arm-waving. It introduces three improvements: an ensemble of five CNN architectures (Deep CNN, Wide CNN, MobileNetV2, ResNet50V2, EfficientNetB0), aggressive data augmentation (time-warping, frequency masking, noise addition), and evaluation under cross-scenario and cross-antenna settings.

Result: The ensemble model achieved 94.87% test accuracy on the LOS scenario with a Biquad antenna, surpassing the best single model by 0.66%. Data augmentation improved Random Forest accuracy from 60% to 95%. Cross-scenario and cross-antenna evaluations showed only small drops in accuracy, indicating strong generalization.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that combining CNN ensemble learning with aggressive data augmentation yields robust and accurate WiFi-based human activity recognition across different environments and antenna types, making the approach suitable for real-world deployment.

Abstract: Human Activity Recognition (HAR) using WiFi signals has emerged as a transformative technology for smart homes, healthcare monitoring, security systems, and ambient assisted living. Unlike traditional camera-based systems that raise significant privacy concerns and fail in low-light conditions, or wearable sensors that require user compliance, WiFi-based HAR is non-intrusive, privacy-preserving, cost-effective, and works seamlessly in any lighting condition. This paper presents a comprehensive approach to recognize three distinct human activities: "No Presence" (empty room), "Walking", and "Walking + Arm-waving" using the Wallhack1.8k WiFi spectrogram dataset. We propose three key improvements to address the main challenges in WiFi-based HAR. First, to address high performance variance, we implement ensemble learning with five different CNN architectures (Deep CNN, Wide CNN, MobileNetV2, ResNet50V2, and EfficientNetB0). Second, to address the small dataset size limitation, we apply aggressive data augmentation techniques including time-warping, frequency masking, and noise addition. Third, to evaluate real-world generalization capability, we perform cross-scenario evaluation (training on Line-of-Sight and testing on Non-Line-of-Sight) and cross-antenna evaluation (training on Biquad antenna and testing on PIFA antenna). Our ensemble model achieved a test accuracy of 94.87% on the LOS scenario with Biquad antenna, outperforming the best individual model by 0.66%. Data augmentation improved Random Forest performance from 60% to 95%. Cross-scenario evaluation showed minimal accuracy drops of only 1.37% and 2.07%, demonstrating strong generalization capabilities. The results indicate that the proposed approach is robust, reliable, and suitable for real-world deployment in diverse environments with different hardware configurations.

</details>


### [151] [Inducing Reasoning Primitives from Agent Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02994)
*Zhihan Lei,Jiarui Yan,Joshua Momo,William W. Cohen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Mine successful ReAct traces once, convert recurring reasoning steps into reusable pseudo-tools, and let a standard ReAct agent call them later; this consistently boosts accuracy and often lowers cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ReAct-style agents repeatedly rediscover similar reasoning procedures across tasks, but those useful routines remain ephemeral inside scratchpads instead of being stored and reused. The work aims to externalize and operationalize these recurring reasoning moves.

Method: The paper proposes Reasoning Primitive Induction, a single-pass pipeline that extracts successful ReAct traces, clusters recurring reasoning actions, and turns frequent patterns into typed pseudo-tools described by natural-language docstrings. At test time, a standard ReAct agent invokes and composes these induced primitives.

Result: The induced primitive libraries outperform the base agent on multiple tasks: RuleArena NBA 30->74, MuSR team allocation 38->68, and NatPlan meeting planning 7->29. With one fixed configuration across five subtasks, the method improves over zero-shot Chain-of-Thought on every subtask, matches or exceeds expert-authored decompositions, and beats AWM with lower average inference cost.

Conclusion: Inducing a reusable library of reasoning primitives from successful ReAct traces can improve downstream performance beyond the original trace-generating agent, while also reducing inference cost relative to some prior methods.

Abstract: ReAct-style LLM agents often rediscover the same reasoning routines across problems, yet leave those routines trapped in transient scratchpads. We introduce Reasoning Primitive Induction, a single-pass method that mines successful ReAct traces, clusters recurrent reasoning moves, and converts the most frequent moves into a compact library of typed pseudo-tools. Each pseudo-tool is specified by a natural-language docstring interpreted by an LLM at invocation time, and a standard ReAct loop composes these primitives at test time. The central result is that induced libraries outperform the very agent that generated their traces: by +44pp on RuleArena NBA (30 -> 74), +30pp on MuSR team allocation (38 -> 68), and +22pp on NatPlan meeting planning (7 -> 29). Across five comparable subtasks spanning narrative deduction, rule application, and constraint-satisfaction planning, a single fixed configuration improves over zero-shot Chain-of-Thought on every subtask, matches or surpasses expert-authored decompositions, and outperforms AWM at lower average inference cost.

</details>


### [152] [AUDITFLOW: Executable Symbolic Environments for Structured Financial Reporting Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03031)
*Yan Wang,Xuguang Ai,Jaisal Patel,Xueqing Peng,Fengran Mo,Yupeng Cao,Haohang Li,Mingyu Cao,Lingfei Qian,Víctor Gutiérrez-Basulto*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AuditFlow把财务审计做成“LLM搜索 + 符号系统验算”的多代理流程，用US-GAAP和XBRL图谱做证据底座，显著提升审计准确率；实验表明真正关键的是确定性验证，而不是仅靠模型读文本。


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 语言模型代理在结构化财务审计验证中表现受限，因为正确性依赖结构化证据而非纯文本理解；模型必须将报告事实映射到taxonomy概念、遍历计算或维度关系、重算期望值并应用审计规则，因此需要一种把符号验证能力与模型推理能力结合的方法。

Method: 提出AuditFlow，一个图驱动的多代理框架：从静态US-GAAP taxonomy graph和动态XBRL filing graph构建符号环境，并通过类型化工具支持事实检索、taxonomy遍历、数值校验和规则评估。系统由两名初级审计代理分别从监管视角与证据视角审查案例，一名高级审计代理负责分歧裁决并可要求进一步调查，最终通过evidential aggregation生成审计结论、期望值、证据链和可信度分数。

Result: 在FinAuditing-derived的FinMR样本上，AuditFlow在GPT-5.5下达到82.09%的joint audit accuracy，比最强基线高14.93个百分点；去除确定性检查后，准确率降至17.91%。

Conclusion: AuditFlow通过将自适应搜索与确定性验证分离，并结合图结构证据与多代理审计流程，能显著提升结构化财务审计验证的准确性；确定性符号检查对该任务是关键且不可被语言模型稳定替代。

Abstract: Structured financial audit verification is difficult for language-model agents because correctness depends on structured evidence rather than text alone. A model must link reported facts to taxonomy concepts, traverse calculation or dimensional relations, and recompute expected values before applying an audit rule. We propose AuditFlow, a graph-grounded multi-agent framework that separates adaptive search from deterministic verification. AuditFlow builds a symbolic environment from a static US-GAAP taxonomy graph and a dynamic XBRL filing graph, and exposes it through typed tools for fact retrieval, taxonomy traversal, numerical checking, and rule evaluation. Two junior auditors inspect each case from regulatory and evidentiary views, while a senior auditor resolves disagreements and can request further investigation. The final reports are fused through evidential aggregation to produce an audit verdict, expected value, evidence trail, and trustworthiness score. On a FinAuditing-derived FinMR sample, AuditFlow reaches 82.09% joint audit accuracy under GPT-5.5, outperforming the strongest baseline by 14.93 points. Removing deterministic checks drops accuracy to 17.91%, showing that the symbolic environment performs the verification step that the model cannot reliably replace.

</details>


### [153] [TriEval: A Resource-Efficient Pipeline for LLM Bias, Toxicity, and Truthfulness Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03036)
*Akshatha Srikantha,Manpreet Singh,Yash Jajoo,Shyamal Lakhanpal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TriEval is an efficient, laptop-friendly framework for evaluating LLMs across bias, toxicity, and truthfulness at once, and experiments show notable differences between popular open and closed models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM evaluation tools often test only one dimension at a time or require substantial compute resources, making comprehensive and practical safety evaluation difficult for many researchers.

Method: The paper proposes TriEval, a resource-efficient pipeline that jointly evaluates LLM outputs along multiple dimensions, specifically bias, toxicity, and truthfulness, and applies it to both open-source and closed-source models on standard laptop hardware.

Result: TriEval was tested on Llama 3 8B, Mistral 7B, Gemma 2 9B, and Claude Haiku, and the experiments revealed clear differences between open-source and closed-source models, particularly for toxicity and truthfulness.

Conclusion: TriEval is a lightweight, open-source evaluation pipeline that enables multi-parameter assessment of LLM outputs, showing meaningful performance differences across models while remaining accessible on standard consumer hardware.

Abstract: LLMs have evolved from basic chatbots to the backbone of the AI ecosystem, now widely used in healthcare, schools, and government services. The domain-wide adoption of LLMs necessitates continuous evaluation to ensure their safety and fairness. Common issues encountered after deploying LLMs include inconsistent outputs and hallucinations of incorrect information. Although numerous LLM evaluation tools exist, most are limited to testing a single parameter at a time or require massive computational resources that are not accessible to most researchers. TriEval addresses these challenges by evaluating LLM outputs across multiple parameters, including bias, toxicity, and truthfulness together, while minimizing computing resources. The pipeline is compatible with both open- and closed-source models and runs on a standard laptop without a GPU cluster. TriEval has been tested on four models: Llama 3 8B, Mistral 7B, Gemma 2 9B, and Claude Haiku. The results show clear differences between open-source and closed-source models, especially in terms of toxicity and truthfulness. TriEval is being released as open source to enable broader access for researchers with limited computational resources.

</details>


### [154] [Dynamic Objective Selection with Safeguards and LLM Oversight for Financial Decision-Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03704)
*Keigo Sakurai,Takahiro Ogawa,Miki Haseyama,Anjyu Anan,Kei Nakagawa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DOSS learns to switch between predefined trading objectives based on recent market statistics, adds confidence-based safety controls, and uses an LLM only as a constrained oversight layer to default to a safe objective when needed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed optimization objectives are often suboptimal under changing market regimes, while regime-switching methods that depend on latent regime inference can be noisy, delayed, and operationally unstable because of frequent switching. The paper aims to adapt the objective directly and more robustly.

Method: The paper formulates dynamic objective selection as a rolling-window classification problem. It uses interpretable recent-return statistics as inputs to select one objective from a predefined candidate set, outputs a confidence score, applies confidence-aware gating and switching controls, and allows an LLM only to accept the proposal or override it to a predefined conservative default under deterministic rules.

Result: From the abstract alone, no quantitative experimental results are reported. The claimed outcome is a more forward-looking, interpretable, and operationally safer objective-selection pipeline with reduced misselection risk and excessive switching.

Conclusion: DOSS can adaptively choose among a small set of predefined investment objectives over time without explicit latent regime estimation, while safeguards and LLM-based oversight help reduce harmful low-confidence switches and improve deployment stability.

Abstract: Financial decision-making tasks such as stock recommendation and portfolio allocation typically estimate future return and risk and then select trades or allocations for an investor, and the chosen optimization objective often determines realized performance. However, because market conditions evolve over time, a fixed objective can be suboptimal across regimes, while regime-switching pipelines that rely on latent regime estimates can be noisy or delayed and frequent switching can increase turnover and operational instability. In this paper, we propose DOSS (Dynamic Objective Selection with Safeguards), a learning-based selector that directly chooses the decision-relevant objective function at each time point from interpretable statistical summaries of recent returns, selecting among a small set of candidates (e.g., return-seeking, loss-averse, and risk-adjusted) without introducing intermediate regime variables. DOSS formulates objective selection as a classification problem over objectives and performs sequential updates with a rolling window to make forward-looking selections without temporal leakage, while also outputting a confidence score for each proposal. To mitigate misselection and excessive switching in deployment, DOSS applies confidence-aware gating with a fail-safe that overrides low-confidence proposals to a conservative default and enforces explicit controls tied to switching frequency. We further integrate governance by positioning a Large Language Model (LLM) as an oversight component rather than a generator of new objectives: the LLM is restricted to accept a proposed objective or override it to a predefined safe default, with deterministic rule-based constraints triggering overrides when needed.

</details>


### [155] [RelGT-AC: A Relational Graph Transformer for Autocomplete Tasks in Relational Databases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03040)
*Phillip Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RelGT-AC is a relational graph transformer for database autocomplete that masks target columns, supports multiple prediction types in one model, and uses TF-IDF for text columns; it consistently outperforms GraphSAGE, especially on regression and text-rich tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predictive learning on relational databases is difficult because data are multi-table, heterogeneous, and temporal. Existing autocomplete settings can admit trivial leakage or lose useful lexical information from text fields, so a stronger and more practical relational model is needed.

Method: The paper extends RelGT for autocomplete on relational databases represented as heterogeneous graphs. It introduces target-column masking during subgraph encoding, a unified prediction head for binary/multiclass/regression tasks, and a TF-IDF encoder for automatically detected free-text columns.

Result: Across 7 tasks on 3 RelBench v2 datasets, RelGT-AC beats the GraphSAGE baseline on all 3 regression autocomplete tasks and gains up to 10 AUROC points on text-heavy eligibility tasks when using the TF-IDF text encoder.

Conclusion: RelGT-AC effectively improves relational database autocomplete by preventing target leakage, unifying task handling, and restoring text signal, outperforming a GraphSAGE baseline especially on regression and text-heavy tasks.

Abstract: Relational databases underpin modern enterprise, scientific, and healthcare systems, yet predictive machine learning on such data remains challenging due to their multi-table, heterogeneous, and temporal structure. Relational Deep Learning (RDL) addresses this by representing databases as heterogeneous graphs and applying graph neural networks (GNNs) directly. RelBench v2 recently introduced autocomplete tasks -- a practically motivated task type where the goal is to predict an existing column value from relational context, analogous to an intelligent form-filling assistant. We propose RelGT-AC (Relational Graph Transformer for Autocomplete), extending the RelGT architecture with three targeted contributions: (1) a column masking strategy that prevents trivial solutions by masking the target column during subgraph encoding; (2) a unified task head supporting binary classification, multiclass classification, and regression autocomplete tasks within a single model; and (3) a TF-IDF text encoder that automatically detects and encodes free-text columns, recovering strong lexical signal that categorical encoders discard. Across 7 tasks spanning 3 RelBench v2 datasets (rel-trial, rel-f1, rel-stack), RelGT-AC outperforms the GraphSAGE baseline on all 3 regression autocomplete tasks and achieves up to +10 AUROC points on text-heavy eligibility tasks via the TF-IDF encoder.

</details>


### [156] [ToolGate: Token-Efficient Pre-Call Control for Tool-Augmented Vision-Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03054)
*Anjie Liu,Yan Song,Zhixun Chen,Ziqin Gong,Zhongwei Yu,Jun Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ToolGate在工具调用前做“执行/跳过”决策，用很小的控制器显著减少工具使用成本，并基本不损失性能；在同领域训练时还能带来小幅精度提升。 / ToolGate makes execute/skip decisions before tool invocation, using a small controller to cut tool-use cost substantially with little to no performance loss, and even modest accuracy gains with matched-domain training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 动机在于：现有工具增强VLM代理往往默认执行所有提议的工具调用，但这些调用代价高，而且很多并非必要，甚至可能有害。作者发现基线代理的局部选择性很差，帮助性与有害性调用比例接近，且大多数调用并不会改变即时预测，因此需要一种显式的调用筛选机制。 / The motivation is that existing tool-augmented VLM agents tend to execute all proposed tool calls, which is costly and often unnecessary or even harmful. The authors find poor local selectivity in the baseline agent: helpful and harmful calls occur at similar rates, and most calls do not change the immediate prediction, motivating an explicit filtering mechanism.

Method: 作者研究“预调用控制（pre-call control）”问题：当ReAct风格VLM代理提出OCR、检测、分割等感知工具调用时，先判断应执行还是跳过。为此提出ToolGate，一个轻量级外部控制器，基于轨迹文本和简单结构特征预测execute/skip决策，并在两个Qwen3-VL骨干和五个基准上评测。 / The authors study the pre-call control problem: when a ReAct-style VLM agent proposes OCR, detection, segmentation, or other perceptual tool calls, the system decides whether to execute or skip them beforehand. They propose ToolGate, a lightweight external controller that predicts execute/skip decisions from trajectory text and simple structural features, and evaluate it on five benchmarks with two Qwen3-VL backbones.

Result: 在五个基准上，ToolGate将token成本降至无限制ReAct基线的64%-69%，并在跨领域设置下保持平均准确率。在Qwen3-VL-30B的同领域轨迹训练下，平均准确率进一步提升1.65个百分点。 / Across five benchmarks, ToolGate reduces token cost to 64%-69% of the unrestricted ReAct baseline while preserving average accuracy in cross-domain settings. With matched-domain trajectory training on Qwen3-VL-30B, it further improves average accuracy by 1.65 points.

Conclusion: 该论文表明，工具增强型视觉语言代理不仅需要更强的感知工具，还需要在工具调用前进行显式控制；通过在调用前判断是否执行工具，可显著降低成本，并在合适训练条件下保持甚至提升准确率。 / The paper concludes that tool-augmented vision-language agents need not only better perceptual tools but also explicit pre-call control; deciding whether to execute a tool before use can substantially cut cost while preserving or even improving accuracy under suitable training settings.

Abstract: Tool-augmented vision-language agents can acquire external perceptual evidence through OCR, detection, segmentation, and other tools, but executing every proposed tool call is costly and sometimes unnecessary. We study the pre-call control problem: after a ReAct-style VLM agent proposes a perceptual tool call, should the call be executed, or skipped before its output enters the context? Across five benchmarks, we find that the baseline agent exhibits poor local selectivity: helpful and harmful calls occur at similar rates (11.8% vs. 9.9%), while most calls do not change the immediate forced-answer prediction. We introduce ToolGate, a lightweight external controller that predicts execute/skip decisions from trajectory text and simple structural features. Across two Qwen3-VL backbones, ToolGate reduces token cost to 64-69% of the unrestricted ReAct baseline while preserving average accuracy in cross-domain settings. With matched-domain trajectory training on Qwen3-VL-30B, it further improves average accuracy by 1.65 points. These results show that tool-augmented VLM agents benefit not only from better perceptual tools, but also from explicit control over when tool outputs are worth paying for.

</details>


### [157] [SkillDAG: Self-Evolving Typed Skill Graphs for LLM Skill Selection at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03056)
*Tong Bai,Zhenglin Wan,Pengfei Zhou,Xingrui Yu,Wangbo Zhao,Yang You,Ivor W. Tsang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillDAG turns skill selection for LLM agents into graph-based structural retrieval, combining similarity search with typed relations and online updates; this yields better scalability, recall, and task performance than prior baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When LLM agents use large skill libraries, choosing skills becomes a structural reasoning problem because skills can depend on, conflict with, specialize, or duplicate each other. Pure embedding similarity or full enumeration cannot capture these inter-skill relations effectively.

Method: The paper proposes SkillDAG, a typed directed graph over skills with an inference-time, agent-callable retrieval interface. Each query returns vector matches, typed-edge neighbors, and conflict signals. A propose-then-commit protocol allows agents to add execution-validated edges during execution, enabling online graph evolution across episodes.

Result: On ALFWorld and SkillsBench with MiniMax-M2.7, SkillDAG achieves 67.1% success and 27.3% reward, outperforming the strongest reported Graph-of-Skills baseline by 12.8 and 8.6 points. The improvement also transfers to gpt-5.2-codex, and SkillsBench Ret@K improves from 65.5 to 78.2 under matched queries.

Conclusion: SkillDAG demonstrates that structural, graph-based skill retrieval is more effective and scalable than similarity-only or fixed graph pipelines for LLM agents, and that online graph updates can accumulate useful execution-backed structure over time.

Abstract: As LLM agents adopt large skill libraries, selecting the right subset becomes a structural problem rather than a similarity-matching one: skills depend on, conflict with, specialize, or duplicate one another, a structure invisible to both full enumeration and embedding similarity. We present SkillDAG, which models inter-skill relationships as a typed directed graph and exposes it to an LLM agent as an inference-time, agent-callable structural retrieval interface, queried and evolved during execution rather than baked into a fixed retrieval pipeline: each search returns vector matches, typed-edge neighbors, and conflict signals, and a propose-then-commit protocol lets the agent register execution-backed edges so the graph accumulates structure across episodes. On ALFWorld and SkillsBench with MiniMax-M2.7, SkillDAG reaches 67.1% success and 27.3% reward, exceeding the strongest reported Graph-of-Skills baseline by +12.8 and +8.6 points; the advantage ports to gpt-5.2-codex, and intrinsic SkillsBench Ret@K rises from 65.5 to 78.2 under matched queries. These gains trace to isolable mechanisms: candidate ranking that stays robust as the pool grows 10x where a fixed seeding-diffusion pipeline degrades, and set-monotone online edits that enlarge ground-truth recall without evicting prior hits.

</details>


### [158] [CORE: Conflict-Oriented Reasoning for General Multimodal Manipulation Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03066)
*Jinjie Shen,Yaxiong Wang,Yujiao Wu,Lechao Cheng,Tianrui Hui,Nan Pu,Zhihui Li,Zhun Zhong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CORE teaches multimodal LLMs to detect fake news by spotting cross-modal and world-knowledge conflicts, using a newly annotated conflict dataset; it generalizes better than prior methods and achieves SOTA results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal fake news detectors depend on manipulation-specific architectures and large labeled datasets, which limits generalization when new manipulation types emerge. The authors are motivated by the idea that manipulated misinformation is fundamentally characterized by intrinsic conflicts, making conflict detection a more generalizable signal.

Method: The paper proposes CORE, a Conflict-Oriented Reasoning framework for multimodal large language models. It first builds the Conflict Attribution Corpus (CAC) with fine-grained annotations of conflict factors and sources, then trains models through conflict-oriented representation enhancement and reasoning so they can explicitly capture semantic and physical inconsistencies across modalities and against world knowledge.

Result: Extensive experiments show that CORE surpasses state-of-the-art models and can quickly adapt to unseen manipulation types with only a few samples or even in zero-shot scenarios. The work also contributes a publicly available dataset and codebase.

Conclusion: CORE demonstrates that explicitly modeling intrinsic conflicts in multimodal misinformation enables stronger robustness and generalization than manipulation-specific detectors, outperforming prior state-of-the-art methods while adapting effectively to unseen manipulation types in few-shot and zero-shot settings.

Abstract: The rapid rise of generative AI has made multimodal fake news increasingly realistic and pervasive, posing severe threats to public trust and social stability. Existing detection methods rely heavily on manipulation-specific models and large-scale labeled data, resulting in poor generalization to emerging manipulation types. We observed that the essence of manipulated misinformation lies in its intrinsic conflicts, \textbf{i.e.,} semantic or physical inconsistencies either across modalities or with common world knowledge. Inspired by this observation, we propose \textbf{C}onflict-\textbf{O}riented \textbf{RE}asoning (\textbf{CORE}) framework, an effective paradigm that learns to endows multimodal large language models (MLLMs) with explicit conflict-capturing capability. To this end, CORE first constructs the Conflict Attribution Corpus (CAC) with fine-grained annotations of conflict factors and sources, providing essential data support for subsequent conflict perception training. By performing conflict-oriented representation enhancement and reasoning based on CAC, CORE achieves robust and generalizable conflict detection, effectively and rapidly adapting to unseen manipulation types with a few samples or in even zero-shot settings. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CORE surpasses state-of-the-art models. The dataset and code are publicly available at https://github.com/shen8424/CORE.

</details>


### [159] [DELTAMEM: Incremental Experience Memory for LLM Agents via Residual Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03083)
*Haoran Tan,Zeyu Zhang,Zhicheng Cao,Rui Li,Xu Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DeltaMem 把 agent 经验记忆从“平铺存储”改为“残差树存储”：共享共性、只记录增量差异，并通过失败惩罚检索和自动整合提升长期交互中的学习效果。 / DeltaMem replaces flat experience storage with residual trees that share common structure and store only incremental differences, improving retrieval and continual learning through failure-aware matching and automatic consolidation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 现有基于 LLM 的 agent 记忆通常把经历存成彼此独立、扁平的单元，导致大量冗余；而相似经历之间的细微差异又会在检索时产生冲突甚至相互矛盾的指导，因此需要一种能表达“新经验是旧知识增量变化”的记忆结构。 / Existing LLM-agent memory systems often store experiences as isolated flat units, causing heavy redundancy; subtle differences among similar episodes also create retrieval conflicts and contradictory guidance, motivating a memory structure that models new experience as an incremental variation of prior knowledge.

Method: 论文提出 DeltaMem：将记忆划分为目标条件任务经验树和场景级环境知识树。每棵树由泛化的根节点和表示增量变化的 delta 节点组成；检索时使用带失败惩罚的相似度扫描找到最佳匹配，并通过从根到匹配节点的链式组合重构完整经验；同时通过自主整合机制把高频路径蒸馏为新的根节点。 / The paper proposes DeltaMem: memory is split into a goal-conditioned task-experience tree and a scene-level environment-knowledge tree. Each tree contains generalized root nodes and delta nodes that encode incremental variations; retrieval uses a failure-penalized similarity scan to find the best match and reconstructs full experience by composing the root-to-match chain; an autonomous consolidation mechanism distills frequent paths into new roots.

Result: 在多种交互式环境实验中，DeltaMem 始终优于现有基线方法，表明其在记忆压缩、检索有效性与持续学习能力方面具有优势。 / Across diverse interactive environments, DeltaMem consistently outperforms existing baselines, indicating advantages in memory compression, retrieval effectiveness, and continual learning.

Conclusion: DeltaMem 通过将经验组织为两棵残差树并结合检索与自主整合机制，能在持续交互环境中更高效地存储、重构和利用经验，整体性能稳定优于现有基线。 / DeltaMem shows that organizing experience into two residual trees, combined with retrieval and autonomous consolidation, enables more efficient storage, reconstruction, and use of experience in continual interaction, consistently outperforming prior baselines.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM)-based agents increasingly rely on memory to learn from experiences over continual interactions. However, storing experiences as independent, flat units leads to substantial redundancy and retrieval conflicts, as similar episodes repeat overlapping content and subtle scene variations cause retrieved memories to offer contradictory guidance. To address this, we introduce residual experience, positing that newly acquired experience is often an incremental variation of existing knowledge. We propose DeltaMem, a framework that organizes experience memory into two independent residual trees, one storing goal-conditioned task experience as reusable skills and another for scene-level environment knowledge. Each tree uses a root node for generalized base experiences and incremental delta nodes for subsequent variations, allowing related experiences to share a common foundation without duplication. For retrieval, a failure-penalized similarity scan locates the best match, reconstructing the full experience via root-to-match chain composition. An autonomous consolidation mechanism distills high-frequency paths into new root nodes, enabling the trees to self-organize from general heuristics to specialized variants. Experiments across diverse interactive environments show that DeltaMem consistently outperforms existing baselines. To facilitate future research, we release the code at https://github.com/import-myself/DeltaMem.

</details>


### [160] [The Shadow Price of Reasoning: Economic Perspective on Optimal Budget Allocation for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03092)
*Xu Wan,Speed Zhu,Jianwei Cai,Guang Chen,XiMing Huang,Wiggin Zhou,Mingyang Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper treats test-time token allocation as an economics-style optimization problem and proposes CLEAR, which shifts budget away from low-return queries toward solvable ones near the threshold of success, yielding much better accuracy-cost tradeoffs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inference-time scaling can improve LLM reasoning, but practical deployment is limited by fixed computation budgets. The paper aims to allocate limited reasoning tokens across queries more efficiently than uniform allocation.

Method: The paper formulates inference budget allocation as a global constrained optimization problem. It models each query's reasoning utility with a shifted-surge function, derives an optimal policy via a global shadow price that equalizes marginal utility, and implements this policy in the CLEAR framework with rational abandonment and reallocation.

Result: Across multiple reasoning tasks and traffic streams, CLEAR significantly improves the Pareto frontier of token cost versus mean accuracy. In resource-scarce settings, it achieves up to 3x higher global accuracy than uniform allocation.

Conclusion: CLEAR provides a theoretically grounded inference-time budget allocation policy that improves global reasoning performance under strict compute constraints by reallocating resources according to marginal utility.

Abstract: Inference-time scaling has emerged as a critical avenue for enhancing Large Language Models' performance, yet real-world deployment is constrained by strict computational budgets. In this work, we formulate inference budget allocation as a global constrained optimization problem governed by economic principles. By modeling per-query reasoning utility with a shifted-surge function, we derive an optimal allocation policy based on a global shadow price that equilibrates marginal utility under resource scarcity. Based on this theory, we propose Constrained Latent-utility Equilibrium Allocation for Reasoning (CLEAR). It performs rational abandonment and reallocates resources from insolvent queries to solvable queries near their emergence thresholds.
  Extensive experiments on several reasoning tasks with different traffic streams demonstrate that CLEAR significantly improves the Pareto frontier of total token cost versus mean accuracy. In resource-scarce regimes, CLEAR achieves up to a 3x improvement in global accuracy compared to uniform allocation.

</details>


### [161] [Decomposing how prompting steers behavior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03093)
*Fan L. Cheng,Nikolaus Kriegeskorte*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: 论文把“提示词如何改变模型行为”解释为“提示词如何几何性地重组隐藏表示”。多数变化可由简单保形变换解释，但真正接近恢复目标任务结构和行为的是仿射变换，表明跨维线性混合是关键。 / The paper explains prompt effects as geometric reorganization of hidden representations. Simple shape-preserving transforms explain much of the change, but affine transformation is the first that nearly restores target task structure and behavior, highlighting cross-dimensional linear mixing as the core mechanism.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 现有研究知道提示词能在不更新权重的情况下改变模型行为，但不清楚这种指令变化如何重塑内部表示并最终产生行为差异。作者希望以可解释、可因果检验的方式刻画提示引起的表征变化机制。 / Prompting can steer LLMs and VLMs without weight updates, but it is unclear how changing instructions reshapes internal representations to yield different behaviors. The motivation is to provide an interpretable and causal account of prompt-induced representational changes.

Method: 作者提出嵌套几何分解框架：对同一刺激在两种提示下的表示，依次用平移、刚体变换加统一缩放、逐轴缩放、仿射变换和非线性变换进行对齐；随后在单层上用映射后的隐藏状态替换原状态，并测试是否恢复目标提示下的表示几何和行为。该方法在3个LLM、3个VLM和6个文本/图像数据集上评估。 / The paper introduces a nested geometric decomposition framework that aligns same-stimulus representations under two prompts using increasingly expressive stimulus-invariant maps: translation, rigid transform with uniform scaling, sequential axis scaling, affine transform, and nonlinear transform. It then performs single-layer causal state replacement to test recovery of target-prompt geometry and behavior across 3 LLMs, 3 VLMs, and 6 datasets.

Result: 跨模型与任务的实验表明，提示词会把表示重塑为更符合指令任务结构的几何形态。交叉验证的方差分解显示，大量提示诱导变化可由保形映射解释，尤其是平移和刚体加统一缩放；但要接近恢复目标任务几何并获得对应行为增益，仿射层级是第一个足够有效的层级。不同模型和任务在各层使用这些几何路由策略的模式不同。 / Across models and tasks, prompts consistently reshape representations toward the instructed task structure. Variance decomposition shows much of the activation change is captured by shape-preserving maps, especially translation and rigid transformation with uniform scaling, while affine transformation is the first tier that nearly recovers target task geometry and yields matching behavioral gains. Layerwise tier profiles vary by model and task.

Conclusion: 提示词主要通过可解释的几何变换来重组隐藏表示，其中仿射变换是首个能够接近恢复目标提示任务几何与相应行为的层级，说明跨维线性混合是提示驱动行为变化的关键机制。 / Prompt-induced changes in hidden representations can be decomposed into interpretable geometric transforms, with affine transformation being the first tier that nearly recovers target-prompt task geometry and behavior, implying cross-dimensional linear mixing is a key mechanism of prompt-driven behavior.

Abstract: Prompting steers large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs) without weight updates, but it remains unclear how instruction changes reshape internal representations to produce behavior. We introduce a nested geometric decomposition framework that treats prompting as a transformation of the representational geometry of the content following the prompt. For each prompt pair, we align representations of the same stimuli under two prompts using increasingly expressive stimulus-invariant maps: translation, rigid transformation with uniform scaling, sequential axis scaling, affine transformation, and nonlinear transformation. We then causally test each map by replacing a single layer's prompt-A hidden state for held-out stimuli with its mapped counterpart and measuring recovery of prompt-B representational geometry and behavior. Across three LLMs, three VLMs, and six text or image datasets spanning style, emotion, scene content, and number, prompts consistently reshape representations toward the instructed task structure. Cross-validated variance decomposition shows that much prompt-induced activation change is captured by shape-preserving maps, especially translation and rigid transformation with uniform scaling, while tier profiles reveal model- and task-specific routing strategies across layers. Crucially, although translation and rigid tiers already improve behavioral agreement, affine transformation is the first tier to nearly recover target-prompt task geometry and yields corresponding behavioral gains. This suggests that cross-dimensional linear mixing is a key mechanism by which prompts reorganize representations toward instructed task structure. Our framework decomposes prompt-induced representational change into interpretable geometric components and reveals how models route task-relevant structure to produce prompt-driven behavior.

</details>


### [162] [From Long News to Accurate Forecast: Importance-Aware Fusion and PRM-Guided Reflection for Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03097)
*Mingyang Liu,Qingcan Kang,Yuke Wang,Shixiong Kai,Kaichao Liang,Hui-Ling Zhen,Tao Zhong,Mingxuan Yuan,Linqi Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: 中文：这篇论文让LLM先压缩最有用的新闻，再有监督地选择下一批补充新闻，从而在有限上下文下更快、更准地做时间序列预测。 English: This paper helps an LLM first compress the most useful news, then retrieve additional news under supervision, enabling faster and more accurate time-series forecasting under strict context limits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 中文：动机在于，新闻能提供历史序列无法恢复的突发外生信息，但现有基于LLM的新闻预测流程存在两大实际瓶颈：相关新闻总长度常超出上下文窗口；补充新闻的迭代检索缺乏明确指导，导致冗余更新和收敛缓慢。 English: The motivation is that news provides abrupt exogenous signals unavailable from historical values alone, but existing LLM-based pipelines suffer from two practical issues: relevant news often exceeds the context window, and iterative supplementary retrieval is unguided, causing redundancy and slow convergence.

Method: 中文：方法上，作者构建了两个离线训练模块。其一是重要性奖励模型，用于估计每篇新闻对预测的效用，并据此在顺序两两融合压缩中动态分配压缩预算；其二是过程奖励模型，根据当前误差画像和已选新闻历史，对补充新闻候选进行排序，实现受控检索。推理阶段使用冻结的过滤与压缩模块，无需反思式循环。 English: The method has two offline-trained components: an importance reward model that estimates each article’s forecasting utility and allocates compression budget during sequential pairwise fusion, and a process reward model that ranks supplementary news candidates based on the current error profile and previously selected articles. Inference uses frozen filtering/compression modules without a reflection loop.

Result: 中文：在金融、能源、交通和比特币预测基准上，该方法优于强基线，显著减少相较于迭代式基线的细化轮数，并且在相关新闻长度达到数千token时仍保持有效。 English: On finance, energy, traffic, and bitcoin benchmarks, the method outperforms strong baselines, significantly reduces the number of refinement iterations versus iterative baselines, and remains effective even when relevant news spans thousands of tokens.

Conclusion: 中文：该论文提出的框架通过“重要性感知压缩 + 过程级检索监督”有效解决了新闻增强时间序列预测中的上下文受限与检索低效问题，在多领域基准上提升了精度并减少了迭代次数。 English: The paper concludes that combining importance-aware compression with process-level retrieval supervision effectively addresses context-window limits and inefficient iterative retrieval in news-augmented time-series forecasting, improving accuracy while reducing refinement steps across domains.

Abstract: Incorporating news into time series forecasting is appealing because news can reveal abrupt exogenous events that historical values alone cannot recover. However, existing LLM-based news-forecasting pipelines face two practical limitations: relevant news articles often exceed the model's context window, and iterative retrieval of supplementary news is typically unguided, leading to redundant updates and slow convergence. We address these issues with a novel framework that combines importance-aware news compression and process-level retrieval supervision. First, we train an importance reward model that estimates the forecasting utility of each article and uses this signal to allocate compression budgets during sequential pairwise fusion, preserving informative content within a fixed context limit. Second, we introduce a process reward model (PRM) that ranks multiple supplementary-news candidates conditioned on the current error profile and the history of previously selected articles, replacing one-shot blind retrieval with quality-controlled selection. Both components are trained offline using historical data with ground truth; inference uses the frozen filtering logic and compression modules without any reflection loop. Experiments on finance, energy, traffic, and bitcoin forecasting benchmarks show that our method improves prediction accuracy over strong baselines, significantly reduces the number of refinement iterations compared to the iterative baseline, and remains effective when relevant articles span thousands of tokens.

</details>


### [163] [DeskCraft: Benchmarking Desktop Agents on Professional Workflows and Human-in-the-Loop Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03103)
*Wenkai Wang,Tao Xiong,Jingchen Ni,Yunpeng Bao,Xiyun Li,Tianqi Liu,Hongcan Guo,Zilong Huang,Shengyu Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DeskCraft is a new benchmark for realistic, long, interactive desktop workflows in creative software, and current agents perform far from reliably on it.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing desktop GUI benchmarks oversimplify real-world use by focusing on short tasks with fully specified instructions upfront, failing to capture long-horizon execution and human-in-the-loop collaboration common in professional software workflows.

Method: The paper introduces DeskCraft, a benchmark for professional desktop GUI workflows in creative and engineering software. It defines a multilevel difficulty taxonomy, formalizes a human-agent interaction protocol for mid-turn and post-turn exchanges, and evaluates 18 proprietary and open-source agents on 538 benchmark tasks.

Result: DeskCraft covers domains including design, video, audio, and 3D creation, with long-horizon tasks exceeding 50 steps. In evaluation, GPT-5.4 achieves 31.6% on standard tasks and 27.6% on interactive tasks. The analysis shows persistent weaknesses in long-horizon task completion and proactive clarification behavior.

Conclusion: DeskCraft exposes major gaps in current desktop GUI agents for long-horizon, interactive professional workflows; even the best tested model, GPT-5.4, achieves only modest performance, especially on interactive tasks.

Abstract: Real-world professional desktop workflows in specialized creative and engineering software unfold over long horizons and often require human-in-the-loop coordination, where agents proactively seek necessary information and users provide additional instructions, clarifications, feedback, or corrections as the task progresses. Yet existing desktop GUI benchmarks mostly reduce this setting to short, simplified tasks with all user instructions provided upfront. To address this issue, we introduce DeskCraft, a desktop GUI benchmark targeting long horizon creative and engineering workflows and proactive human-agent collaboration. DeskCraft organizes tasks into a multilevel difficulty taxonomy, with long horizon tasks requiring over 50 execution steps, and covers professional creative software across design, video, audio, and 3D creation. Furthermore, DeskCraft formalizes human-agent collaboration into an interaction protocol covering mid-turn and post-turn exchanges. Mid-turn interaction captures both agent-initiated clarification under uncertainty and user-initiated interruption during execution, while post-turn interaction accommodates user-driven feedback after the agent signals completion, together spanning the full space of realistic collaboration patterns. We evaluate 18 proprietary and open source agents on 538 tasks and find that GPT-5.4 reaches 31.6% on standard tasks and 27.6% on interactive tasks. Further analyses reveal persistent failures in long horizon workflow delivery and proactive clarification. We will open-source all evaluation codes, tasks, and data at https://github.com/mrwwk/DeskCraft.

</details>


### [164] [EvoTrainer: Co-Evolving LLM Policies and Training Harnesses for Autonomous Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03108)
*Guhong Chen,Yingcheng Shi,Yongbin Li,Binhua Li,Xander Xu,Hu Wei,Shiwen Ni,Min Yang,Jieping Ye*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EvoTrainer automatically improves not just the LLM policy but also the training system around it, leading to stronger or comparable RL performance than human-designed setups, especially on long-horizon software engineering tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static training harnesses are a bottleneck in autonomous LLM training, especially in agentic RL where failure modes shift over time and scalar rewards hide the real causes of errors. The authors aim to make the training process itself adaptive rather than only searching for better training recipes.

Method: The paper proposes EvoTrainer, an autonomous training framework that co-evolves LLM policies and training-side harnesses using empirical feedback. It analyzes rollout-level evidence, updates diagnostics, backtests candidate interventions, and stores reusable skills for later optimization.

Result: Across mathematical reasoning, competitive-programming code generation, and repository-level software engineering, EvoTrainer matches or outperforms human-engineered RL baselines under the same data, codebase, and evaluation setup. The largest improvement appears in long-horizon agentic software engineering tasks. Trajectory analysis shows domain-specific strategy divergence, improved diagnostics that block invalid high-scoring branches, and beneficial reuse of learned skills.

Conclusion: Autonomous LLM reinforcement learning should move beyond static recipe search and instead jointly evolve both the model policy and the training harness that diagnoses and guides learning.

Abstract: Autonomous LLM training is often framed as recipe search, which leaves the training harness largely static. This limitation sharpens in agentic RL, where shifting bottlenecks and scalar rewards mask diverse failure modes. We introduce EvoTrainer, an autonomous training framework that co-evolves LLM policies and training-side harnesses through empirical feedback: it diagnoses rollout-level evidence, revises diagnostics, backtests interventions, and accumulates reusable skills. Evaluated on mathematical reasoning, competitive-programming code generation, and repository-level software engineering, EvoTrainer matches or exceeds the human-engineered RL references under the same data, codebase, and evaluation protocol, with the largest gain on long-horizon agentic SWE. Trajectory analyses show that retained strategies diverge across domains, evolving diagnostics prevent invalid high-scoring branches from being promoted, and reusable skills shape later search. Autonomous LLM RL should move beyond recipe search toward joint evolution of policies and the training harnesses that interpret them.

</details>


### [165] [Uncertainty-Aware Clarification in LLM Agents with Information Gain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03135)
*Mengyi Deng,Zhiwei Li,Xin Li,Tingyu Zhu,Ying Zhao,Zhijiang Guo,Wei Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Train LLM agents to ask clarification questions that maximize information gain about user intent; this raises task success by 3.7% with almost no extra interaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents often receive underspecified instructions, creating latent uncertainty about user intent. Without effective clarification, agents may take incorrect tool actions, so the paper aims to make clarification behavior explicitly optimize ambiguity resolution.

Method: The paper introduces an Information Gain Reward that measures how much a clarification exchange updates the agent’s Bayesian belief toward the ground-truth user goal, and uses this reward to train an LLM clarifier in an agent-tool-user setting. The framework is evaluated in a clarification-enhanced tau-Bench with cross-agent testing over five heterogeneous model backbones.

Result: Across five heterogeneous backbones in the clarification-enhanced tau-Bench, the proposed method improves success rate by 3.7% over a no-clarification baseline while increasing interaction by only 0.3 total steps on average.

Conclusion: A goal-oriented clarification framework trained with an Information Gain Reward helps LLM agents ask more useful clarification questions, improving task success with minimal extra interaction.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents often operate under underspecified user instructions, where latent uncertainty over user intent leads to erroneous tool actions. To address this challenge, we propose a goal-oriented clarification framework that aligns clarification behavior with ambiguity resolution. Central to our approach is the Information Gain Reward, a metric that quantifies the utility of clarification questions by measuring the Bayesian belief update towards the ground-truth goal induced by the clarification exchange. We train the clarifier (LLM) using this reward to optimize for high information gain, ensuring that clarifications effectively reduce uncertainty and improve task completion within the agent-tool-user environment. We validate our framework within a clarification-enhanced $τ$-Bench environment, conducting cross-agent evaluations across five heterogeneous backbones. Empirical results demonstrate that our method consistently improves the success rate by 3.7\% over the no-clarification baseline, while adding only 0.3 total interaction steps on average.

</details>


### [166] [Think-Before-Speak: From Internal Evaluation to Public Expression in Multi-Agent Social Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03137)
*Kaiqi Yang,Tai-Quan Peng,Sanguk Lee,Hui Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TBS separates what agents think from what they say, letting researchers track how internal appraisals lead to speaking intentions and then public dialogue in multi-agent LLM simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent dialogue simulation frameworks mainly model observable turns or aggregate outputs, making it hard to inspect how silence, speaking intention, and public expression emerge from agents’ internal evaluative processes.

Method: The paper proposes TBS (Think-Before-Speak), an interval-based multi-agent simulation framework in which all agents first update structured private internal states from dialogue history and memory, then an orchestrator resolves competing speaking intentions and selects one public utterance per interval.

Result: In simulated town hall discussions on a climate-related policy issue, TBS generated coherent internal-state traces that varied systematically with turn-allocation, silence, and memory settings. Dissonance-related appraisal increased willingness to speak, silence-pressure appraisal reduced it, and turn-allocation rules primarily shaped public expression after speaking intention formed.

Conclusion: TBS makes the pathway from private evaluation to public expression observable in LLM-based multi-agent simulations, enabling mechanism-sensitive analysis of social interaction and opinion dynamics.

Abstract: LLM-based multi-agent simulation offers a promising way to study social interaction, deliberation, and collective opinion dynamics. However, many existing dialogue simulation frameworks represent interaction mainly as observable turn exchange or aggregated outputs, leaving the internal evaluative processes behind silence, speaking intention, and public expression difficult to examine. We introduce TBS (Think-Before-Speak), an interval-based multi-agent simulation framework that separates agents' private reasoning from public utterance generation. At each interval, all agents update structured internal states based on the shared dialogue history and their own memory. These states include dissonance-related appraisal, perceived opinion climate, perceived isolation risk, response strategy, and willingness to speak. The orchestrator then resolves competing speaking intentions and commits one utterance to the public dialogue, allowing internal evaluation and public interaction to co-evolve over time.
  We evaluate TBS in simulated town hall discussions on a climate-related policy issue. Results show that TBS produces coherent internal-state traces and that these traces vary systematically across turn-allocation, silence, and memory conditions. Dissonance-related appraisal increases agents' willingness to speak, whereas silence-pressure appraisal decreases it. Once speaking intention is formed, public expression is shaped mainly by turn-allocation rules. These findings suggest that TBS supports mechanism-sensitive social simulation by making the pathway from internal evaluation to public expression observable and analyzable.

</details>


### [167] [GTBench: A Curriculum-Grounded Benchmark for Evaluating LLMs as Mathematical Research Assistants in Graph Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03144)
*Noujoud Nader,Ibrahem Aljabea,Patrick Diehl,Deepti Gupta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper builds a graph theory benchmark for testing LLM mathematical reasoning and finds a steep performance gap: GPT-5 performs strongly across levels, while other models struggle badly on harder proof tasks, and automated proof judging is not fully reliable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used for self-study in technical fields, but their reliability as mathematical reasoning assistants, especially in graph theory, is not well understood; the paper aims to provide a curriculum-grounded benchmark to measure this systematically.

Method: The paper introduces GTBench with 63 graph theory problems across three difficulty groups, then evaluates five frontier LLMs under zero-shot and chain-of-thought prompting using exact match, LLM-as-judge, and hybrid human-plus-LLM evaluation depending on task difficulty.

Result: GPT-5 nearly saturates easy problems and retains strong performance on graduate-level proofs, while the other models degrade sharply as difficulty increases; common failures include correct algorithm but incorrect execution, incomplete reasoning in proofs, and notable disagreement between human and automated judges on near-complete or verbose proofs.

Conclusion: GTBench is the first curriculum-grounded benchmark for evaluating LLM graph-theoretic reasoning, and it shows that GPT-5 is substantially more reliable than the other tested models, though proof evaluation still requires careful human oversight due to judge disagreement.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as self-study assistants in technical disciplines, yet their reliability as mathematical reasoning assistants remains poorly understood. We introduce GTBench, a curriculum-grounded benchmark for evaluating LLMs as mathematical research assistants in graph theory, comprising 63 problems organized into three groups of increasing difficulty: undergraduate definitions and basic properties (Group 1), algorithm tracing and structural reasoning (Group 2), and graduate-level proof construction (Group 3). Problems are sourced from verified academic materials including Diestel's Graph Theory. We evaluate five frontier models -- GPT-5, Claude Sonnet 4.6, Gemini 2.5 Flash-Lite, Llama 3.3 70B, and Mistral Large 3 -- under zero-shot and chain-of-thought prompting, using exact-match and LLM-as-judge evaluation for Groups 1 and 2, and a hybrid human expert and LLM-as-judge protocol for Group 3. Our results reveal a pronounced performance hierarchy: GPT-5 approaches ceiling on Group 1 (95.8% zero-shot) and maintains meaningful accuracy on graduate proofs (82%), while all other models degrade substantially with difficulty, with Llama achieving 0% under human evaluation on Group 3 zero-shot. Failure mode analysis shows that correct algorithm, wrong execution errors dominate Groups 1 and 2, while Group 3 additionally surfaces incomplete reasoning failures and reveals systematic disagreement between human evaluators and the automated judge, particularly on verbose or near-complete proofs (kappa = 0.48-0.83 across human pairs). GTBench provides the first curriculum-grounded evaluation framework for graph-theoretic reasoning in LLMs, with direct implications for the governance of AI tools in mathematical education and scientific research.

</details>


### [168] [ClinicalMC: A Benchmark for Multi-Course Clinical Decision-Making with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03157)
*Ruihui Hou,Siyi Zhu,Ziyue Huai,Guangya Yu,Yongqi Fan,Chunming Wang,Tong Ruan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ClinicalMC is a new bilingual benchmark for testing how well LLMs handle multi-stage, evolving clinical decision-making across several hospital courses, using static and dynamic evaluation settings with multi-agent simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing medical LLM benchmarks mainly test single-course scenarios and do not systematically evaluate longitudinal cases where patient conditions change over time, which is critical for realistic clinical decision-making.

Method: The paper proposes ClinicalMC, a bilingual benchmark containing Chinese and English longitudinal clinical cases spanning four stages from admission to discharge, and evaluates multiple categories of LLMs under both single-turn static and multi-turn dynamic settings using a multi-agent framework with patient, examiner, and doctor agents.

Result: From the abstract, the main concrete outcome is the construction of the ClinicalMC benchmark and the multi-agent evaluation framework; specific comparative model performance results are not reported in the abstract.

Conclusion: ClinicalMC fills an important benchmarking gap for evaluating LLMs in evolving, multi-course clinical decision-making, and it is intended to improve understanding of model strengths and weaknesses for safer healthcare deployment.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have been widely adopted in healthcare, yet they still encounter significant challenges in complex clinical decision-making scenarios. Existing benchmarks primarily assess LLM performance in single-course settings and lack systematic evaluation in multi-course scenarios, where a patient's condition evolves over time. To address this gap, we propose ClinicalMC, a benchmark for multi-course clinical decision-making. It includes 1,275 Chinese and 5,804 English samples across four stages from admission to discharge. These stages cover triage, first-course examination/diagnosis/treatment, subsequent multi-course examination/assessment/treatment, and final diagnosis.
